SYNTH-0001♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Rebecca Hill♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother was in the hospital for six weeks in 2019 after a series of complications from a routine knee replacement, and what I remember most is not the machines or the monitors but the one surgeon who would come in at 7am and actually sit down and talk to her. Not stand in the doorway, not glance at a screen. Sit down. That relationship is what I keep thinking about when I read that HHS wants to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care.⏎⏎The question I want to address is what guardrails or conditions should accompany AI tools before they are deployed in direct patient care. My answer is that the guardrails being discussed are not adequate and in some cases they may not be possible to design well enough to matter.⏎⏎Here is what worries me most concretely. AI systems trained on historical data will inherit whatever was wrong with that history. We already know from published research, including a widely cited 2019 study in Science magazine by Obermeyer and colleagues, that a commercial algorithm used by hospitals across the country systematically underestimated the health needs of Black patients because it used healthcare spending as a proxy for health need. Black patients had been given less care historically, so the algorithm concluded they needed less care going forward. The system appeared to be working. It had metrics and validation scores. It was being sold to hospitals. And it was quietly making racial disparities worse.⏎⏎Now HHS wants to accelerate this. I understand the appeal. Clinicians are burned out. Administrative burdens are real. But I do not think the answer to physician burnout is to hand more decision-making to systems that cannot be held accountable when they are wrong.⏎⏎There is something that gets lost in the efficiency framing that I cannot quite put into words except to say this: when a doctor makes a mistake, there is a person you can ask why. You can look them in the eye. You can file a complaint. When an algorithm makes a mistake, the hospital says the vendor is responsible, the vendor says the hospital deployed it incorrectly, and the patient's family is left with nothing. I have seen this happen to people I know and it is infuriating in a way that is hard to explain to people who haven't experienced it.⏎⏎I am also genuinely skeptical about the workforce claims. Every technology that was supposed to free up time for care has, in practice, often just increased documentation expectations or been used to justify not hiring another nurse. EHRs were going to transform care and reduce administrative burden. Instead clinicians now spend more time on documentation than they did before, according to multiple surveys. Why would AI be different if the institutional pressures are the same?⏎⏎My specific request to HHS is to slow down. Require independent prospective validation, not just vendor-supplied testing. Require demographic breakdowns of error rates before any system is approved for use. Require that hospitals disclose to patients when AI is being used to inform their care or their insurance denials. Require that there be a named human being responsible for every AI-assisted decision, someone who cannot legally point to the algorithm as the reason they cannot explain a choice.⏎⏎I also want to say something about insurance claim denials specifically because I think that use case is particularly alarming and I notice it doesn't get as much attention as the clinical side. When insurers use AI to deny claims, they can process hundreds of thousands of denials with a review time that in some reported cases has been under a second per claim. Cigna was reported in 2023 to have used a system that allowed physicians to reject claims without reviewing individual files. That is not a hypothetical risk. That already happened. Accelerating AI adoption in that context is not improving care, it is industrializing the denial of care.⏎⏎I don't have a tidy way to end this except to say that I think the framing of this RFI is already pointing in the wrong direction. Asking how to accelerate adoption assumes adoption is the goal. I'd rather HHS ask first whether adoption in a given use case is actually good for patients, and I'm not confident that question is being asked with enough seriousness. SYNTH-0002♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Brian Johnson♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in 2021 after a medication error that nobody caught until her kidneys started failing. The error was caught, eventually, by a nurse who noticed something felt off. Not a dashboard. Not an algorithm. A person who had been watching her for three days and knew something had changed. I keep thinking about that whenever I read about how AI is going to transform clinical care and make it safer and more efficient.⏎⏎The question I want HHS to actually sit with is this: what happens to accountability when a machine is involved in a decision that harms someone? Because right now, if a doctor prescribes the wrong dose, there is a person who is responsible. A license. A name. You can ask them what they were thinking. With an AI recommendation that a clinician "approved," that chain gets murky very fast. The clinician says they were following the system. The hospital says the vendor validated it. The vendor says it was used outside its approved parameters. I have watched this happen already with prior authorization AI tools that insurance companies use to deny care, and it is genuinely hard to get anyone to own the decision.⏎⏎I understand the argument that AI could catch errors like the one that almost killed my mother. Maybe. But the reason that nurse caught the problem was not that she had access to better data. The data was right there in the chart. She caught it because she was paying attention to a human being over time. If you staff a unit with fewer nurses because you have an AI monitoring system, you may actually get worse outcomes even if the AI is technically more accurate than any individual nurse on any individual data point. The interactions matter. The presence matters. I'm not sure how you measure that or put it in a cost-benefit analysis, but that doesn't mean it isn't real.⏎⏎There is also something that bothers me about the framing of this whole RFI. It asks how HHS can accelerate AI adoption. The assumption is that faster adoption is better. But a lot of AI tools in healthcare right now are being deployed before anyone has really studied them in the populations where they're being used. The dermatology AI systems that have worse accuracy on darker skin tones is the famous example, but that problem is not limited to dermatology. Training data in American healthcare reflects decades of unequal access and documentation, and a system trained on that data is going to encode those inequities even if nobody intends it to.⏎⏎HHS should be asking harder questions about what independent, post-market surveillance of these tools looks like, not just how to get them deployed faster. Right now vendors self-report performance data, which is obviously not sufficient. If a pharmaceutical company told us to trust their own efficacy data without independent review we would laugh them out of the room, but somehow with AI tools the evidentiary bar is much lower.⏎⏎I also want to say something about administrative AI because I think it gets lumped in with clinical AI as if it's safer or less consequential. Prior authorization algorithms are administrative. They are also directly causing people to be denied chemotherapy and insulin. The people who used to make those decisions were not perfect, but they were human beings who could be reasoned with, appealed to, and held accountable by state insurance commissioners and courts. When an algorithm denies a claim, the appeals process is often designed to be so burdensome that most people give up.⏎⏎My specific ask is that HHS not treat acceleration as a goal in itself. Before any further regulatory action to speed adoption, the department should commission an independent review of post-market surveillance mechanisms for AI tools already in use in clinical and insurance settings, with particular attention to how adverse events are defined, reported, and investigated when AI is involved in the care pathway. The FDA has a framework for this with drugs and devices. There is no equivalent for most of the AI tools being deployed right now, including the ones making coverage decisions for Medicare patients.⏎⏎I don't know how HHS weighs all of this against the genuine potential benefits. I am not saying AI has no role ever. SYNTH-0003♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Ashley Rodriguez♔Association for Molecular Pathology♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Vincent's in Indianapolis in February 2023 after a sepsis diagnosis that took nearly 36 hours to catch. During that time I watched nurses and doctors do something no algorithm can replicate: they argued with each other. They disagreed. They changed their minds when my mother, who was confused and frightened and not giving a clear history, finally said something offhand about a medication she'd stopped taking months before. That offhand comment changed her treatment. A pattern-matching system working off her chart would never have waited for it.⏎⏎I want to focus my comments on what the RFI describes as opportunities to accelerate AI adoption in clinical decision-making, because that's the area I find most troubling.⏎⏎The framing of this RFI assumes that speed of adoption is itself a goal. I don't think it is. Medicine is one of the few fields where being slower and more deliberate is often the right answer. The question isn't how do we get more AI into clinical care faster, it's whether we have any real evidence that doing so makes patients safer or healthier in the actual messy conditions of real hospitals, not in controlled pilots with well-curated data sets. The evidence on that is a lot thinner than the vendor presentations would suggest.⏎⏎What worries me practically is the deployment gap. A system can be validated in one academic medical center with a particular patient population and EHR configuration, and then sold to a community hospital in rural Georgia with completely different demographics, staffing ratios, and documentation practices, and nobody is systematically tracking whether it still works. The FDA's current framework for software as a medical device doesn't fully close this gap. Hospitals aren't equipped to audit these tools on their own. So what happens is the tool gets used, something goes wrong eventually, and then there's a long argument about whether the clinician deviated from the recommendation or followed it too closely, and the vendor points to the terms of service, and the hospital points to the vendor, and the patient or the patient's family is left holding nothing.⏎⏎I'm not saying AI has no place in healthcare at all. I work in health policy and I understand the arguments about diagnostic imaging and sepsis alerts and medication reconcilliation. But there's a difference between a narrow tool with a clear validation record and a defined use case, and the broader push to embed AI into clinical workflows in ways that change how decisions get made and who feels responsible for them. The second thing is what this RFI seems to be encouraging, and I think HHS should pump the brakes.⏎⏎The employment dimension also deserves more attention than the RFI gives it. When hospitals talk about AI reducing administrative burden, what they often mean in practice is that they want to do the same work with fewer people. That's not necessarily bad in every case but it's not neutral either. Medical coders, prior authorization reviewers, care coordinators, these are jobs held disproportionately by women and by workers without four-year degrees, and the displacement of those jobs doesn't get offset by the productivity gains that flow to health systems and insurers.⏎⏎HHS should at minimum require prospective outcome tracking for any AI tool deployed in clinical or coverage decision contexts before offering any regulatory or reimbursement incentives for adoption. Not retrospective review after something goes wrong. Prospective, standardized, publicly reported data on whether the tool is actually doing what it claims in the population where it's actually being used.⏎⏎I'd also ask the Department to be honest about the fact that some of the pressure to adopt AI quickly is coming from vendors with significant financial stakes in this RFI's outcome, and that the framing of "accelerating adoption" reflects those interests more than it reflects♔♔2026-01-25T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-25T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare diffuses responsibility across clinicians, hospitals, and vendors in ways that leave patients without a clear accountable party when errors cause harm.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔hopeful♔Colorado♔executive director♔44♔A 2024 TMA physician survey of 4,200 licensed Texas physicians found that 58% reported using at least one AI-assisted diagnostic or administrative tool in their clinical practice, yet 74% of that group identified inconsistent payer reimbursement policies as the primary barrier to broader integration. SYNTH-0004♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Joshua Brown♔Center for Democracy and Technology♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Brigham and Women's Hospital in Boston in February of 2023, and what I watched during those eleven days was not primarily a technical process. It was a series of human judgment calls made under pressure by people who knew her name, watched her face, and talked to our family at two in the morning when we were scared out of our minds. I keep thinking about that experience when I read about how HHS wants to "accelerate" AI adoption in clinical care, and honestly my stomach drops a little.⏎⏎The RFI asks about barriers to AI adoption and what the department can do to remove them. I want to suggest that some of those "barriers" are actually there for good reasons, and that the framing of this whole exercise worries me. When you set up a process asking specifically how to speed something up, you've already decided the direction. You're not really asking whether to go, just how fast.⏎⏎Here is what I think gets lost in the efficiency argument. Medicine is not mostly an information retrieval problem. The nurses who took care of my mother were not primarily processing data points. They were watching her breathe, noticing when she seemed more agitated than the monitors showed, pushing back on a resident's order because something felt off to them based on thirty years of experience. I don't know how you encode that in a training dataset. And I'm genuinely uncertain whether anyone does, or whether the people selling these systems are being honest about that uncertainty.⏎⏎The bias issue is something I feel strongly about. These systems are trained on historical records, and historical records in American medicine are full of documented disparities in how pain is assessed, how symptoms are taken seriously, which patients get aggressive treatment and which get palliative pathways steered at them early. A 2021 study in JAMA looked at sepsis prediction algorithms and found meaningful differences in performance across racial groups, and that's one study about one condition. We don't actually know the scope of how these biases are baked in because the vendors often won't disclose their training data or their validation populations. HHS accelerating adoption before that transparency exists seems backwards to me.⏎⏎What concerns me almost as much as the clinical safety piece is what happens to the workforce. I have a friend who works as a medical coder, has done it for fifteen years, and she's been told her department is being "augmented" by AI tools that will let them handle twice the volume with the same headcount. That's not augmentation, that's a staffing cut that hasn't happened yet. And when errors occur in that environment, who is responsible? The coder who approved a code she barely had time to review? The software vendor? The hospital administrator who decided the productivity targets were achievable?⏎⏎The accountability question is the one I can't get past. In the current system, when something goes wrong in a hospital, there is at least a theoretical chain of human responsibility. It's imperfect and often fails patients badly, I know that. But at least it exists. When you insert an AI system into that chain, responsibility gets distributed in ways that are genuinely hard to trace. The clinician says she was following the system's recommendation. The hospital says the clinician should have exercised independent judgment. The vendor says the system was used outside its validated parameters. Patients and families end up in a maze.⏎⏎I'm not saying technology has no role in medicine. I'm saying HHS should be asking much harder questions before it starts clearing regulatory pathways and building reimbursement incentives designed to push these tools into clinical settings faster. The question shouldn't be "what barriers can we remove" but "what safeguards do we need before any of this scales."⏎⏎And frankly I'm not sure the current post-market surveillance infrastructure for software as a medical device is anywhere close to adequate for what's being contemplated here. The FDA's Digital Health Center of Excellence has been working on this for years and the framework still has enormous gaps, particularly around algorithm updates and how to handle drift in model performance over time without triggering a full re-review.♔♔2026-02-05T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-05T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI-powered insurance decision-making automates prior authorization denials and coverage restrictions in ways that are opaque, unappealable, and systematically biased against vulnerable patients.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔resigned♔Oregon♔executive director♔50♔A 2024 landscape analysis conducted across GLI's network of 47 hepatology centers of excellence identified that fewer than 18% currently employ any validated AI-assisted diagnostic tool for early-stage fibrosis detection or hepatocellular carcinoma surveillance, despite the availability of multiple FDA-cleared algorithms. SYNTH-0005♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Charles Nelson♔American Pharmacists Association♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in March of 2023, and I watched what good clinical care actually looks like. Her attending physician, Dr. Okafor, spent maybe forty minutes with her on one of those days just sitting and asking questions, not about her labs, but about her life, her fear of being a burden, the fact that she hadn't told her sister how sick she was. That conversation changed her treatment plan. No algorithm would have found that. I don't think any algorithm could.⏎⏎So when I read that HHS is looking at ways to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care, my first reaction was genuinely just dread. Not because I'm opposed to technology in general, but because I've spent enough time in hospitals and clinics to know that the thing people are trying to automate is often the thing that cannot be automated without losing something essential.⏎⏎The RFI asks about barriers to AI adoption, and I want to answer the question slightly differently: some of those barriers exist for good reason and HHS should think very carefully before removing them.⏎⏎Here's what concerns me most. When a hospital or an insurer deploys an AI tool for clinical decision support, or for prior authorization, or for flagging high-risk patients, there is an almost immediate diffusion of accountability. The clinician can say the algorithm recommended it. The hospital can say the vendor built it. The vendor can say the training data was the problem, or that the user didn't follow the deployment guidelines. I have seen this happen already with prior authorization tools. In 2023 a Senate investigation found that UnitedHealth's nH Predict algorithm was denying post-acute care claims at a rate that bore essentially no relationship to the clinical judgment of the treating physicians, and the company's defense was more or less that the tool was a guide, not a decision-maker. That is exactly the kind of accountability gap that gets people hurt.⏎⏎And I think the safety argument is being systematically underweighted in this conversation. A tool that performs well on average can still fail badly for specific patients, and medicine is full of patients who are not average. Elderly patients with multiple comorbidities, patients with rare genetic conditions, patients whose symptoms don't fit the textbook presentation, patients whose records are incomplete because they've moved or been uninsured for years. These are exactly the patients who get hurt by pattern-matching systems trained on data that didn't include them in sufficient numbers. I keep thinking about the well-documented problems with pulse oximetry in patients with darker skin tones, which took decades to fully acknowledge despite being a relatively simple device. AI systems are far more complex and the bias is far harder to see.⏎⏎I'm also worried about what AI does to the clinician workforce over time. The argument that AI reduces burnout is plausible in narrow cases, but the same tools can easily become productivity monitors, standardization mechanisms, and justification for staffing cuts. If a hospital can demonstrate that an AI flags 85% of deteriorating patients before a nurse would, that creates pressure to run thinner nursing ratios. That's not hypothetical. There are already hospital systems piloting "AI-assisted" monitoring as part of arguments to state regulators that minimum staffing ratios are unnecesary.⏎⏎HHS asks what it can do to accelerate adoption. My answer is that acceleration is not obviously the right goal right now. What would actually serve patients is rigorous post-market surveilance requirements, mandatory disclosure to patients when AI is involved in a clinical or coverage decision, and clear rules about who is legally liable when an AI-influenced decision causes harm. None of those things require slowing down beneficial innovation, but they do require treating AI in healthcare as something that needs to earn trust rather than something that should be assumed trustworthy until proven otherwise.⏎⏎The RFI doesn't ask directly about liability frameworks, but I'd urge HHS to coordinate with whoever does have jurisdiction over that question because the gap there is real and it's going to matter before very long.♔♔2026-02-10T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-10T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI-powered insurance decision-making automates prior authorization denials and coverage restrictions in ways that are opaque, unappealable, and systematically biased against vulnerable patients.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔angry♔Iowa♔research director♔47♔A 2024 landscape analysis conducted across 47 affiliated research institutions and health systems found that 68% identified reimbursement ambiguity as the primary barrier to deploying validated AI clinical decision-support tools, outranking technical integration challenges and workforce readiness concerns combined. SYNTH-0006♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔David Taylor♔Carbon Health♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Advocate Lutheran General Hospital in Park Ridge, Illinois in February of 2023. She had a septic infection that the attending physicians initially thought was a straightforward UTI. What saved her was not a pattern-matching algorithm. It was a night nurse who noticed that her breathing had changed in a way that didn't match any of the numbers on the monitors, who called the resident, who called the attending, who made a judgment call at 2am based on thirty years of watching people get sick. That chain of human attention and human responsibility is exactly what I'm afraid gets broken when we start routing clinical decisions through systems that nobody can fully explain and nobody is clearly accountable for.⏎⏎I want to address specifically the question of what HHS should do to "accelerate" AI adoption in clinical settings, because I think the framing of this RFI is itself the problem. The whole document assumes that faster adoption is the goal and asks only how to remove the obstacles. But the obstacles are sometimes there for good reasons. Validation requirements, liability frameworks, physician oversight rules - these aren't just bureaucratic friction. They exist because healthcare errors kill people and someone needs to be answerable when that happens.⏎⏎What worries me practically is the insurance side of this. AI-driven prior authorization systems are already being deployed by major payers and there is documented evidence that some of these systems have denial rates that far exceed what physician reviewers would produce. A ProPublica and CBS News investigation in 2023 found that UnitedHealth's nH Predict algorithm was flagging patients for discharge from rehabilitation facilities at rates that internal reviewers couldn't justify clinically. That's not theoretical harm. Those are real patients who got less care because an algorithm said so and because the system made it difficult for anyone to push back effectively. Accelerating AI adoption in that context doesn't mean better care. It means faster, cheaper, and harder to contest.⏎⏎There's also something I keep coming back to which is the question of who actually benefits from moving quickly here. The vendors benefit. Large hospital systems that can afford to buy and implement these tools benefit, at least in terms of cost reduction. I'm genuinely uncertain whether patients benefit on average, and I'm pretty confident that patients in under-resourced settings, rural hospitals, federally qualified health centers, places that can't afford the good implementations and will end up with whatever's cheapest, those patients may end up worse off. I don't see this RFI grappling seriously with that distributional question.⏎⏎I'm not saying no AI tools should ever be used in healthcare. I'm saying that "accelerate" is the wrong verb. The right question is how do we ensure that any tool that influences a clinical or coverage decision has been validated in populations that look like the patients who will actually be affected by it, and that there is a real human being whose professional license is on the line if it goes wrong. Right now neither of those conditions is reliably met.⏎⏎HHS should be requiring much more rigorous post-market surveillance for AI tools that are already deployed, not looking for ways to lower the bar for new ones. The FDA's current framework for software as a medical device has significant gaps when it comes to adaptive algorithms that continue to change after they're cleared, and the agency has acknowledged this. Before the department encourages broader adoption, it should close that gap.⏎⏎I also want to say something about administrative AI because this RFI keeps implying that clinical AI and administrative AI are separable and that administrative uses are lower risk. They're not always. Scheduling algorithms that triage urgency, documentation tools that summarize patient histories for physicians who then don't read the underlying notes, coding tools that determine what diagnoses get submitted to payers - all of these have downstream effects on care. A physician who is reviewing an AI-generated summary of a patient's chart instead of reading the chart is not in the same epistemic position as one who read it themselves. That matters and I don't think it's being taken seriously enough in how these tools are being categorized and regulated.♔♔2026-01-25T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-25T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔The AI healthcare market is dominated by a small number of powerful vendors whose commercial interests in rapid deployment conflict with patient safety, and HHS's acceleration agenda risks embedding those vendors permanently into critical healthcare infrastructure.♔organization♔high♔angry♔Georgia♔chief medical officer♔60♔A 2024 internal deployment analysis across 47 health system partners found that clinical AI tools integrated directly into physician workflows reduced documentation burden by an average of 34 minutes per provider per day, yet 61% of those same health systems reported that reimbursement uncertainty and liability ambiguity were the primary barriers preventing broader rollout beyond pilot units. SYNTH-0007♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Andrew Thomas♔University of Texas at Austin Dell Medical School♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February 2023 after a sepsis diagnosis that got missed for almost 36 hours. I'm not going to pretend I know exactly what went wrong. But I know this: the thing that finally caught it was a nurse who had been on that floor for nineteen years and noticed something was off about how my mother was breathing. Not a number on a screen. A person who had seen enough sick people to know when something wasn't adding up.⏎⏎I bring this up because the framing of this RFI bothers me. The question being asked is essentially how do we speed this up, and I think that's the wrong starting point. The question should be whether we should be doing this at all, or at least whether we should be doing it as fast and broadly as the vendors and health systems are pushing for.⏎⏎Medicine is not primarily an information problem. I know that's not a popular thing to say right now, but I believe it. A patient who comes in terrified and can't describe their symptoms clearly, or an elderly person with no family present who doesn't really understand what the doctor is asking, or someone whose chart is full of errors from a previous provider who didn't really know them -- none of that is a pattern matching problem. It's a relationship problem. A judgment problem. And when you put an AI layer between the clinician and the patient, even if the AI is technically accurate, you are changing what the encounter is. You are making it more transactional. The clinician starts managing recommendations instead of actually thinking. I've read enough about automation bias to know that people tend to follow what the machine says even when they shouldn't.⏎⏎The bias issue is also something I want to flag specifically. There's documented research -- the Obermeyer et al. study from 2019 in Science is probably the most cited example -- showing that widely deployed commercial algorithms systematically underestimated the health needs of Black patients because they used healthcare costs as a proxy for health needs, and Black patients had been historically denied care and therefore had lower costs. That algorithm was used on millions of patients before the problem was identified. Now we're talking about deploying AI much more broadly, much faster, with much less scrutiny. The fact that one tool got caught doesn't mean the others will.⏎⏎What really worries me though is the insurance side. The question of AI in clinical care is partly a question about what counts as medically necessary and who decides. If insurers are using AI to generate denial recommendations, and they are, and those systems are opaque, and the clinician who wants to appeal has to fight through a prior authorization process that is already exhausting, adding AI into that chain makes accountability worse not better. There's no one to argue with. There's no one who made the decision. There's just a score.⏎⏎I'm also not persuaded by the argument that AI will reduce clinician burnout. That argument is convenient for hospital systems that want to reduce staff. The Electronic Health Record was also sold as something that would make clinicians' lives easier. Ask any physician what they think of their EHR documentation burden now. The tools that promised to free up time have mostly added more data entry, more compliance checkboxes, more things to click through. I don't see why AI tools deployed in a similar institutional context would behave differently.⏎⏎HHS should at minimum require pre-deployment validation studies that are specific to the patient population and care setting where a tool will actually be used, not just the population where it was trained. It should require that any AI used in coverage or denial decisions be subject to independent audit. It should require that clinicians have a documented, functional way to override AI recommendations without it affecting their performance metrics or generating additional paperwork.⏎⏎But honestly my larger concern is that this RFI is being conducted in a way that treats acceleration as the default and caution as the thing that needs to be justified. I'd like to see HHS ask a different question: what evidence do we require before allowing AI to influence clinical decisions, and who bears the burden of proof. Right now it feels like the burden is on patients and skeptics to prove harm after the fact, which is backwards.⏎⏎The nurse who noticed something wrong with my mother's breathing had no algorithm. She had experience and attention and the professional obligation to trust her own judgment. I don't know how you quantify that or whether an AI system trained on a million sepsis cases would have caught what she caught. Maybe it would have. But maybe it would have flagged fifty other patients that day as higher priority and she would have been in a different room.♔♔2026-02-05T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-05T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔The AI healthcare market is dominated by a small number of powerful vendors whose commercial interests in rapid deployment conflict with patient safety, and HHS's acceleration agenda risks embedding those vendors permanently into critical healthcare infrastructure.♔academic_organization♔high♔hopeful♔North Dakota♔assistant professor♔51♔A 2024 CAP member survey encompassing responses from more than 1,200 board-certified pathologists across academic medical centers, community hospitals, and independent laboratories found that 68% had encountered at least one AI-assisted diagnostic tool in their practice environment, yet only 23% reported that their institution had established formal validation protocols governing clinical deployment of those tools. SYNTH-0008♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Brian Baker♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Inova Fairfax Hospital in 2022 after a sepsis diagnosis that almost got missed entirely, and what saved her was a night-shift nurse who noticed she was shivering in a way that didn't match her chart. Not an algorithm. A person who had been doing that job for sixteen years and recognized something she couldn't have put into a data field. I've been thinking about that a lot since I heard about this RFI.⏎⏎What bothers me most about the framing of this whole effort is the assumption that the problem in American healthcare is insufficient automation. That the answer to a system that already feels impersonal, rushed, and transactional is to add more tools that process people as inputs and outputs. My mother, during those eleven days, was scared and sometimes confused and occasionally said things that didn't make sense because she was running a fever of 103. An AI system trained on patient records would have had a very hard time with her. The nurse knew to discount certain things she said and weight other things more heavily, and that judgment came from being a human being in a room with another human being.⏎⏎I understand there are people who will say AI is just a tool, that clinicians will still make the final call, that this is about reducing administrative burden not replacing doctors. I've heard that argument and I don't entirely dismiss it. But I've also watched what happens when institutions get a new tool that promises efficiency. The tool gets used more broadly than intended, the humans who were supposed to remain "in the loop" get stretched thinner because the tool is supposedly handling things, and within a few years the original caution is forgotten. That's not speculation, that's just how healthcare organizations respond to cost pressure.⏎⏎The specific question I want to address from this RFI is the one about reimbursement structures that could accelerate AI adoption in clinical settings. My concern is that if Medicare and Medicaid start reimbursing AI-assisted diagnosis or AI-assisted prior authorization review at higher rates, or if payers start preferring or requiring AI tools as a condition of contracts, you will create a financial incentive that runs way ahead of any evidence base. We already have a problem in this country where medical devices and software get deployed at scale before we really understand their failure modes. The FDA's 510(k) clearance process has been criticized for decades for allowing devices to reach patients without rigorous clinical testing, and AI diagnostic tools present a similar or worse problem because their failures can be subtle, correlated across large populations, and invisible until something goes badly wrong.⏎⏎The bias issue is real and I don't think it gets taken seriously enough in these conversations. Training data in American healthcare reflects decades of undertreatment of Black patients, dismissal of women's pain complaints, and underrepresentation of elderly patients with multiple comorbidities in clinical trials. A system trained on that data will reproduce those patterns at scale, and it will do so with an air of objectivity that makes the bias harder to see and harder to challenge than when a human doctor does the same thing. At least when a human is biased you can confront them.⏎⏎I work in social services, not medicine, so I don't have a technical background here. But I've watched a lot of vulnerable people navigate healthcare systems and what they need is more human contact, not less. The populations who already get the worst care, elderly people, people with disabilities, people who don't speak English as a first language, people with serious mental illness, are exactly the populations who will be hardest for AI systems to serve well and easiest for institutions to justify deprioritizing once a machine has flagged them as complicated or low-probability-of-good-outcome.⏎⏎I really think HHS should be asking harder questions about who benefits from accelerating this adoption. Vendors benefit. Some large health systems with capital to invest benefit. It's less clear to me that patients benefit, and it's pretty clear to me that frontline healthcare workers don't benefit in any way that actually improves their working conditions rather than just adding a layer of machine oversight to their already overloaded days.⏎⏎There's also something I keep coming back to about accountability. When an AI system contributes to a wrong diagnosis or a wrongful denial of coverage, who is responsible? The hospital will say the clinician should have caught it. The clinician will say the tool gave them bad information. The vendor will say the tool was used outside its validated parameters. I've seen this dynamic play out already with prior authorization AI tools that insurance companies have been using to deny claims, and there are lawsuits pending right now, including one involving UnitedHealth Group's use of an AI model with a reported 90% error rate on post-acute care denials, that should give everyone pause before we decide the answer is more AI faster.⏎⏎The administrative burden argument is the one I find most persuasive for AI use, honestly. If AI can genuinely reduce the time clinicians spend on documentation without changing what gets documented or how it gets used, that seems worth exploring carefully. But "carefully" is doing a lot of work in that sentence, and I'm not confident that's how it will go.♔♔2026-01-03T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-03T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare diffuses responsibility across clinicians, hospitals, and vendors in ways that leave patients without a clear accountable party when errors cause harm.♔individual♔high♔frustrated♔Colorado♔librarian♔40♔When my mother was being evaluated for a cardiac procedure at a regional hospital outside Pueblo, the cardiology team mentioned, almost in passing, that an AI flagging tool had identified an anomaly in her echocardiogram that the first reviewing technician had marked as insignificant. SYNTH-0009♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Nancy Clark♔University of Chicago Center for Health and the Social Sciences♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Advocate Lutheran General Hospital outside Chicago in March of 2023, and I watched what good nursing actually looks like up close. It is not pattern matching. Her nurse, a woman named Darlene who had been doing this for twenty-two years, caught something in my mother's breathing that the monitors weren't flagging. She couldn't have told you exactly what she noticed. She just knew. That is not a data point. That is not something a training set captures.⏎⏎So when I read that HHS is looking for ways to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care, my first reaction is honestly a kind of dread. Not because I think technology is bad, I use it constantly, but because "accelerate adoption" sounds like a decision that has already been made and the comment period is more of a formality. I hope that's wrong.⏎⏎The question I want to address is the one about what regulatory actions HHS can take to support AI use in clinical settings. My answer is that before you do anything to accelerate this, you should require proof that it works for the patients who are already underserved, not just proof that it works on average. Average performance in healthcare is a misleading number. A system that is 94% accurate sounds impressive until you realize what it means to be in the other 6% when you're having a stroke or your chest pain gets coded as anxiety because you're a Black woman in her forties and that's what the historical data looks like.⏎⏎There's a lot of talk about AI reducing clinician burnout, and I don't doubt that some administrative uses might genuinely help. But I am worried, and I think this is a legitimate worry, that "reducing burden on clinicians" becomes a justification for thinner staffing. If an AI tool can flag deteriorating patients, a hospital administrator might decide you need fewer nurses per floor. That is not a hypothetical. Hospitals have been doing this kind of math for decades, and they will do it again. The efficiency gains get captured by the institution, and the risk gets distributed onto patients.⏎⏎I'm also not sure who is accountable when an AI recommendation turns out to be wrong. This is the part that keeps me up at night more than anything else. If Darlene makes a call and it's the wrong call, there is a person, a license, a process. If an algorithm from a vendor whose contract is buried in a hospital's procurement system makes a recommendation that a tired resident follows without questioning, and something goes wrong, the chain of responsibility just dissolves. The vendor says the clinician should have used their judgment. The hospital says the vendor's tool was validated. The clinician says they were following the system. Nobody is responsible and the patient is just gone.⏎⏎I think HHS should require, before any acceleration happens, mandatory prospective studies in real clinical environments with diverse patient populations, not retrospective validation on cleaned datasets. And those studies should be publicly available, not proprietary. The FDA's 510(k) pathway for software as a medical device is not sufficient for tools that are making or influencing clinical decisions in real time, and HHS knows this.⏎⏎One more thing that I don't see discussed enough: the administrative AI applications. Insurance companies are already using algorithmic tools to deny prior authorizations, and there is documented evidence from the Senate Finance Committee's 2023 investigation into Medicare Advantage plans that denial rates from AI-assisted review systems were dramatically higher than from human reviewers, and that many of those denials were later overturned on appeal. That's not efficiency. That's obstruction dressed up as automation. If HHS is serious about patient welfare, the RFI should have asked specifically about that use case and whether it should be restricted rather than expanded.⏎⏎I realize this comment is focused more on caution than on answering the specific questions in the RFI the way a policy expert would. SYNTH-0010♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Nancy Adams♔American Academy of Orthopaedic Surgeons♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Regions Hospital in St. Paul in February of 2023, and I can tell you with absolute certainty that what got her through it was not pattern recognition. It was a night-shift nurse named Delores who noticed something was off about the way my mother was breathing even though the monitors said everything was fine. Delores had been doing this for twenty-two years. She called the attending at 3am. My mother had a pulmonary embolism that the automated early warning system had not flagged. So when HHS asks how to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care, my first reaction is something between a laugh and a cold fear, because I am not sure the people writing this RFI have spent enough time thinking about what gets lost.⏎⏎I want to focus on two things specifically. One is the question of what AI actually does to clinical judgment, and the other is who benefits economically when hospitals adopt these tools. I am not going to pretend I have no understanding of the other side. I know clinicians are burned out, I know documentation is crushing, and I know there are radiology contexts where AI has shown real accuracy gains. I am not a complete skeptic. But the way this RFI is framed worries me, because it treats adoption as the goal rather than as a means to an end, and that framing tends to produce policy that pushes speed over scrutiny.⏎⏎On clinical judgment: medicine requires something that I do not think AI can replicate, which is the ability to hold uncertainty without resolving it prematurely. A good clinician can look at a patient and say I don't know what's wrong yet, and sit with that, and keep looking. AI systems are built to produce outputs. They are trained on historical data, which means they reflect past decisions, including past mistakes and past biases. The ProPublica investigation into the COMPAS recidivism algorithm is not a healthcare story, but it is a story about exactly what happens when you trust a scoring system over human judgment and don't audit it carefully enough. Healthcare already has its own version of this problem. The pulse oximetry data is one example that's gotten more attention lately: devices that were less accurate on patients with darker skin tones, validated on populations that didn't represent everyone, deployed for decades before anyone really pushed back. AI systems trained on EHR data from institutions that have historically undertreated Black patients or undertreated women's pain are going to encode those patterns. And the output will look authoritative. That is the dangerous part.⏎⏎On the economic question, I keep thinking about what prior authorization AI means in practice. Insurance companies are already using algorithmic tools to deny claims faster. There was a ProPublica and CBS News investigation published in late 2023 that found a former Cigna medical director reviewed and denied over 60 patient claims in one day, guided partly by algorithmic recommendations. Sixty in one day. The question of whether that's a human decision or an AI decision starts to feel like a semantic argument at that point. HHS asking how to accelerate this is, in that context, asking how to give those systems more legitimacy and more speed, not more accountability.⏎⏎I understand that's not the only use case. But the RFI doesn't draw a hard enough line between tools that assist clinicians in ways clinicians can verify and override, and tools that are being used to remove clinicians from the loop or to reduce the time clinicians spend per patient. Those are categorically different things and they should not be treated as different points on a spectrum of adoption. One is augmentation and the other is replacement, and the reimbursement structures HHS designs will determine which one wins in practice.⏎⏎There's also something that I think gets underweighted in these policy conversations, which is what happens to the healthcare workforce. Radiologists in training, medical students learning to read an ECG, nurses developing clinical intuition over years of practice - all of that expertise develops through repetition and through making judgments and getting feedback. If AI handles the straightforward cases and humans only see the edge cases, the pipeline for developing that judgment collapses over time. We don't have good research yet on what that does to the profession over twenty years, and I am not sure HHS should be pushing adoption faster than that research can keep up.⏎⏎The question of accountability is where I want to end, though I realize this is getting long. When Delores called the attending at 3am, there was a chain of human accountability that I could follow. I knew who made the call, I knew who was responsible, and if something had gone wrong I would have known who to ask. With AI-assisted care, that chain gets murky in ways that are not just philosophical. Vendors say the tool is a decision support aid, not a decision maker. Clinicians say they were following the recommendation. Hospitals say they deployed it according to the vendor's instructions. This is not a hypothetical breakdown; it is already happening in malpractice litigation around AI-assisted diagnostic tools. HHS should be thinking much harder about that accountability gap before it designs incentives to make these tools more widespread.♔♔2026-01-03T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-03T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI-powered insurance decision-making automates prior authorization denials and coverage restrictions in ways that are opaque, unappealable, and systematically biased against vulnerable patients.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔urgent♔Indiana♔research director♔37♔A 2024 survey of 2,170 dental practices conducted through the Dental Standards Institute's annual membership review found that 61% had evaluated at least one AI-based diagnostic tool—primarily for radiographic caries detection and periodontal bone loss assessment—yet fewer than 18% had proceeded to full clinical integration, citing ambiguous reimbursement pathways and the absence of clear federal performance benchmarks as the dominant barriers. SYNTH-0011♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Thomas Thompson♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Cedar Rapids in March of 2023 after a sepsis diagnosis that her primary care doctor had missed twice. What saved her, ultimately, was a hospitalist who sat with her for forty minutes, noticed she was holding her left side in a way that didn't match the chart, and ordered an additional scan that hadn't been on any protocol. I think about that a lot when I read about AI being positioned as the solution to diagnostic gaps in medicine.⏎⏎Nobody is asking whether we lose something when that kind of attention gets replaced, or even supplemented, by a system that is fundamentally pattern-matching on historical records. My mother is an 74-year-old woman with atypical symptom presentation who had never been hospitalized before. There is no meaningful training set for her. The whole point is that the doctor had to throw out the pattern.⏎⏎I want to respond specifically to the question this RFI poses about what regulatory actions HHS can take to accelerate AI adoption in clinical settings, because I think that framing is already pointing the wrong direction. The question assumes acceleration is the goal. I'd argue the department should be asking whether deployment should happen at all in certain contexts before it asks how to speed things up.⏎⏎The safety issues here are not hypothetical. The literature on sepsis prediction algorithms, for instance, includes the Epic Sepsis Model, which was studied independently by researchers at UCSF and published in JAMA Internal Medicine in 2021. They found it missed more than two thirds of sepsis cases that clinicians caught and generated an enormous number of false alerts. Hospitals were already using it widely before that study came out. That is the pattern I am worried about: tools get deployed, they seem impressive in a vendor demonstration or a pilot, and by the time rigorous independent evaluation happens the system is already embedded in workflow and people have started deferring to it.⏎⏎And I do think people defer. That is not speculation. There's research on automation bias going back decades, and it is particularly concerning in environments where clinicians are exhausted and understaffed, which describes most American hospitals right now. If a system flags something or doesn't flag something, that changes behavior even when clinicians know they are supposed to override it. The liability and accountability structure in most institutions makes clinicians reluctant to document that they acted against the recommendation of a deployed tool. So in practice, the tool's judgment starts to dominate even when the nominal policy says the human is in charge.⏎⏎I'm genuinely worried that the people most harmed by this will be patients who already have the hardest time advocating for themselves. Elderly patients. Patients with limited English. Patients with mental illness. Patients in rural hospitals where staffing is thin and there is pressure to rely on automated systems precisely because there aren't enough people. These are also the patients whose data is probably least well represented in whatever training sets these algorithms were built on.⏎⏎I understand there's an argument that AI could help with administrative burden and that maybe that's lower risk than clinical decision support. But honestly I'm skeptical of that too. Insurance prior authorization is an administrative function and we already know that automated denial systems are being used to override physician judgment at scale. Cigna was reported in 2023 to have had medical reviewers spending an average of 1.2 seconds per denial decision. That is not a human reviewing a claim. That is a human providing cover for an automated recommendation. Calling it "AI-assisted" doesn't change what it is.⏎⏎HHS should at minimum require that any AI tool used in a clinical or coverage decision context be subject to independent prospective validation before deployment, not just post-market surveillance that vendors can largely manage themselves. The FDA's current framework for software as a medical device doesn't really capture the ongoing drift that happens when a model gets used in populations and settings that weren't part of the original validation. And I don't see anything in this RFI that suggests HHS is seriously grappling with that problem rather than looking for ways to get out of the way of industry.⏎⏎I'm not opposed to technology in medicine in some blanket way. I just think the burden of proof should be on the people deploying these systems to show they work for real patients in real settings, not on patients and clinicians to prove after the fact that something went wrong. The way this RFI is written, the working assumption seems to be that adoption is being slowed by unnecessary friction and that HHS's job is to remove it. I think the friction is sometimes doing important work and we should be careful about what we're removing.⏎⏎There's also no serious discussion in the framing of this RFI about♔♔2026-01-30T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-30T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI-powered insurance decision-making automates prior authorization denials and coverage restrictions in ways that are opaque, unappealable, and systematically biased against vulnerable patients.♔individual♔medium♔concerned♔Delaware♔nurse♔52♔During a twelve-hour shift at Christiana Hospital, I watched a physician spend forty minutes hunting through conflicting medication records in our EHR system while a post-op patient's pain went unmanaged — and I kept thinking, if we had a reliable AI tool flagging those discrepancies in real time, that patient wouldn't have suffered waiting. SYNTH-0012♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Christopher Lopez♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Riverside University Health System in Moreno Valley in February 2023. The way one particular resident, a young woman who couldn't have been more than 28, figured out that my mother was refusing to eat because she was embarrassed, not because she wasn't hungry. No algorithm would have caught that. I don't know how you'd even begin to train one to catch it.⏎⏎I want to respond specifically to the question about what barriers HHS should remove to accelerate AI adoption in clinical settings, because my answer is that I think HHS is asking the wrong question. The question shouldn't be what's slowing AI down. It should be whether we actually want AI making or influencing clinical decisions at a faster pace than we can understand what it's doing.⏎⏎What concerns me is the framing of this whole RFI. It assumes the answer is yes, we want more AI, and now we're just working out details. But the case for going slower is serious and I don't think it's being taken seriously enough. Medicine is full of situations where a system that works well on average fails badly for a specific person. A Black patient with kidney disease, an elderly woman with atypical cardiac symptoms, someone who doesn't speak English and can't describe their pain accurately. These aren't edge cases, there are tens of millions of people in those situations. AI tools trained on historical data will inherit whatever biases were already in that data, and the problem is that an AI system looks authoritative even when it's wrong. A doctor who is wrong can be questioned. A score or a flag from an AI system tends to become the floor of the conversation rather than one input among many.⏎⏎I also want to say something about the insurance side of this, because the RFI mentions administrative functions. Insurers are already using automated systems to deny claims faster than any human reviewer could have processed them. UnitedHealth's use of an AI model called nH Predict to deny post-acute care for Medicare Advantage patients was the subject of a federal lawsuit in 2023, and reporting suggested its denial rate was far higher than what individual medical reviewers would have determined. That is a real, documented example of AI being used not to improve care but to cut costs at patients' expense. HHS accelerating AI adoption in administrative functions without serious guardrails would very likely produce more of that, not less.⏎⏎I understand there are genuine uses for AI that might help clinicians. I'm not saying the technology is worthless. But the way this RFI is structured, asking for ways to remove barriers and accelerate adoption, puts HHS in the position of a promoter rather than a regulator. The question of who is responsible when an AI-influenced decision harms a patient is genuinely unresolved. Vendors disclaim liability. Hospitals say they're using tools that were cleared by FDA. Clinicians say they were following what the system recommended. That chain of non-responsibility is already a problem in a small number of cases. Scaling up AI use before that accountability question is answered seems like a serious mistake.⏎⏎What I would ask HHS to do is pause the acceleration framing entirely and instead ask: what would it take to make any AI tool used in a clinical or insurance decision provably safe for the specific populations it will be used on, with a named human who is legally accountable for its outputs?⏎⏎That is a harder question and it would slow things down considerably. SYNTH-0013♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Matthew Lewis♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's Hospital in Kansas City in February of 2023, and what got her through it was not a pattern-matching algorithm. It was a night-shift nurse named Delia who noticed something was off in how my mother was holding her left arm and flagged it to the attending before the chart said anything was wrong. That observation came from thirty years of sitting with sick people. I don't know how you train a model on that.⏎⏎I'm responding specifically to the question about what barriers HHS should address to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care, because I think the framing of that question is already wrong. The assumption seems to be that the barriers are the problem and adoption is the goal. What if the barriers exist for good reasons? What if some of them are just called "medicine"?⏎⏎The thing that worries me most, honestly, is not any single AI tool. It's the cumulative pressure. Hospitals are already understaffed and under financial pressure. When a vendor comes in and says their system can reduce documentation time by 40% or flag deteriorating patients before nurses notice, administrators hear "we can cut FTEs." I've talked to nurses in Missouri and Illinois who are already being told that AI-assisted monitoring means their floor can run with fewer people overnight. That is not a hypothetical future concern. That's happening now. The AI doesn't replace a nurse on paper, it just creates the conditions where the hospital feels justified in not replacing the one who left.⏎⏎There's also a bias problem that I don't think gets taken seriously enough. The datasets these systems train on reflect decades of unequal care. Black patients have historically been undertreated for pain, undertested for certain conditions, seen less frequently. A system trained on that history will reproduce it, and it will do so with a confidence score attached, which makes it harder to argue with than a human clinician whose reasoning you can at least question out loud. I'm not a researcher, I don't know all the technical details, but I've read enough to know that ProPublica documented this in the criminal justice context with the COMPAS tool back in 2016, and there's no obvious reason healthcare AI is immune to the same dynamics.⏎⏎What I'd ask HHS to consider is this: before accelerating anything, require that any AI system used in a clinical or insurance decision context be subject to independent auditing for disparate impact by race, income, disability status, and age. Not vendor self-certification. Independent auditing. And require that the results be public. If the system can't pass that, it shouldn't be deployed regardless of how well it performed in a controlled trial at an academic medical center.⏎⏎The insurance piece especially concerns me. Prior authorization is already a nightmare. My father-in-law's pulmonologist in Topeka spent something like four hours last year fighting an automated denial for a CT scan that was pretty clearly indicated. The insurer's system flagged it as not meeting criteria. Nobody could explain exactly which criteria or why. If AI gets embedded deeper into that process, and I have every reason to think it will because it saves insurers money, the accountability problem gets worse not better. At least with a human reviewer you can theoretically appeal to someone. With an algorithm making the first-pass decision, and then a human rubber-stamping it because the queue is too long to actually review each case, you've just added a layer of false legitimacy to a denial process that was already pretty opaque.⏎⏎I understand there are genuine use cases where AI may help, I'm not saying every application is bad. But HHS is asking how to accelerate adoption, and I think the more responsible question is how to ensure that whatever gets adopted actually helps patients and doesn't just help the financial interests of the institutions deploying it. SYNTH-0014♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Sandra Allen♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Regions Hospital in St. Paul in February of 2023, and during that time the thing that mattered most was not the monitoring equipment or the lab results. It was the night nurse who noticed she seemed more confused than her numbers suggested and pushed for a second opinion on her medication dosing. A computer looking at her chart would have seen stable vitals. A human being saw something else. That distinction is the whole argument, as far as I'm concerned.⏎⏎What worries me about this RFI is that HHS seems to be asking how to go faster, not whether to go at all. The questions are framed around acceleration, adoption, removing barriers. There is almost no language about what happens when these tools are wrong, or who is responsible, or what patients lose when clinical judgment gets quietly replaced by a recommendation engine that a busy hospitalist learns to rubber-stamp because they have fourteen other patients.⏎⏎I want to say something about the bias question because I think it gets underplayed in these kinds of policy conversations. The data that trains these systems comes from decades of medical practice that was itself biased. There is documented evidence that pain in Black patients has historically been undercoded, undertreated, underbelieved. If you train a sepsis prediction model or a pain management algorithm on that history, you are not correcting for bias, you are compressing it into software and then calling it objective. The model will look authoritative. It will generate numbers and confidence intervals. Nobody in the room will feel comfortable saying the number is wrong. That is actually more dangerous than the original human error, because at least a human clinician can be questioned, can change their mind, can be shamed or sued or retrained.⏎⏎The employment piece also deserves more attention than I've seen in most of these discussions. I keep reading that AI will reduce clinician burnout by handling documentation. Maybe. But I work in healthcare administration and I can tell you the conversations I've heard in the last two years are not mostly about reducing burnout. They are about FTE reduction. They are about whether you still need a human coder or a human prior authorization specialist once you have a tool that does it faster. And prior authorization is already a nightmare for patients. Automating it does not make it more humane. It makes the denial faster and the appeal process more opaque, because now you are arguing with a system that cannot tell you why it decided what it decided except in terms that no patient can interpret.⏎⏎I am genuinely uncertain whether there is any version of AI in clinical care that I would feel comfortable with, and I realize that probably sounds extreme. I am not opposed to technology. I use the same EHR systems everyone else does, I understand the value of decision support for drug interactions, things like that. But there is a meaningful difference between a system that flags a potential adverse drug reaction and a system that generates treatment recommendations or triages patients or decides who gets approved for a procedure. The first one is a tool. The second one is practicing medicine, and I don't think we've had an honest public conversation about whether we want software doing that.⏎⏎The accountability gap is the thing I keep coming back to. When my mother's nurse intervened, she was accountable. Her license was on the line. Her professional judgment was on the line. She had skin in the game in the most literal sense. When an algorithm makes a recommendation and the clinician follows it and the patient is harmed, the accountability just sort of dissolves. The vendor says the clinician should have used their judgment. The clinician says they were following standard of care based on available tools. The hospital says they implemented the system according to guidance. Nobody is actually responsible in the way that matters, which is the way that would have prevented the harm in the first place.⏎⏎I think HHS should be asking much harder questions about liability before any of this goes further, and I would also want to see independent validation requirements that are not funded or designed by the vendors themselves. The current framework for software as a medical device is already struggling to keep up with what's being deployed, and adding acceleration on top of that♔♔2026-02-16T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-16T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Even administrative healthcare functions that appear routine require human contextual judgment that AI cannot replicate, and automating them will produce systematic errors that harm patients and workers.♔individual♔high♔resigned♔New York♔retired nurse♔48♔During my final two years on the medical-surgical floor at Westchester Medical Center, I watched my charge nurse spend forty minutes every shift manually reconciling discharge summaries because the documentation system flagged half her entries for physician review — a bottleneck that existed purely because no one had bothered to train the software on how nurses actually document. SYNTH-0015♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Barbara Brown♔Harvard Medical School Department of Biomedical Informatics♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February of 2023 after a sepsis diagnosis that got missed on her first ER visit. She's fine now, but what I keep thinking about is what actually saved her: a night nurse who noticed something was off about the way she was holding her arm, who asked a question that wasn't on any checklist, and who pushed back on the attending until someone ordered a second blood panel. That nurse had been doing her job for 22 years. She knew my mother was scared and wasn't describing her symptoms accurately because she didn't want to be a bother. No algorithm would have caught that. I don't know how you train a model on "patient is downplaying pain because she doesn't want to inconvenience anyone."⏎⏎I want to respond specifically to the questions HHS is asking about what actions the department can take to accelerate AI adoption in clinical settings, because I think the framing of this RFI is itself the problem. The question isn't how do we accelerate this, it's whether we should be accelerating it at all and who bears the risk when we get it wrong.⏎⏎The safety question gets glossed over too quickly in most of these conversations. AI systems trained on historical patient data will inherit whatever biases were baked into how care was actually delivered, which means they will systematically underperform for Black patients, for low-income patients, for people with disabilities, for people whose conditions don't fit the typical presentation because their demographics were underrepresented in whatever training set the vendor used. There's documented evidence of this already. A widely cited 2019 study in Science found that a commerically used healthcare algorithm was less likely to refer Black patients for extra care because it used cost as a proxy for need, and Black patients historically receive less expensive care even when they're sicker. That wasn't a fringe product. It was being used on tens of millions of patients. Now imagine that kind of bias embedded in AI tools that are making triage recommendations or flagging patients for follow-up, and imagine the liability is diffuse enough that nobody is clearly responsible when outcomes diverge along racial lines.⏎⏎And that's the piece that I think really needs to be addressed before HHS does anything to accelerate deployment: accountability. Right now, when an AI system makes a bad recommendation and a clinician follows it and a patient is harmed, the legal and institutional responsibility is genuinely unclear. The vendor says the clinician should have exercised judgment. The hospital says they followed the vendor's guidance. The clinician says the system presented the recommendation with high confidence and there was no practical way to override it in the workflow. The patient or their family is left with no clear path to recourse. That's not a hypothetical scenario, it's already happening with prior authorization AI tools that insurance companies are using to deny claims automatically. There have been lawsuits. There was a congressional inquiry in 2023 into UnitedHealth Group's use of an AI model called nH Predict to deny post-acute care claims at a rate that multiple courts found violated Medicare Advantage rules. That's the real world version of AI in healthcare, not the demo version.⏎⏎What I'd actually want HHS to do is slow down and require that any AI tool used in a clinical or coverage decision context be subject to prospective clinical validation in the specific population where it will be used, not just the population it was trained on. I'd want mandatory disclosure to patients when AI played a role in a coverage decision or a clinical recomendation (I know there are existing transparency provisions but they're not being enforced in any meaningful way). And I'd want a moratorium on AI-driven prior authorization until there's a clear legal framework establishing that insurers using these tools are fully liable for outcomes, not partially liable, not "shared" liability with some software company in a jurisdiction with favorable terms.⏎⏎The efficiency argument for AI keeps getting made as though efficiency is a neutral good in healthcare. It's not. Efficiency in a for-profit healthcare system often just means finding faster ways to deny care or reduce staff. The hospitals and insurers who are most eager to adopt AI are frequently the same ones with the worst records on patient outcomes and worker treatment.♔♔2026-02-21T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-21T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI-powered insurance decision-making automates prior authorization denials and coverage restrictions in ways that are opaque, unappealable, and systematically biased against vulnerable patients.♔academic_organization♔high♔supportive♔Iowa♔PhD researcher♔32♔A 2024 ACC survey of 2,847 cardiologist members found that 63% reported using at least one AI-assisted diagnostic or workflow tool in their practice, yet 71% of those respondents identified inconsistent reimbursement pathways as the primary barrier to broader implementation.
