SYNTH-0016♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Michelle Wilson♔Epic Research♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in March of 2023, and what I kept watching was not her monitors but the nurses. The way a nurse would walk in, take one look at her, and know something was off before any machine said so. That kind of perception is not a dataset. I don't know what you call it exactly, but I know it saved her life at least once when her pressure readings were technically stable and everything was "within normal range" and a nurse named Delia just didn't like the way she was breathing and called the resident anyway. No algorithm flagged that. A person did.⏎⏎So when I read that HHS is looking at ways to accelerate AI adoption in clinical settings, my honest reaction is dread. Not because I'm against technology in general, I use it constantly, but because I think there's a real confusion happening between what medicine is and what it looks like from the outside. From the outside it looks like information processing. Lots of data, lots of decisions, lots of patterns. Feed it into a model, get better outputs. But from the inside, from the patient side of the bed, medicine is something else entirely. It's someone being accountable to you. Personally accountable, in a way that matters.⏎⏎What I want to focus on here is the question of who bears responsibility when something goes wrong, because I don't think HHS has grappled seriously with that. When a physician makes a mistake, there is a chain of accountability. Licensing boards, malpractice law, hospital credentialing, peer review. It's imperfect but it exists. When an AI system makes a mistake, that chain becomes a maze. The hospital says it followed the vendor's guidelines. The vendor says the model performed within its stated parameters. The clinician says she was relying on the tool's recommendation. The insurer says coverage decisions were made by the system, not a human. And the patient is left with an injury and no one who is clearly, unambiguously responsible. I have read about this happening already with AI-driven prior authorization denials. UnitedHealth's nH Predict tool was the subject of a class action lawsuit in 2023 precisely because it was allegedly denying post-acute care claims at a rate that didn't match clinical reality, and patients and families couldn't get a straight answer about who had actually made the decision. That's not a hypothetical. That already happened.⏎⏎I'm also skeptical of the argument that AI will reduce clinician burnout. Maybe it will for some tasks. But I think there's an equal or greater chance that it becomes a justification for cutting staff, increasing caseloads, and then pointing to AI tools as evidence that the remaining clinicians can handle more. We've seen this in other industries. Technology gets adopted, productivity expectations go up, and workers end up doing more with less while the efficiency gains flow somewhere else. There's no reason to think healthcare is immune to that dynamic, and there are specific reasons to think it might be worse, because the costs of getting it wrong are borne by patients who are often not in a position to complain effectively.⏎⏎The populations I worry about most are elderly patients with complex comorbidities, people with limited english proficiency, people with psychiatric conditions that affect how they present symptoms, and people who are simply frightened and don't give a clean history. These are exactly the patients for whom standardized pattern-matching is least reliable. They're also, not coincidentally, the patients who are most likely to be in settings where cost pressures are highest and where AI adoption might be most aggressively pushed.⏎⏎I'm not asking HHS to ban research or stop all development. But the question this RFI is asking, what can we do to accelerate adoption, assumes that acceleration is the right goal. I don't think it is. I think the right goal is careful, limited, thoroughly validated deployment in very specific use cases with robust human override requirements and genuine liability clarity before any of this scales. The word "accelerate" in the title of this request worries me more than anything else in it.⏎⏎Also I want to flag that the comment period for this RFI closes on what seems like a fairly short timeline given the scope of what's being asked, and a lot of the people most affected by these decisions, patients, bedside nurses, home health aides, don't have lobbyists or policy teams monitoring federal registers.♔♔2026-01-29T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-29T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔angry♔Minnesota♔research director♔59♔A 2024 survey conducted across 612 member laboratories found that 47% have deployed at least one AI-based diagnostic algorithm in routine clinical workflows, yet 78% of those sites reported that reimbursement ambiguity—specifically the absence of dedicated CPT codes or coverage determinations for AI-assisted interpretation—was the primary barrier to broader implementation. SYNTH-0017♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Emily Mitchell♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in 2019 after a sepsis diagnosis that was initially missed for almost 36 hours. What eventually caught it was a nurse who noticed something was off about how she was breathing and pushed back on the attending. Not a score, not an algorithm. A person who had been doing that job for twenty-two years and had a feeling. I keep thinking about that when I read materials like this one, because everything in this RFI seems to treat the "problem" of clinical care as fundamentally a data problem, and I don't think it is.⏎⏎I want to address the question about what regulatory actions HHS should take to accelerate AI adoption in clinical settings, because I think the framing of the question itself is the problem. The question assumes acceleration is the goal. I'd argue the opposite: HHS should be looking at where to put the brakes on, or at minimum build in much longer evaluation periods before any of this gets normalized.⏎⏎Here is what concerns me most practically. AI tools in healthcare are being validated in specific hospital systems, often large academic medical centers with relatively clean and complete EHR data, and then sold to community hospitals and rural clinics where the patient population is different, the data is messier, and the staff don't have the same resources to catch errors when the tool gets something wrong. The FDA cleared a sepsis prediction algorithm a few years back that performed well in the original study, and then a 2021 analysis in Nature Medicine found it missed more than two thirds of sepsis cases in real-world deployment and generated a huge number of false positives. That's not a minor discrepancy. That's a tool that could genuinely make care worse while giving everyone the impression it's making care better. I don't know how HHS accelerates adoption in that environment without significantly increasing patient risk.⏎⏎There's also the insurance piece that I think gets underplayed. When the RFI talks about administrative functions, it's including prior authorization and coverage decisions. Insurers are already using algorithmic tools to generate denial recommendations at speeds no human reviewer could match. UnitedHealth was sued in 2023 specifically over an AI system that was allegedly overriding physician judgment to deny post-acute care for elderly patients. That case is still working through the courts as far as I know. Calling that an administrative efficiency is, I think, a pretty sanitized way to describe what's actually happening to real patients. My concern is that once HHS signals it wants to accelerate AI adoption broadly, that signal gets used by payers to justify exactly this kind of automation, and there's no clear line between clinical AI and administrative AI in the way the rule seems to imply.⏎⏎I also think the workforce question is being handled too gently in this RFI. Hospitals are already using AI adoption as cover for staffing decisions. If a documentation tool can generate a clinical note in thirty seconds, the argument becomes that you need fewer people doing documentation, which sometimes means fewer people on the floor noticing things. The nurse who caught my mother's sepsis wasn't doing something that shows up in an efficiency metric.⏎⏎What I'd actually want to see from HHS is a mandatory post-market surveillance requirement for any AI tool used in clinical decision support, with public reporting of real-world performance data broken down by patient demographics. Not the validation data from the original study. What it actually does in practice, in different kinds of hospitals, over time. That doesn't exist right now in any consistent way, and I don't see how you can responsibly talk about acceleration when you don't have that infrastructure in place.⏎⏎I also think there should be explicit rules about when AI-generated outputs can and cannot be the basis for insurance coverage decisions, and what level of human review is actually required, not just nominally present.♔♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare diffuses responsibility across clinicians, hospitals, and vendors in ways that leave patients without a clear accountable party when errors cause harm.♔individual♔high♔resigned♔Idaho♔construction worker♔71♔My orthopedic surgeon in Boise ran an AI-assisted imaging tool to flag stress fractures in my wrist after thirty years of framing houses — caught a hairline break the first radiologist had missed on the plain film. SYNTH-0018♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Kenneth White♔Illumina♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in 2021 and I watched what good nursing care looks like up close. The nurses knew when she was in pain before she could say it. They noticed she wasn't eating right, they caught a medication interaction that the overnight resident missed, and one of them sat with her for twenty minutes at 2am just because she was scared. None of that was in a chart. None of that was a data point. I have been thinking about that experience constantly since I heard HHS was soliciting comments on accelerating AI adoption in clinical care, and I have to say my reaction is pretty close to alarm.⏎⏎The RFI asks about barriers to AI adoption and what HHS can do to remove them. I want to suggest that some of those barriers exist for good reasons and the department should think hard before treating them as obstacles to be cleared away.⏎⏎Here is what concerns me most. When you push AI into clinical decision-making, you don't just add a tool. You change who is responsible. The clinician who used to make a call based on their training and their read of the patient now becomes someone who either agrees with the algorithm or has to justify overriding it. That's a real shift in accountability and I don't think it goes in a good direction. If the AI recommends a course of treatment and the doctor follows it and the patient is harmed, who is answerable? The vendor will say the doctor should have used clinical judgment. The hospital will say the vendor's product was validated. The doctor will say the system flagged it as low risk. That's not a hypothetical, there have already been cases where algorithmic tools used in sepsis prediction and in prior authorization decisions produced bad outcomes and the accountability question was genuinely murky.⏎⏎And the prior authorization piece is actually where I want to spend most of my comment because I think it gets less attention than the clinical side. Insurers are already using AI to automate denials. Cigna made headlines in 2023 when it came out that their system was rejecting claims at a rate that made it basically impossible for any human reviewer to have actually looked at the cases, something like 1.7 seconds per claim on average. That is not AI helping anyone. That is AI being used to put distance between a corporation and a decision that harms a patient, while making the decision look like it came from a neutral technical process rather than a cost-cutting incentive. HHS accelerating AI adoption without specifically and forcefully addressing this use case would be, in my view, irresponsible.⏎⏎I know there are people who will say AI can reduce burnout, flag drug interactions, help with documentation. I'm not completely dismissing that. But the documentation argument in particular feels like a bait and switch to me. Clinicians are drowning in paperwork because of billing requirements and liability concerns and insurance prior auth demands, and AI that helps document more efficiently doesn't fix any of those underlying problems, it just makes it slightly more bearable to work inside a broken system. That's not nothing, but it's not the same as improving care.⏎⏎The populations I worry about most are elderly patients, patients with psychiatric conditions, patients who don't speak English well, and patients in rural areas who may be seeing providers who have less training and fewer resources to push back when an AI recommendation seems off. These are also, not coincidentally, the populations whose data is most likely to be underrepresented in whatever training sets these tools were built on. A system trained mostly on data from large academic medical centers in the northeast is going to perform differently in a critical access hospital in rural Mississippi and there is not nearly enough scrutiny of that gap in how these tools get validated before deployment.⏎⏎I don't think HHS should ban AI outright. I'm not sure that's even a coherent ask. But I do think the framing of this RFI, which is oriented almost entirely around acceleration and adoption, gets the emphasis wrong. The question shouldn't be how do we remove barriers to AI in healthcare. It should be how do we make sure that when AI is used, someone is genuinely accountable for the outcome, the tool has been validated in the actual setting where it's being deployed, and there is a meaningful way for patients and clinicians to understand and contest what the system is recommending.⏎⏎On that last point, contestability, I don't see nearly enough attention in current discussions. If a patient is denied coverage or a treatment is recommended based in whole or part on an algorithmic output, they should have a right to a plain-language explanation of how that output was generated and a real human review process that isn't just a rubber stamp. Right now that's often not the case and the systems are opaque enough that even the clinicians using them don't fully understand what's driving the recommendations.⏎⏎I'm also genuinely worried about what happens to the healthcare workforce. Not just job losses, though that's real, but the devaluation of expertise that happens when institutions start treating clinical judgment as something that needs to be checked against a machine. There's a nurse I know who has been doing wound care for 30 years. She can look at a wound and tell you things that no photograph captures. If her hospital starts requiring algorithmic sign-off on wound care protocols, what message does that send her? And if the algorithm is wrong, does she have the institutional support to say so, or does she face pressure to defer?⏎⏎I realize I've been somewhat all over the place here. The short version of my comment is: please do not treat acceleration as the goal. The goal should be patient safety, clinician accountability, and equitable outcomes, and AI may or may not serve those goals depending on how carefully it's implemented, where, by whom, and with what oversight structures in place. The RFI as written makes me nervous because it seems to assume the answer is yes and is just asking how to get there faster.⏎⏎One more thing that I haven't seen addressed anywhere in the background materials: what happens to data that gets fed into these systems? If a hospital uses an AI documentation tool and that tool's vendor is also selling analytics services to insurers, there is a potential conflict that patients are not being told about and that I don't think current consent frameworks are equipped to handle.♔♔2026-02-27T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-27T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔3♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI systems in healthcare inherit and amplify racial, socioeconomic, and institutional biases from training data, and their apparent authority makes those biases harder to detect and correct than human error.♔organization♔high♔hopeful♔North Dakota♔practice manager♔49♔A 2024 operational survey of PINC's network of more than 1,500 affiliated hospitals and health systems found that 68% of participating facilities had deployed at least one AI-assisted clinical decision support tool, yet fewer than 30% reported having a formal governance framework aligned with current CMS or ONC guidance. SYNTH-0019♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Melissa Baker♔MIT Computer Science and Artificial Intelligence Laboratory (CSAIL)♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in Cincinnati in February of 2023, and I watched what good nursing looks like up close. I watched a nurse named Darlene notice that my mother's breathing had changed in a way that wasn't showing up on any monitor yet. She didn't run an algorithm. She just knew, from years of sitting with sick people, that something was shifting. That kind of knowledge doesn't get captured in a training dataset.⏎⏎I'm responding specifically to the questions HHS is asking about what regulatory actions might accelerate AI adoption in clinical care, and my answer is: please don't. Or at least slow down considerably and think harder about what you're actually building.⏎⏎The framing of this RFI bothers me. It asks how to accelerate adoption, not whether to accelerate it. That's a significant assumption buried in the question itself. There are real reasons to be skeptical that faster is better here.⏎⏎What worries me most, and I keep coming back to this, is the accountability problem. Right now if a doctor makes a wrong call, there's a person who made that call. They can be questioned, disciplined, sued, or simply asked to explain themselves to a grieving family. When an AI system contributes to a bad outcome, who answers for it? The hospital says they were following the vendor's guidance. The vendor says the hospital deployed it outside its validated parameters. The clinician says the screen said one thing and they trusted it. I've seen how hard it already is to get straight answers after a medical error. Adding a machine into that chain of custody for decisions seems like it makes accountability significantly worse, not better.⏎⏎There's also something that doesn't get said enough in these conversations, which is that AI in healthcare isn't being proposed primarily to help patients. It's being proposed because it might reduce costs and increase throughput. I understand why hospitals and insurers care about that. But "reduce costs" in healthcare often means fewer nurses per patient, fewer minutes per visit, fewer second opinions. If AI tools let administrators justify cutting staff because the machine is handling more of the cognitive load, the people who pay for that are patients, specifically the ones who most need human attention and are least able to advocate for themselves.⏎⏎I've read some of the research on AI diagnostic tools and the accuracy numbers can look impressive until you dig into which populations were in the training data and which weren't. There was a widely cited study, I believe published in Science around 2019, that found a commercial algorithm used by millions of patients was significantly less likely to refer Black patients for additional care compared to equally sick white patients, specifically because it used prior healthcare costs as a proxy for health needs. That's not a corner case. That's a fundamental problem with what happens when you optimize a model on historical data in a system that already has inequities baked in. I don't have confidence that we've solved that problem. I don't think the vendors have solved it either, they've just gotten better at talking about it in their marketing materials.⏎⏎I want to be honest that I don't think every use of AI in healthcare is wrong. Scheduling software and billing code assistance probably aren't going to hurt anyone. But the RFI seems to be pointing toward much more consequential applications, diagnostic support, treatment recommendations, insurance prior authorization. Those are places where errors have real consequences for real people.⏎⏎The prior authorization piece in particular. Insurers are already using automated systems to deny claims at scale, and patients and doctors have almost no visibility into how those decisions get made. Giving that process more AI infrastructure before there are serious transparency and appeals requirements in place seems like exactly the wrong order of operations. My neighbor spent four months fighting a denial for a medication her rheumatologist had prescribed, and she never once got a straight answer about what criteria the insurer was applying or who had actually reviewed her case.⏎⏎I think HHS should be asking different questions. Not how do we accelerate adoption, but what harms are already occurring from existing deployments, what recourse do patients have when AI contributes to a bad outcome, and whether the current legal and regulatory framework is even capable of assigning responsibility in these situations. I suspect the answer to that last one is no, and that seems like something that should be sorted out before the technology is further embedded into clinical workflows.♔♔2026-02-27T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-27T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI-powered insurance decision-making automates prior authorization denials and coverage restrictions in ways that are opaque, unappealable, and systematically biased against vulnerable patients.♔academic_organization♔high♔resigned♔North Dakota♔biostatistician♔36♔A 2024 CAP member survey encompassing responses from over 1,200 pathology practices found that 68% reported active evaluation or deployment of at least one AI-assisted diagnostic tool, yet 74% of those practices identified the absence of standardized clinical validation frameworks as the primary barrier to broader integration. SYNTH-0020♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Barbara Moore♔Natera♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Regions Hospital in St. Paul in February of 2023, and I want to be honest that this experience shapes everything I'm about to say. The care she received was extraordinary not because of any technology but because a pulmonologist named Dr. Reyes sat with our family for forty minutes on a Tuesday night and talked us through what was happening in language we could actually understand. He was tired. He probably had six other patients. But he stayed, and he answered every question, and he looked my mother in the eye. I keep thinking about what that interaction would have looked like if half his attention was going to reviewing AI-generated flags and alerts and risk scores on a dashboard somewhere.⏎⏎That's the thing HHS doesn't seem to be asking in this RFI. The questions are all about how to accelerate adoption, how to reduce barriers, how to align reimbursement with AI use. There's no question about what we lose. There's no question about whether some of what AI is replacing was actually doing important work that we can't quantify.⏎⏎Medicine is not just an information matching problem. I know that sounds obvious but I think the whole framing of this RFI treats it like one. Patients are scared and confused and sometimes they can't describe their symptoms clearly, and they present differently depending on who's in the room and whether they trust that person. A clinician reading body language, picking up on something a patient said offhandedly, noticing that the family member in the corner looks exhausted and frightened - that is not inefficiency waiting to be optimized. That is the actual work. And there's good reason to think that layering AI recommendations into that process pushes it toward something more transactional, where the clinician starts unconsciously anchoring to the machine's output rather than doing their own independent assessment.⏎⏎There's also the bias question, which I don't think gets taken seriously enough. AI systems trained on historical health data are trained on decades of undertreatment of Black patients, dismissal of women's pain reports, inadequate care for people on Medicaid versus private insurance. The 2019 Obermeyer study in Science - which I'm sure HHS is familiar with - showed that a widely used commercial algorithm was systematically underestimating the health needs of Black patients because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for health needs, and Black patients historically received less care even when equally sick. That's not a bug that got fixed and went away. That's a structural feature of what happens when you train systems on real-world data from a healthcare system that has never treated everyone equally.⏎⏎And I worry a lot about the insurance side of this, maybe even more than the clinical side. The RFI mentions administrative functions almost as an afterthought, but prior authorization denials powered by AI are already affecting real patients right now. UnitedHealth's use of an algorithm called nH Predict to deny post-acute care claims was the subject of a class action lawsuit filed in 2023, with plaintiffs alleging the algorithm had a 90 percent denial rate and was overriding physician judgments. That's not a hypothetical future harm. That's happening while HHS is asking how to accelerate adoption.⏎⏎What I'd want HHS to actually do is slow down and require that any AI system used in a clinical or coverage decision context be subject to mandatory prospective bias audits before deployment - not retrospective reviews after harm has already occurred. And I'd want there to be a clear legal rule that when an AI system contributes to a denial or a clinical recommendation that causes harm, the vendor and the institution share liability. Right now the liability structure is so diffuse that nobody is really accountable. The clinician says they were following the system, the hospital says the vendor validated it, the vendor says it was used outside the approved indication.⏎⏎I'm also genuinely worried about what this does to the workforce over the next decade. Not just job losses, though that's real, but the slower erosion of clinical judgment and expertise that happens when residents and nurses and even experienced physicians start relying on algorithmic recommendations as a crutch. Skills atrophy. If a radiologist spends ten years reviewing AI-flagged images rather than doing independent reads first, what happens to their ability to catch the thing the AI missed?⏎⏎The RFI asks about what regulatory actions HHS could take to accelerate AI adoption. My answer is that HHS should instead be building a much more robust framework for when AI use in healthcare is actually appropriate and safe before any acceleration happens. The pace right now is being set by vendors with financial incentives, not by evidence of patient benefit. And the reimbursement proposals being floated would essentially pay providers to adopt tools that have not been rigorously validated in the specific populations and settings where they'd be used.⏎⏎I recognize there are probably some narrow applications where AI genuinely helps - certain imaging analyses, maybe some administrative scheduling functions that don't involve clinical judgment. But the RFI isn't asking about narrow applications. It's asking how to make AI adoption broadly faster, and that framing itself is the problem.⏎⏎One more thing that keeps nagging at me: once these systems are embedded in the EHR workflow, they are almost impossible to remove. Epic and Cerner and the major vendors build these tools into the core interface. A hospital that signs a five-year contract with an AI vendor is not going to be in a position to pull that tool after two years even if evidence of harm starts accumulating, because the whole clinical workflow has been reorganized around it. The switching costs become a reason to keep using something even when you have doubts about it.♔♔2026-02-06T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-06T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔3♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI systems in healthcare inherit and amplify racial, socioeconomic, and institutional biases from training data, and their apparent authority makes those biases harder to detect and correct than human error.♔organization♔high♔concerned♔Alabama♔practice manager♔59♔A 2024 deployment analysis across 47 clinical sites integrating Onboard AI's decision-support tools found that 68% of physicians cited documentation burden as the primary barrier to consistent AI utilization, with the average clinician spending 2.3 additional minutes per encounter reconciling AI-generated outputs against legacy EHR fields that lack standardized AI input mappings. SYNTH-0021♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Stephanie Hill♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's Medical Center in Milwaukee in February of 2023 after a sepsis diagnosis that her primary care doctor had nearly missed twice. What saved her was not an algorithm. It was a second-year resident who walked into the room, looked at her, looked at my father's face, and said something is wrong here that the numbers aren't telling me. That resident ordered a blood culture panel that wasn't in any protocol. I keep thinking about what happens to that instinct when we hand everything over to a system that was trained on historical data and optimizes for the average patient.⏎⏎I want to focus my comments on the question of how HHS is thinking about clinical safety review before these tools are deployed, and also on the workforce question, because I don't think the RFI takes the employment and deskilling concern seriously enough.⏎⏎On safety: the framing of this RFI is almost entirely about acceleration. The word "accelerating" is right there in the title. But healthcare AI tools are already being deployed faster than the evidence supports. A 2023 review in JAMA found that the overwhelming majority of AI tools approved or cleared by the FDA had been validated on data from a single institution. Single institution. That means a tool trained mostly on patients from, say, a large academic hospital in the northeast will get deployed in a rural federally qualified health center in Mississippi and nobody will have any systematic way to know whether its performance holds up. Hospitals are buying these products because vendors are good at sales and because administrators see labor cost savings, not because there's clear evidence the tool works in their specific patient population.⏎⏎There's also the bias problem and I don't want to just name it and move on because I think people have started treating it like a checkbox issue. The bias in these systems isn't always obvious. It doesn't show up as the tool refusing to treat Black patients. It shows up as the tool consistently undertriaging them, or recommending watchful waiting where it would recommend intervention for someone else, because the training data reflected decades of undertreament and that undertreament looks, to the algorithm, like a different baseline. The tool is just learning what happened. It doesn't know that what happened was wrong.⏎⏎The workforce piece is something I feel strongly about and I don't think I'm being a luddite here. The argument that AI will reduce clinician burnout is plausible in narrow cases but it's also exactly what every labor-displacing technology promises before it displaces labor. Radiology is already seeing consolidation driven partly by AI-assisted reads being used to justify fewer radiologists covering more images faster. That's not burnout reduction. That's speedup. And when something gets missed, who is accountable? The radiologist who reviewed 400 studies that day because the AI was supposed to flag anything serious? The vendor who sold a tool with a 94% sensitivity rate in the trial population but never disclosed what the false negative rate looks like for patients over 80 with comorbidities?⏎⏎I think HHS should require, before any AI clinical decision support tool can be marketed to Medicare and Medicaid participating institutions, a prospective multi-site validation study that includes the specific demographic and geographic populations the tool will actually be used with. Not a retrospective chart review. An actual prospective study. And the results should be publicly posted, not buried in a 510(k) submission that takes a FOIA request to access.⏎⏎The administrative AI question bothers me too, maybe even more in some ways, because I think people assume that because it's "just" prior authorization or coding or scheduling, the stakes are lower. They aren't. Prior authorization denials cost people their medications, their surgeries, their lives sometimes. Automating denials with AI doesn't make them more accurate. It makes them faster and harder to contest, because now you're arguing with a black box and the insurance company can say the tool made the recommendation, not the human reviewer. That's a feature for the insurer. It is absolutely not a feature for the patient sitting at home trying to figure out why their insulin pump request was denied for the third time.⏎⏎I recognize there are genuine potential benefits here and I'm not saying never. I'm saying the burden of proof should be on the people deploying these systems, not on the patients and workers who have to live with the consequences. Right now that burden is basically inverted.⏎⏎One more thing I didn't see addressed clearly in the RFI: what happens to existing contracts when a tool is later found to have systematic errors? Are institutions required to disclose adverse events attributable to AI recommendations the same way they report other adverse events? Because right now I don't believe there's any consistent requiremnet for that and that seems like a pretty significant gap before we start talking about acceleration.♔♔2026-02-04T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-04T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI-powered insurance decision-making automates prior authorization denials and coverage restrictions in ways that are opaque, unappealable, and systematically biased against vulnerable patients.♔individual♔low♔hopeful♔Alaska♔construction worker♔45♔My husband Ricky tore his rotator cuff on a job site in Fairbanks two winters back, and getting a specialist appointment meant driving six hours to Anchorage and waiting three months just to confirm what the ER doc already suspected. SYNTH-0022♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Joseph Jackson♔American College of Emergency Physicians♔♔♔♔♔My mother was in the hospital for six weeks in 2022 with a complicated post-surgical infection, and I watched what it looked like when a care team actually functioned as a care team. Her wound care nurse, a woman who had been doing that job for over twenty years, caught something one afternoon that the attending had missed because she knew my mother, had been watching her for days, and noticed that her affect was slightly off in a way that didn't show up in any chart. That observation led to a sepsis workup that probably saved her life. I keep thinking about what happens to that kind of judgment when you start routing clinical decisions through a system that has never met my mother, cannot read her face, and was trained on data from institutions that may not look anything like the hospital in Fresno where she was being treated.⏎⏎The question HHS is asking about accelerating AI adoption is framed as though the main barriers are bureaucratic and the solution is to remove friction. But I think the friction is sometimes doing real work. When a physician has to stop and think through a diagnosis rather than confirm a machine recommendation, that pause is not inefficiency. It is medicine.⏎⏎What concerns me most, and I'll be honest that I'm not sure exactly how to articulate this, is that the push toward AI in clinical settings feels like it is being driven more by what AI can do technically than by any clear evidence that it improves outcomes for real patients in real settings. The studies I've seen cited tend to come from controlled environments or from the vendors themselves, and the populations studied often don't reflect the diversity of patients who show up at safety-net hospitals. There was a widely discussed study, I believe it was published in Science around 2019, that found a commercial algorithm used by millions of patients was systematically underestimating the health needs of Black patients because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for health needs, and Black patients had historically received less care even when equally sick. That kind of bias doesn't announce itself. The system just keeps outputting numbers that look authoritative.⏎⏎I want to address specifically the question about what regulatory actions HHS could take to accelerate adoption, because I think the framing of that question is itself worth pushing back on. Acceleration assumes we know where we are going and that the main problem is speed. I don't think we know enough yet to be accelerating. We should be requiring much more rigorous post-market surveillance of AI tools already deployed in clinical settings. We should be asking who is liable when an AI-assisted diagnosis is wrong and the patient is harmed, because right now the answer to that question is genuinely unclear and that unclarity protects vendors more than patients. Hospitals can say the vendor's tool was approved. Vendors can say the hospital deployed it outside the validated use case. Clinicians can say they followed the recommendation they were given. The patient's family is left trying to figure out who to hold accountable and finding that responsibility has been distributed so widely it effectively disappears.⏎⏎There is also the employment piece, which I think gets dismissed too quickly in these discussions as being about workers protecting turf. It isn't only that. When you reduce staffing ratios because an AI system is supposedly handling part of the cognitive load, you are making a bet that the system won't fail in ways that require human intervention. And when it does fail, there may not be enough experienced humans around who remember how to catch the problem without the tool.⏎⏎I'm not saying AI has no place anywhere in medicine. I'm saying HHS should stop treating acceleration as the default goal and start asking harder questions about what safeguards need to be in place before any of this gets embedded deeply enough that reversing it becomes practically impossible. Once hospital systems build their workflows around a particular vendor's platform, and that vendor gets acquired or changes its pricing model or gets hacked, the institution is stuck in a way that affects patient care directly. That dependency risk is real and I have not seen it taken seriously in any of the materials HHS has put out so far.♔♔2026-01-02T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-02T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔concerned♔South Dakota♔research director♔34♔A 2024 analysis of our MS Navigator program data, drawn from interactions with more than 28,000 people living with MS, found that 67% reported experiencing delays of six months or longer in receiving a confirmed MS diagnosis — a window during which AI-assisted imaging interpretation and symptom-pattern recognition tools have demonstrated measurable diagnostic improvement in peer-reviewed trials. SYNTH-0023♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Barbara Wright♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in Cincinnati in February of 2023, and I watched nurses spend more time documenting in the EHR than they spent at her bedside. I am not opposed to technology on principle. But what I keep coming back to, reading through this RFI, is the question nobody seems to want to ask directly: what problem are we actually trying to solve, and for whom?⏎⏎The push to "accelerate AI adoption" in clinical care treats speed of adoption as obviously good. I don't think that's obvious at all. When my mother was in the ICU, what mattered was that her nurse noticed she was more agitated than her vitals suggested, and made a judgment call that turned out to be right. That is not a pattern matching problem. That is a human being paying attention to another human being in a way that cannot be cleanly separated from the fact that they were both human. I worry that we are building systems that will make it easier to replace that kind of attention with something that looks like attention but isn't.⏎⏎On the specific question of what regulatory actions HHS should take, I want to focus on two things.⏎⏎First, the reimbursement question. The RFI asks about reimbursement structures that could support AI adoption. My concern is that reimbursement incentives will do what reimbursement incentives always do, which is push the behavior that gets paid for regardless of whether it's actually better for patients. If CMS creates billing codes or incentive payments tied to AI tool deployment, hospitals will deploy AI tools. That does not mean patients will be better off. The evidence base for most clinical AI tools is genuinely weak. A 2023 review in the BMJ looked at hundreds of AI diagnostic tools and found that the vast majority had never been tested in a real clinical environment outside the institution that built them. Paying for deployment before we have that kind of external validation seems backwards.⏎⏎Second, and this is the one I feel more strongly about, the administrative and insurance side. The RFI frames AI in insurance decisions somewhat gently, but what we're really talking about is prior authorization automation. United Healthcare's use of an AI model called nH Predict to deny post-acute care claims was the subject of a class action lawsuit filed in 2023. The lawsuit alleged the model had a 90% overturn rate on appeal, meaning that when humans reviewed the denials, they were wrong nine times out of ten. That is not efficiency. I don't know what to call it exactly but it is not efficiency.⏎⏎There's also something that bothers me about the framing of this whole RFI, which is the assumption that the main barriers to AI adoption are regulatory friction and slow reimbursement. That might be true for some vendors. But for clinicians and patients, the barriers might include things like: the tools don't work well enough, the tools introduce new liability questions nobody has answered, the tools are trained on data that underrepresents certain populations, and nobody has figured out who is responsible when an AI recommendation contributes to a bad outcome. Those aren't friction problems. They're substantive problems, and accelerating past them doesn't make them go away.⏎⏎I think HHS should be asking hospitals and health systems to demonstrate clinical benefit, not just deployment, before any new reimbursement pathways are created. SYNTH-0024♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Jessica Nguyen♔UnitedHealth Group♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February of 2023 and what I remember most is how much it mattered that her night nurse, a woman named Deb, actually knew her. I don't think that's true, and I think HHS should be extremely cautious before using its regulatory and reimbursement authority to push AI into clinical settings faster than we actually understand what we're doing.⏎⏎The question I want to respond to is the one about what HHS can do to encourage adoption. My answer is: less than you think, and more slowly than you're planning.⏎⏎Here's what worries me concretely. AI tools can perform well in the research setting where they were validated and then behave very differently in actual deployment, in community hospitals with incomplete records, with patient populations that don't look like the training data, with overworked staff who don't have time to second-guess a recommendation that comes out of a system the hospital paid a lot of money for. People trusted it.⏎⏎What I'd actually want HHS to do is slow down on the reimbursement incentives side. You'll get adoption without accountability.⏎⏎The employment piece also doesn't get talked about enough honestly. I've heard the argument that AI will reduce clinician burnout by handling administrative tasks, and maybe that's true in some narrow cases. Insurance companies already use algorithmic tools to deny claims at scale, and patients have almost no recourse when that happens. Accelerating AI adoption in that context means accelerating a process that is already failing people.⏎⏎I'm not saying AI has no role anywhere ever. SYNTH-0025♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Linda Moore♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February of 2023 after a sepsis diagnosis that her primary care physician's office had flagged as "low risk" using an automated triage tool. I'm not saying the tool caused her near-death. I genuinely don't know. But nobody could tell me what it had recommended or why, and that uncertainty has stayed with me ever since.⏎⏎What I want to say to HHS is this: the question you're asking in this RFI is framed wrong. You're asking how to accelerate AI adoption. That framing assumes acceleration is the goal. I'd ask you to slow down and think about whether embedding these systems more deeply into clinical care is actually something patients have consented to, structurally, as a society. We haven't had that conversation.⏎⏎Medicine works because a specific human being is accountable to you. Your doctor can lose their license. They can be sued. They can look you in the eye when they've made a mistake. When an algorithm gets it wrong, who do you call? The vendor will say the hospital misconfigured it. The hospital will say the clinician should have overridden it. The clinician will say they were following protocol. I've seen this exact argument play out, not in my mother's case but in a lawsuit I read about involving an insurers AI-driven prior authorization system that denied a post-surgical rehabilitation stay and the patient deteriorated at home. No one was held accountable in any meaningful way.⏎⏎The bias problem is also not theoretical. There's documented evidence that some sepsis prediction algorithms perform worse on Black patients because the training data reflected historical disparities in who got admitted to ICUs and when. If you accelerate deployment of these tools without fixing that, you are accelerating inequity. Faster is not better here.⏎⏎I also want to say something about the employment question because I think HHS is probably not thinking about it hard enough in this RFI. Medical coders, care coordinators, prior authorization nurses, patient navigators -- these are real jobs that real people with healthcare training do, and they do them with judgment that comes from talking to actual patients. My cousin is a care coordinator in Tulsa and she's already watching her department shrink. The hospital tells the staff it's "efficiency." What it actually is, is that an AI tool now handles the first pass on about 60% of the cases she used to manage, and they didn't backfill the two positions that left. So now she's managing higher complexity cases with less collegial support and more documentation burden because the AI outputs require human sign-off that takes longer than just doing the thing herself.⏎⏎That's the reality. Not the demo. The demo always works.⏎⏎I'm honestly not anti-technology as a general matter. I use electronic health records, I think telehealth saved my father-in-law from missing a medication adjustment during COVID. But there's a difference between tools that support clinical judgment and systems that supplant it or that quietly shift decision-making authority to vendors who have no clinical training and no accountability to patients.⏎⏎The question I'd actually want HHS to answer is: what happens when one of these systems is wrong in a way that harms thousands of patients before anyone notices? That's not a regulatory framework that warrants acceleration. The Epic deterioration index, for example, generated so many alerts at some institutions that nursing staff started ignoring them, which is probably worse than having no alert at all. That's alarm fatigue, and it's a well documented problem, and yet we're talking about adding more AI-generated signals into already overwhelmed clinical environments.⏎⏎What worries me just as much is the accountability question. When an AI system recommends a course of treatment and the clinician follows it and the patient is harmed, who is responsible? I have watched something like this play out with my mother's billing - not AI exactly, but an automated prior authorization system that denied a medication her cardiologist prescribed, and it took nine days and two appeals to get it approved. That is automation of denial. SYNTH-0028♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Anthony King♔Carnegie Mellon University Block Center for Technology and Society♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in 2019 after a medication interaction that three different physicians missed on rounds. And I want to push back on the premise before I get to any specifics, because the framing assumes that faster adoption is the goal and the only question is how to remove friction. That worries me. Some of the friction is there for a reason.⏎⏎What I want to address is the safety question and what I'd call the accountability question, because I think they're related in ways the RFI doesn't quite grapple with.⏎⏎On safety: healthcare is genuinely different from other domains where AI has shown promise. A recommendation engine that gets it wrong shows you a bad movie. A clinical decision support tool that gets it wrong can contribute to a missed diagnosis, a delayed treatment, an unnecessary procedure. The difference matters. And the problem isn't just that AI systems can be wrong, it's that they can be wrong in ways that are hard to detect and that look authoritative. Clinicians are already under enormous time pressure. If a tool gives a confident-seeming output, the realistic response in a busy ED or a short primary care visit is often going to be to accept it rather than interrogate it. That's not a failure of the clinician. That's just how cognitive load works.⏎⏎There's also a validation problem that I don't think gets enough attention. A system might be validated on data from academic medical centers in the northeast and then deployed in rural critical access hospitals with completely different patient populations, documentation practices, staffing ratios. The performance gap between those settings can be enormous. I've read studies suggesting that some sepsis prediction models show AUROCs that drop by 0.10 to 0.15 or more when moved outside their training environment. That's not a small margin. And there's no systematic requirement right now that I'm aware of that forces ongoing post-deployment monitoring in the way that, say, a new drug would require post-market surveillance.⏎⏎The accountability piece is maybe even harder. When a clinician makes an error, there is a person. There is a license. There is a malpractice system, however imperfect. When an AI system contributes to a bad outcome, the question of who is responsible becomes genuinely murky. The vendor says the clinician was supposed to exercise independent judgment. The hospital says the vendor represented the tool as validated. The clinician says they were following the system's recommendation because they had no reason to doubt it and no time to second-guess every output. That's not a hypothetical. That pattern is already showing up in litigation and in incident reports.⏎⏎I'm not saying AI has no place in healthcare. I'm genuinely not sure where I land on all of this. There are narrow applications, like flagging certain imaging findings for radiologist review, where the evidence seems more solid. But the push to accelerate adoption broadly, across clinical care and administrative functions and insurance decisions, feels like it's running ahead of the evidence and ahead of the governance structures we'd need to make it safe.⏎⏎HHS should be asking not just how to speed this up but whether certain uses should require demonstrated real-world performance data before deployment, whether there should be mandatory incident reporting when AI tools are involved in adverse events, and who bears liability when the tool and the clinician and the institution all point at each other.⏎⏎The administrative side is worth mentioning separately because I think it gets treated as lower stakes and it isn't. Prior authorization denials affect real patient care. If those decisions are being shaped or automated by AI systems trained on cost-minimization objectives, the consequences for patients can be just as serious as a clinical error. And the people on the receiving end of those denials, often already sick, often already dealing with a lot, have even less ability to push back against an opaque algorithmic decision than they would against a human one.⏎⏎I'd really like to see HHS focus this effort on governance, transparency requirements, and liability clarity before talking about incentives for faster adoption. The incentives for adoption are already enormous on the vendor side.♔♔2026-02-06T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-06T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Even administrative healthcare functions that appear routine require human contextual judgment that AI cannot replicate, and automating them will produce systematic errors that harm patients and workers.♔academic_organization♔high♔hopeful♔Massachusetts♔postdoctoral fellow♔45♔A 2024 CRA survey of 847 computing research faculty and postdoctoral fellows found that 68% are actively engaged in projects targeting clinical deployment of AI systems, yet 54% of those researchers identified fragmented reimbursement frameworks and unclear FDA/HHS oversight pathways as the primary barriers preventing their work from reaching patient care settings. SYNTH-0029♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Ashley Gonzalez♔Society of Critical Care Medicine♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's Medical Center in Milwaukee in February 2023, and not once did I feel like the nurses and doctors who cared for her were moving too slowly or making decisions that needed to be replaced by a computer. What I saw was people thinking hard about a complicated situation, talking to us, changing their minds, asking questions, and sometimes just sitting with her when she was frightened. I keep thinking about that when I read about efforts to bring AI into clinical care, because I genuinely cannot see what problem is being solved for patients like her.⏎⏎The question I want to address is the one about what barriers HHS thinks it should remove to accelerate AI adoption. My answer is that before removing barriers, someone should ask whether the barriers exist for good reasons. Informed consent processes, documentation requirements, liability frameworks, these aren't just red tape. They are the residue of things that went wrong. If AI tools are being held back by requirements that they demonstrate safety and effectiveness in real clinical populations before wide deployment, that is not a barrier. That is the point.⏎⏎What worries me most is that the push to accelerate adoption seems to assume that speed is a value in itself here. But consider what happened with early sepsis prediction algorithms. A study published in JAMA Internal Medicine in 2021 looked at Epic's sepsis prediction tool and found it performed significantly worse in real-world conditions than the company had claimed, generating large numbers of false alarms that nurses described as exhausting and distracting. The hospital systems that deployed it weren't doing anything wrong exactly, they were trying to save lives, but the gap between the validation data and actual performance was wide enough to cause real harm. That's one documented example of a well-funded, widely used system falling short, and there are more.⏎⏎There's also the question of who pays for these tools and who benefits from them. Hospitals and insurers are under enormous financial pressure, and AI gets sold to administrators as a productivity solution. I have no way to verify this from the outside, but I strongly suspect a significant portion of the AI deployment happening right now in health systems is driven less by clinical evidence and more by the desire to justify leaner staffing or to speed up prior authorization denials. That's not care improvement. When AI is used to automate the denial of claims, which UnitedHealth Group has already faced lawsuits over related to its nH Predict tool, the patient doesn't experience that as innovation. They experience it as getting told no by something they can't argue with.⏎⏎I want to be honest that I'm not opposed to technology in medicine as a general principle. I understand that there are things like imaging analysis where machine tools may genuinely help. But there's a difference between a specific, validated, narrowly scoped tool used as one input among many, and a broad institutional push to put AI into clinical workflows across the board before we understand what we're doing.⏎⏎The depersonalization concern is real and I don't think it gets taken seriously enough in these policy conversations. When a clinician is managing an AI dashboard and reviewing flagged cases rather than talking to a patient, something has shifted. The patient may not even know it happened. And if something goes wrong, the trail of accountability gets very murky very fast. The clinician says they followed the recommendation, the hospital says the vendor was responsible, the vendor says the clinician should have used judgment. That loop doesn't resolve cleanly and in the meantime a patient or their family is left without a clear answer about what happened or why.⏎⏎HHS should be asking vendors to provide disaggregated performance data by race, age, insurance status, and geography before any tool is used in a federally funded setting. There should be mandatory reporting when an AI system contributes to an adverse outcome, similar to how medical device adverse events get reported to the FDA. Right now I don't think that infrastructure exists in any consistent way and the absence of it means we're flying somewhat blind about the actual harm being caused.⏎⏎I'm also not sure the administrative AI argument is as safe as proponents suggest. Administrative work in healthcare isn't just form-filling. A billing specialist who has worked in oncology for fifteen years has contextual knowledge that shapes how they code, what they flag, what they question. SYNTH-0030♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Kenneth Garcia♔Cotiviti♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's Medical Center in Kansas City in February of 2023, and during that time I watched a parade of residents, nurses, and attending physicians piece together what was happening with her through conversation, observation, and a kind of slow collaborative reasoning that no system could have replicated. The algorithm was systematically underestimating the needs of Black patients because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for health needs, and Black patients had historically received less care, so the model learned to predict less care for them. This wasn't a fringe or obscure finding. It was published prominently and the algorithm was in use at hundreds of hospitals. That is not a problem you solve by moving faster. SYNTH-0031♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Michael Hall♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Advocate Christ Medical Center in Oak Lawn, Illinois in February of 2023, and what got her through it was not data. Not because I am against technology in the abstract, but because I think the framing of "acceleration" is exactly the wrong frame for this particular domain.⏎⏎The question I want to address is essentially the one about what barriers HHS should remove to help AI move faster into clinical settings. My answer is that some of those barriers exist for good reasons and removing them would be a mistake.⏎⏎Here is what concerns me most. When a hospital or insurer says they are implementing AI to improve care, what that often means in practice is that they are implementing AI to reduce costs and staff. I am not being cynical for the sake of it. There is a real pattern here. UnitedHealthcare's use of an algorithm called nH Predict to deny post-acute care claims was documented extensively in 2023, and a federal lawsuit alleged the system had a 90% overturn rate on appeal, meaning the AI was wrong nine times out of ten when patients pushed back. And the people most affected are elderly Medicare Advantage enrollees who often don't have the energy or resources to appeal.⏎⏎If HHS accelerates AI adoption without first putting hard rules in place around this kind of use, I think we will see more of it, not less. The economic incentive is too strong. Insurers and large hospital systems have every reason to use these tools to cut costs and shift decisionmaking into systems that are difficult to audit and even harder to challenge.⏎⏎I also want to say something about what gets lost when AI becomes the default layer between a patient and a clinical decision. Medicine involves a lot of ambiguity. Patients don't always describe their symptoms accurately. They leave things out because they're embarrassed or scared or they don't think it's relevant. A good clinician reads between the lines. An AI system trained on structured data does not read between the lines, it reads the lines, and if the lines are incomplete or misleading the output can be confidently wrong in a way that a human clinician would have caught because they would have asked a follow-up question or noticed the patient seemed to be holding something back.⏎⏎I am not saying there is no possible role for any kind of decision-support tool anywhere in medicine. SYNTH-0032♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Charles Harris♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother was admitted to a hospital in Cleveland in February 2023 with what turned out to be a relatively straightforward case of sepsis caught early. The question shouldn't be "what's slowing AI down" as though the slowness is obviously a problem. Sometimes friction is the system working. Hospitals requiring review, insurers needing human sign-off, clinicians pushing back on algorithmic recommendations - that's not inefficiency. That's accountability in action, and it's one of the few things standing between patients and errors that scale catastrophically.⏎⏎The bias problem is real and I don't think HHS is taking it seriously enough in how this RFI is written. Training data in healthcare is a mess. It reflects decades of unequal treatment, undercoding for certain populations, overdiagnosis in others. A 2019 study published in Science - the Obermeyer study - found that a widely used commerical algorithm was systematically underestimating the health needs of Black patients because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for health needs, and Black patients historically received less care, so they cost less, so the algorithm thought they were healthier. That's not a fixable edge case. That's the fundamental problem with using historical data in a system that produced inequitable history. Deploying more AI faster doesn't solve that. It scales it.⏎⏎What also concerns me, and I don't see it addressed anywhere, is the staffing question. The pitch for AI in healthcare keeps saying it will reduce burnout and let clinicians focus on what matters. Maybe. But hospitals are also laying off coders, billing specialists, prior authorization reviewers, and other staff while simultaneously rolling out AI tools to cover those roles. I'm not saying automation is inherently wrong but I am saying that when the same administrators making staffing cuts are also buying AI systems, I'm skeptical that patient care is the primary consideration. And those workers who get displaced - many of them have contextual knowledge about patients and institutions that doesn't transfer to any database. A prior auth reviewer who's worked a regional Medicaid plan for a decade knows things about that population that no model trained on claims data is going to replicate.⏎⏎The other thing HHS should think hard about before accelerating anything is what happens when these systems are wrong and someone gets hurt. Right now if a clinician makes an error there's a relatively clear path: malpractice liability, licensing boards, institutional review. If an AI system contributes to a bad outcome the accountability chain just dissolves. The vendor says the clinician should have overridden it. The hospital says they followed the vendor's implementation guidelines. The clinician says they trusted the system because they were told to. The patient or family is left with nothing. That's not a hypothetical, that's already happening in prior authorization denials where AI tools are rubber-stamping rejections and nobody can explain the reasoning or appeal it to a human who understands the case.⏎⏎HHS should be asking how to preserve human judgment, not how to replace it more efficiently. Those are different questions and the second one is much harder.⏎⏎The RFI asks about barriers to adoption, which tells you where the Department's head is already at. But the problem is that once you normalize the infrastructure, once hospitals have integrated these systems and vendors have contracts and reimbursement codes are built around AI-assisted billing, the scope creeps in directions that are very hard to reverse. The least consequential uses become the justification for the more consequential ones.⏎⏎There's also something that bothers me about the employment angle that doesn't get talked about honestly. They know that a referral from certain zip codes in their city means the patient probably can't afford the specialist copay and someone should flag that. That contextual knowledge doesn't appear in training data. It's not in the EHR. When you automate those roles, you don't just eliminate jobs, you eliminate the humans who were doing invisible, informal work that the metrics never captured.⏎⏎HHS should at minimum require that any AI system used in clinical or coverage decisions be subject to prospective bias audits by independent reviewers, not just the vendors, before deployment. Right now that doesn't exist in any consistent way. "The system recommended" is not an explanation.⏎⏎I'd also ask HHS to think carefully about the difference between AI tools that assist clinicians and AI tools that constrain them. A tool that surfaces information is different from a tool that gatekeeps a decision. The reimbursement and regulatory frameworks being built right now don't clearly distinguish between those two things, and that ambiguity is going to be exploited.⏎⏎My mother's nurse didn't have a protocol for what she noticed. SYNTH-0034♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Dorothy Hall♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Francis Hospital in Hartford in October 2022, and I watched nurses and doctors make judgment calls that no algorithm could have made. She was presenting with symptoms that didn't fit the textbook picture of what was actually wrong with her, and it took a physician who knew how to read her face, who asked my father about how she'd been sleeping, who noticed something about how she was holding her arm, to get to the right answer. I keep thinking about what would have happened if there had been an AI system flagging her chart and pushing the team toward the more statistically likely diagnosis. I genuinely don't know that she'd be here.⏎⏎I want to focus on what I understand to be one of the core questions in this RFI, which is about what actions HHS can take to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care. My answer is: please slow down instead.⏎⏎The safety argument against moving fast here is real and I don't think it gets taken seriously enough. These systems are trained on historical data, and healthcare data is full of the biases and gaps that have always plagued medicine. There's already documented evidence that some AI tools perform worse on Black patients, on women presenting with cardiac symptoms, on patients whose records are fragmented because they moved around or lacked insurance. When those disparities show up in a human clinician's decisions, we can at least ask the clinician to explain themselves, we can report them, we can sue them. When it's baked into a vendor's model, who's accountable? The hospital says they followed the vendor's guidance. The vendor says the hospital deployed it wrong. The patient just got a bad outcome and nobody can really say why.⏎⏎There's also something I'm not sure how to articulate exactly but it's about what medicine is supposed to be. When I was sitting in that waiting room in Hartford I wasn't waiting for an algorithm to process my mother. I was trusting that there were human beings in that building who had taken an oath and who were going to use everything they knew to help her. That relationship, where another person is genuinely responsible for your care, is not a nice-to-have. It's the whole thing. And I worry that if we keep layering AI tools into the process, we slowly shift clinicians into a role where they're mainly checking whether they agree with the machine, and over time the machine's judgment starts to feel more authoritative than their own, and we end up with people who are trained to defer rather than to think.⏎⏎I also want to flag the insurance side of this because it keeps getting folded into the broader AI-in-healthcare conversation but it deserves its own scrutiny. Automating prior authorization denials with AI is not a patient safety improvement. It is a cost-cutting tool dressed up in the language of efficiency. UnitedHealth's use of an algorithm called nH Predict to deny post-acute care claims, which generated a class action lawsuit in 2023, is exactly the kind of thing that HHS should be looking at very carefully before deciding that more AI in administrative functions is a good idea. The people harmed by that were elderly patients who were told their inpatient rehabilitation was no longer medically necessary, based on a model that apparently had a 90% error rate when those denials were appealed.⏎⏎That number needs to sit with people at HHS who are thinking about acceleration. Ninety percent.⏎⏎The employment piece is also something I think about. Medical coders, prior auth nurses, care coordinators, the people who do the actual work of navigating patients through a complicated system - those jobs may look administrative but they require deep knowledge of how specific patients, specific insurers, and specific facilities actually behave. An AI system doesn't have a relationship with the case manager at Aetna who knows that a particular appeal needs to be framed a specific way. That tacit knowledge is going to disappear if we automate those roles, and we won't notice until the system starts failing in ways we can't diagnose.⏎⏎I recognize there are people who genuinely believe AI will reduce burnout and improve access and catch things humans miss. I'm not saying those arguments are worthless. But I don't think HHS has done nearly enough to answer the question of what happens when these tools are wrong, who bears the cost, and whether the populations most likely to be harmed are the same populations that already get the worst care.⏎⏎The RFI asks about barriers to adoption and how to remove them. I'd ask HHS to also think about whether some of those barriers exist for reasons that shouldn't be removed, and whether the pressure to move fast is coming more from vendors who want a market than from patients who want better care.♔♔2026-02-19T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-19T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔individual♔medium♔frustrated♔Oklahoma♔social worker♔49♔My client Marcus, a 34-year-old dad in Tulsa with Type 2 diabetes, waited four months to see an endocrinologist last year while his A1C climbed to dangerous levels — and when I finally got him connected to a telehealth platform that used an AI screening tool, his care team caught a foot ulcer before it became an amputation.
