SYNTH-0035♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Michael Hill♔Consumer Reports♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Riverside University Health System in 2022 after a sepsis diagnosis that got missed for almost 36 hours. I am not blaming any individual nurse or doctor for that. They were overwhelmed, short-staffed, working a system that was already stretched past its limit. But I keep thinking about what would have happened if an AI tool had been in the loop during those 36 hours. Would it have caught the signs earlier? Maybe. Or would a clinician have deferred to a system that said "low risk" and felt even less urgency to push back on that assessment? I genuinely don't know. That uncertainty is exactly why I'm writing this.⏎⏎The question HHS is asking, essentially, is how can we speed this up. And I want to ask why speed is the right goal here at all. Medicine has been moving fast for decades, and a lot of what's gone wrong in American healthcare, the overtreatment, the fragmented records, the prior authorization nightmare, the burnout, came from moving fast and assuming technology would sort out the problems it created. I don't see a compelling reason to believe AI adoption will be different.⏎⏎What concerns me most is the accountability question. When a physician makes a wrong call, there is a person. There is a license. There is a relationship, however imperfect, between that clinician and the outcome. When an AI tool makes a wrong call and a clinician defers to it, who is accountable? The vendor says the doctor should have overridden it. The doctor says the tool said low risk. The hospital says they deployed it according to the vendor's instructions. I've seen this pattern play out already in the prior authorization context, where insurers use algorithmic tools to deny claims and then when you appeal, nobody can tell you exactly why the denial happened or who made the decision. That is already a serious problem and it exists in a context where the stakes are high but usually not immediately life-or-death. Moving that same dynamic into clinical decision support is a genuinely frightening prospect.⏎⏎There's also the staffing argument that I think deserves more scrutiny than this RFI seems to give it. The pitch is always that AI reduces clinician burnout by handling documentation, routine triage, that kind of thing. But hospitals are businesses, and if AI can handle documentation faster, the likely outcome is not that nurses and doctors get more time with patients. The likely outcome is that the hospital decides it needs fewer nurses and doctors. I don't think that's paranoia. It's just how cost pressures work in a system where labor is the biggest expense. And the people who will feel that most acutely are not the senior physicians at academic medical centers who are participating in these pilots. It's the home health aides, the medical coders, the patient navigators, the people doing jobs that look administrative but actually require understanding a patient's specific situation in ways that are hard to quantify.⏎⏎I want to be clear that I'm not saying no AI tool should ever be used anywhere in healthcare. That would be an unreasonable position. But the framing of this RFI, which is about acceleration and adoption, skips over some questions that I think should come first. Like: what is the process for removing a tool from use when post-deployment evidence shows it's causing harm in a subpopulation? How long does that take? Who triggers it? The FDA's current framework for software as a medical device has serious gaps around continuous learning systems, and I have not seen a satisfying answer to what happens when a model updates itself and the validation data from six months ago no longer reflects how the system actually behaves.⏎⏎The honest answer to this RFI, from my perspective, is that HHS should slow down and do the harder regulatory work before it starts clearing paths to faster adoption. Not because technology is bad, but because the infrastructure for accountability, the ability to audit decisions, to trace harms, to hold someone responsible, just isn't there yet and building it takes time that rushing deployment does not allow.♔♔2026-01-02T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-02T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Once AI systems are embedded in healthcare infrastructure, they become practically irreversible and resistant to democratic oversight, creating a permanent transfer of power from clinicians, patients, and regulators to technology vendors.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔urgent♔Minnesota♔director of government affairs♔42♔A 2024 RBMA membership survey of 412 radiology practice administrators found that 67% had deployed at least one FDA-cleared AI algorithm within their imaging workflows, yet 58% of those respondents identified inconsistent Medicare reimbursement pathways as the primary barrier to expanding AI utilization across additional modalities. SYNTH-0036♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔George Davis♔Included Health♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Riverside University Health System in Moreno Valley in 2022, and I watched what it actually looks like when a team of nurses and physicians tries to manage a critically ill patient with incomplete information, communication breakdowns, and exhausted staff. It was terrifying and imperfect and also deeply human in ways I'm still thinking about. The attending physician who finally figured out what was wrong with her did it partly by noticing that she was holding her left arm differently than the day before, something no chart entry captured. I don't know how you train a model on that.⏎⏎The RFI asks what HHS can do to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care. My response to that question is: please slow down, and think harder about whether acceleration is actually the right goal here.⏎⏎What worries me isn't really the technology itself in some abstract sense. It's the combination of financial pressure, vendor salesmanship, and institutional impatience that tends to determine how these tools actually get deployed. A hospital system that is losing money on inpatient margins isn't going to buy an AI tool because it wants better outcomes. It's going to buy it because someone promised it could reduce nursing ratios or speed up prior authorization or flag patients for earlier discharge. The efficiency framing is almost always there from the start, and the safety validation is usually thinner than the marketing.⏎⏎There's a specific thing I'd ask HHS to focus on, which is the question of who bears liability when an AI-assisted recommendation turns out to be wrong. Right now the answer is essentially nobody, or more precisely, it gets diffused across the clinician who "should have caught it," the hospital that "properly implemented" the tool, and the vendor whose contract almost certainly includes language limiting their exposure. A nurse in Moreno Valley or anywhere else cannot practically push back against a system that her hospital has standardized around, especially if it's embedded in the EHR workflow. She clicks through the alert or the recommendation because the alternative is a documentation exception and a conversation with her supervisor. That's not informed human oversight. That's compliance theater dressed up as clinical judgment.⏎⏎The bias problem also doesn't get enough serious attention in conversations like this one. Training data in healthcare reflects decades of unequal access, differential treatment, and underdocumented populations. A sepsis prediction model trained primarily on data from large academic medical centers will not perform the same way at a safety-net hospital serving a predominantly undocumented or uninsured population, and the gap may not show up in aggregate accuracy statistics. It'll show up in the patients who get flagged late, or not at all. I haven't seen a realistic proposal for how HHS would require ongoing, site-specific performance monitoring in a way that's actually enforceable rather than just a box on a certification checklist.⏎⏎The administrative side of this worries me too, and I think people underestimate it. Insurance prior authorization is already a system that produces enormous harm through delay and denial, and there are vendors right now marketing AI tools to payers specifically to automate and scale that process. The fact that a denial is generated by an algorithm rather than a human reviewer doesn't make it more legitimate. It makes it harder to appeal, harder to understand, and harder to hold anyone accountable for. If HHS is thinking about where AI could cause serious near-term harm, that's where I'd look first, not at some hypothetical future clinical decision support tool.⏎⏎I keep coming back to the liability question because I think it's the one that determines everything else. Without a clear answer to "who is responsible when this goes wrong and a patient is harmed," every other safeguard is mostly aspirational. SYNTH-0037♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Michael Rivera♔American Nurses Association♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February 2023, and I watched a parade of residents stare at tablet screens while she tried to tell them her pain felt different than it had the day before. Nobody wrote that down in any way that mattered. I kept thinking: if you layer an AI recommendation system on top of that dynamic, what you get is not better care, you get faster dismissal. The machine said she was stable, so she was stable, end of conversation.⏎⏎I want to focus on the question of what "acceleration" actually means here, because I think HHS is framing this wrong. The RFI keeps using language about removing barriers and speeding adoption as though the barriers are the problem. Some of those barriers exist because clinicians, nurses, and patients pushed back on tools that did not work for them, or worked in the demo but fell apart in a busy ED on a Tuesday night. Calling that friction a barrier to be overcome gets things exactly backwards.⏎⏎The safety argument against rushing this is not abstract. There is a study out of Stanford from 2021 that found a widely-used sepsis prediction algorithm had a positive predictive value of only about 18 percent in real-world deployment, meaning more than 80 percent of the alerts it fired were false positives. Clinicians reported that they started ignoring the alerts. So the tool did not just fail to help, it actively trained people to tune out warnings. That is a concrete, documented outcome. And that was a single institution that actually studied it. Most hospitals do not have the resources or the incentive to do that kind of post-deployment audit, so they never find out whether their AI tools are working or not.⏎⏎What worries me about this RFI is that it is asking how to speed things up before there is any serious national infrastructure for finding out whether the things that have already been deployed are doing harm. The FDA has cleared hundreds of AI/ML-based medical devices over the past several years, many under the 510(k) pathway which does not require clinical trial evidence. Once those tools are in use, there is no mandatory adverse event reporting system specifically designed to catch AI-related failures. Clinicians can file MedWatch reports but most don't, and even if they did, the current system was not built to identify patterns that look like "the algorithm got it wrong" rather than "the device malfunctioned."⏎⏎I also want to say something about insurance AI, which the RFI treats almost as an afterthought. Prior authorization denials powered by AI are not a future risk, they are happening now. UnitedHealth's use of an algorithm called nH Predict to deny post-acute care claims was the subject of a federal lawsuit in 2023. The algorithm reportedly had a 90 percent overturn rate on appeal, which means it was denying claims that were almost always ultimately approved, just making patients and providers fight for them. That is not efficiency. That is obstruction wearing a technical costume.⏎⏎I understand there are genuine benefits in narrow, well-validated applications. Radiology AI that flags a possible fracture for a human radiologist to review is different from an algorithm that decides whether a 74 year old woman gets to stay in a rehab facility. HHS should be making that distinction much more clearly and building regulatory frameworks that treat those cases differently, not issuing a broad invitation to accelerate adoption across the board.⏎⏎The question of who bears the cost when AI gets it wrong also needs a real answer before any of this goes further. Right now the answer is basically: the patient. Sometimes the provider. Almost never the vendor. That is not a workable accountability structure for a domain where the stakes include whether someone lives or dies or goes bankrupt paying for care that was denied or delayed because a model made a bad prediction.⏎⏎I am not sure what the right reimbursement structure for AI tools should look like, but I am fairly sure that paying for AI-assisted care at the same rate as regular care, without any requirement to demonstrate that the AI component actually improved outcomes, is going to produce a race to the bottom where the incentive is to deploy the cheapest available tool and call it innovation. There is probably a version of a certification or coverage with evidence development pathway that could work here but I'd want to see very specific conditions attached, not just a general commitment to "real world evidence" which has historically meant whatever the vendor decides to collect.♔♔2026-01-08T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-08T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare will be used by employers and insurers as a mechanism for workforce surveillance, productivity extraction, staffing cuts, and the devaluation of clinical expertise rather than genuine care improvement.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔frustrated♔Maryland♔director of government affairs♔59♔A 2024 RBMA member survey of 412 radiology practice administrators found that 68% had deployed at least one FDA-cleared AI algorithm in their clinical workflow, yet 74% of those respondents reported that existing reimbursement structures fail to account for the additional capital investment, validation burden, and ongoing performance monitoring required to sustain these tools at scale. SYNTH-0038♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Susan Rivera♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Scripps Mercy in San Diego in February 2023, and I watched nurses and doctors make judgment calls that no algorithm could have made. She kept insisting she felt "wrong" in a way she couldn't describe, and one of her nurses — a woman who had worked that floor for sixteen years — pushed for an additional scan based on nothing more than that vague complaint and something she noticed about how my mother was holding her left arm. It caught a secondary clot that the monitoring systems had not flagged. I keep thinking about what happens when that nurse's intuition gets replaced by a dashboard telling her the patient's vitals are within normal range.⏎⏎The RFI asks broadly about what actions HHS can take to accelerate AI adoption, and I want to register something that probably won't be popular in this comment period: I think that framing is already the wrong one. The question isn't how to speed this up. The question is whether the Department has seriously grappled with what gets lost.⏎⏎Medicine isn't primarily an information processing problem. A patient who is scared, in pain, or cognitively impaired doesn't produce clean data. They contradict themselves, they minimize symptoms, they're embarrassed, they don't want to worry their family. The clinician's job involves reading all of that and holding it against years of experience with similar patients in similar situations. What AI systems do well — pattern matching across large datasets — is genuinely useful in some narrow contexts, like radiology image review or flagging drug interactions. I'm not saying there's zero role here. But the Department seems to be treating AI as a general-purpose improvement to clinical care, and I think that's a category error.⏎⏎There's also the bias problem, which I don't think gets taken seriously enough in these discussions. Training data in healthcare reflects decades of unequal care. Systems trained on that data will reproduce those inequalities, and the problem is that the outputs look authoritative. A clinician might push back against a biased colleague. It's much harder to push back against a number generated by a model that nobody in the room fully understands. A 2019 study published in Science found that a widely used commercial algorithm for allocating healthcare resources was systematically underestimating the needs of Black patients by a significant margin — this wasn't a fringe system, it was being used on approximately 200 million people per year. That study is now five years old and I have no confidence the industry has actually fixed the underlying problem.⏎⏎What worries me about the economic dimension is that AI in healthcare is being marketed as a way to address clinician burnout and staffing shortages, but there's an obvious financial incentive for hospitals and insurers to use it instead as a justification for thinner staffing. Those aren't the same thing. If an AI system can flag deteriorating patients, that's genuinely helpful — but only if you still have enough nurses on the floor to respond. The technology doesn't create capacity, it just reshuffles where attention goes. I've talked to travel nurses in three different states and the pattern I hear is that documentation burdens have increased, not decreased, as these tools get added.⏎⏎On the insurance side I feel even more strongly. Automated prior authorization denials are already causing documented patient harm. HHS should be restricting the use of AI in coverage decisions, not accelerating it. When an algorithm denies a claim, nobody is accountable. The patient gets a letter. There's a phone number. The physician submits an appeal. The system generates another denial. This is happening right now, not in some hypothetical future.⏎⏎I understand there are genuine benefits in specific, narrow applications and I'm not asking HHS to prohibit all use of these tools. But I would ask the Department to consider whether "accelerating adoption" should be the frame at all, versus something more like "establishing conditions under which specific applications can be proven safe and equitable before deployment." The difference matters. Right now vendors are deploying systems in hospitals before those systems have been validated in anything like real-world conditions, and the regulatory infrastructure to catch problems after the fact is nowhere near adequate.⏎⏎There's no equivalent of the FDA's clinical trial requirement for most AI tools used in clinical workflow. A hospital can start using a risk stratification algorithm tomorrow without ever having to demonstrate it works better than what it replaces or that it doesn't produce disparate outcomes for different patient populations. SYNTH-0039♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Robert Baker♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Regions Hospital in St. Paul in March of 2023 and I sat with her through a lot of that. Not the efficiency numbers. Not the vendor presentations. The question of what happens to the texture of care when clinical judgment gets routed through a recommendation engine.⏎⏎I'll focus my comments on the question about what regulatory or reimbursement actions HHS should take, because that's where I think the real damage can happen fast.⏎⏎The concern I have is that reimbursement incentives are going to do the work that no one wants to say out loud. If CMS starts paying more for visits that use AI-assisted diagnostics, or if insurers get permission to use algorithmic prior authorization systems without meaningful human review, then hospitals and practices don't really have a choice. They adopt the tools or they fall behind financially. That's not acceleration, that's coercion dressed up as modernization. And the institutions most likely to adopt fast are also the ones with the least margin to absorb a bad outcome when the system fails on an unusual patient.⏎⏎There's a real problem with how these tools get validated and then deployed. A model gets tested on data from, say, academic medical centers with relatively complete records and decent socioeconomic diversity in the test set, and then it gets sold to a rural critical access hospital in Nebraska where the patient population, the documentation habits, and the staffing ratios are all completely different. The FDA clearance or whatever approval pathway it went through doesn't really address that gap. And the clinicians on the ground often have no idea what the model was actually trained on or what its failure modes look like. They just see a score or a flag and they're expected to act on it or document why they didn't.⏎⏎That accountability structure is backwards. Right now the burden is on the clinician to override the machine and justify it. It should be on the vendor and the institution to prove the tool is appropriate for the specific population being served before it ever enters the workflow. I don't see how HHS accelerating adoption gets us closer to that.⏎⏎I also want to say something about administrative AI because I think it gets treated as low stakes and it isn't. That's not a hypothetical future harm. Speeding up the adoption of that kind of tool is not a neutral act.⏎⏎The HHS framing in this RFI assumes the question is how to accelerate. I think the prior question is whether acceleration in this domain is even appropriate given how little we understand about cumulative effects on care quality, workforce, and patient trust. But on the regulatory and reimbursement side, the ask is straightforward: do not create financial incentives that push adoption faster than oversight can follow. SYNTH-0040♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Jessica Thomas♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Riverside University Health System in Moreno Valley in 2022, and I watched what good nursing looks like up close. The nurses knew when she was anxious before she said a word. They adjusted things based on how she was sitting, what her husband's face looked like in the corner, whether she'd eaten. None of that is in a dataset anywhere. I keep thinking about that when I read about AI being rolled out to help clinicians make decisions, and I get genuinely worried that HHS is treating this as a question of how fast to go rather than whether to go at all.⏎⏎The RFI asks specifically about what regulatory actions could accelerate AI adoption in clinical care. My honest answer is that HHS should be asking the opposite question first. Before you think about accelerating anything, you need to ask what happens when these systems are wrong and what the patient can actually do about it. Right now the answer seems to be: not much. The vendor blames the hospital's implementation. The hospital blames the vendor's training data. The clinician says they were just using a tool as a reference. The patient or their family is left with a bad outcome and a chain of accountability that nobody can follow. That is not a hypothetical. That is the structure that gets built in when you move fast and figure out liability later.⏎⏎There is also the question of whose data trained these systems. The AI tools being marketed to hospitals right now were almost certainly built on records from large academic medical centers, which means they reflect the demographics, documentation habits, and disease patterns of patients who were already well-served by the system. Applying those models to a safety-net hospital in rural Mississippi or a community clinic serving recent immigrants is not the same thing as applying them where they were validated, and I don't think anyone really knows how much that gap matters in practice. A 2019 study in Science found that a widely used commercial algorithm systematically underestimated health needs in Black patients because it used cost as a proxy for need. That was before generative AI, before large language models, before any of the current wave. The bias problem has not gotten simpler.⏎⏎On the employment question, which the RFI sort of gestures at without really engaging with it: I think the honest version of what many health systems want from AI is fewer medical coders, fewer care coordinators, fewer people doing prior authorizations, and eventually fewer nurses per shift because the monitoring is "automated." That is a cost-cutting strategy dressed up as innovation. I'm not saying every administrator thinks that cynically, but the incentive is there and HHS should not pretend otherwise. When you make it easier for an insurer to deploy AI for claims adjudication, you are making it easier to deny care with less human review, and we already have a crisis of people getting necessary procedures denied by reviewers who spend eight minutes per case.⏎⏎I don't think I'm anti-technology. I have a smartphone. My kids use computers for school. But there is something different about a system that is making inferences about whether you are sick enough to be admitted, or whether your pain is real, or whether you need a specialist. Those decisions require someone who can be called to account in a way a model cannot.⏎⏎One more thing that I don't think gets enough attention: once these tools are embedded in the EHR workflow, clinicians stop questioning them not because they trust them but because they are tired and the path of least resistance is to go with what the system suggests. There's a term for it, automation bias, and there is actually decent research showing it increases error rates in high-stakes decision environments. Embedding AI into clinical workflow doesn't just change what tools people use, it changes how people think, and probably within a few years in ways that would be very hard to reverse even if the outcomes data turned out to be bad.⏎⏎HHS should at minimum require that any AI tool used in a clinical or insurance decision context publish its validation data broken down by race, age, insurance status, and the specific care setting where it was tested, before deployment, not after. And there should be a real mechanism for frontline clinicians to flag when a tool seems to be steering them wrong, not just a comment box that nobody reads.♔♔2026-02-13T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-13T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare diffuses responsibility across clinicians, hospitals, and vendors in ways that leave patients without a clear accountable party when errors cause harm.♔individual♔medium♔supportive♔Minnesota♔home health aide♔61♔My client Dorothea, who is 84 and lives alone out in Isanti County, had a fall last February that her family didn't find out about for nearly two days because there was no system in place to flag that something was wrong. SYNTH-0041♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Sharon Rivera♔American Health Information Management Association♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Piedmont Atlanta Hospital in March 2023 after a stroke, and what I watched during those eleven days has shaped everything I think about this question. The nurses knew her. Not her chart — her. They noticed when she was frightened versus when she was in pain, even when she couldn't speak clearly enough to explain the difference. That distinction mattered for how they treated her. No algorithm was going to pick that up, and I don't think HHS should be working to build one that tries.⏎⏎I want to focus on what I think is the most underexamined part of this RFI, which is the question of what happens to accountability when AI is embedded in clinical decision-making. Not the technical question of whether the models are accurate enough. The accountability question. Because right now, if a physician makes a wrong call, there is a person who made that call. They have a license. They can be questioned, disciplined, sued. That is uncomfortable but it is also the structure that makes patients able to trust the system at all. What happens when a physician says, and I have already heard this from doctors I know, "the AI flagged it as low risk so I didn't pursue it further"? Who is responsible then? The hospital that purchased the tool? The vendor? The clinician who deferred? This isn't a hypothetical. A 2023 study published in JAMA Internal Medicine looked at sepsis prediction algorithms deployed across multiple hospital systems and found significant rates of both false positives and false negatives, and in interviews, nurses reported feeling uncertain whether to override the system's recommendations. That uncertainty is not a bug that better training will fix. It is a structural problem with putting a machine in the middle of a judgment that a human being needs to own.⏎⏎There is also something I keep coming back to about the economics of all this that the RFI framing mostly skips over. The pitch for AI in healthcare is that it will reduce clinician burnout and improve efficiency. Maybe. But efficiency for whom? Insurers have already started using AI-assisted prior authorization systems, and the practical effect has not been to make authorization faster or fairer for patients. It has been to process denials at a scale and speed that no human review board could match. UnitedHealth's use of an AI model to adjudicate post-acute care claims became the subject of a Senate investigation in 2023 precisely because the denial rates were dramatically higher than what individual medical reviewers had been producing. That is not a coincidence. That is the system working exactly as the people who bought it intended. HHS should be extremely cautious about any acceleration that makes that kind of automated denial more normalized or harder to challenge.⏎⏎I am genuinely not sure what the right answer is for something like AI-assisted radiology reads in a rural hospital with one radiologist covering four counties. I can see the argument there. But that specific use case is quite different from deploying ambient AI documentation tools in every exam room, or letting AI systems flag patients as high or low utilization risk, or using predictive models to guide staffing decisions. The RFI treats all of these as part of one big "AI adoption" question, but they are not the same thing and they should not be evaluated by the same framework.⏎⏎What I would ask HHS to do, at minimum, is require that any AI system used in a clinical or coverage decision context be subject to mandatory adverse event reporting the same way a medical device is. Right now there is no systematic way to know when an AI tool contributed to a bad outcome. Hospitals are not reporting it. Vendors certainly aren't volunteering it. And without that data, any claim that these tools are safe and effective in real-world deployment is just not verifiable. The FDA's Digital Health Center of Excellence has been working on this for years and still hasn't produced a workable post-market surveillance framework for AI-based clinical decision support. Accelerating adoption before that infrastructure exists seems like exactly the wrong sequence.♔♔2026-02-11T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-11T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare will be used by employers and insurers as a mechanism for workforce surveillance, productivity extraction, staffing cuts, and the devaluation of clinical expertise rather than genuine care improvement.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔concerned♔Utah♔campaign director♔34♔A 2024 Health AI Institute survey of 847 clinical deployment sites found that 68% identified fragmented reimbursement pathways as the primary barrier to scaling validated AI tools beyond pilot phases, with the average institution reporting 14 months of administrative delay between FDA clearance and first billable clinical use. He wasn't consulting a dashboard. He was listening to a frightened 71-year-old woman who couldn't always find the right words. I don't think an AI system, no matter how well it performs on benchmark datasets, would have known what to do in that room.⏎⏎That's where I keep getting stuck when I read about accelerating AI adoption in clinical care. The framing assumes the problem is speed and efficiency, that medicine is slowed down by human bottlenecks that algorithms can clear. But a lot of what makes care work isn't a bottleneck. It's the thing itself.⏎⏎I want to respond specifically to the question HHS is asking about what actions the Department can take to support AI adoption in clinical settings, because I think the question is being asked backwards. Before asking how to accelerate adoption, someone should be asking what happens when it goes wrong and who is accountable. Right now there is no good answer to that. A hospital deploys an AI tool from a vendor, the vendor trained it on data from a different population, a clinician follows the recommendation because it appears in their workflow and they're already seeing 22 patients that day, and the outcome is bad. Who is responsible? The clinician who deferred? The hospital that deployed without adequate validation? The vendor who licensed the product? In practice, all three point at each other and the patient or their family is left with nothing but a malpractice case that will take years to resolve.⏎⏎There's also the bias problem, and I don't think HHS is taking it seriously enough in how this RFI is framed. AI systems trained on historical clinical data will reproduce historical clinical inequities. A model trained mostly on data from well-resourced academic medical centers is going to perform differently for patients at a rural safety-net hospital in Mississippi or a tribal health clinic in South Dakota. "Performs well on average" is not a safety standard that protects anyone specific.⏎⏎What I'd actually want HHS to consider is a moratorium on using AI in prior authorization and coverage denial decisions until there is real independent auditing of those systems. That's not AI yet, but it's the same logic, treating human judgment as an inefficiency to be optimized away. Adding AI to that process doesn't fix the problem, it entrenches it and makes it harder to challenge because now there's a model involved and the model is proprietary.⏎⏎I'm not someone who thinks technology is bad. I use electronic health records every day in my work as a medical interpreter and I've seen them help. The framing of "acceleration" worries me more than almost anything else in this request. The question is not whether AI can do impressive things in controlled settings. The question is what gets lost in the spaces between the data points, and whether we're moving fast enough to even notice what we're losing before it's gone.⏎⏎I want to address specifically the question of what regulatory or reimbursement actions HHS might take to speed adoption. My honest view is that the current reimbursement incentives are already distorted in ways that favor technology purchases over staffing, and adding AI reimbursement pathways on top of that will make things worse, not better. That is not a cynical reading, it's just what the incentive structure produces. We already saw this with the rollout of Prior Authorizations automation, where insurers deployed algorithmic denial systems, and reporting from ProPublica and others showed denial rates increasing sharply at certain payers almost immediately after those systems were deployed. The AI didn't improve care. It improved throughput on the denial side of the ledger.⏎⏎I'm also genuinely uncertain about who bears liability when an AI recommendation contributes to a bad outcome. If residents and nurses and physicians assistants start their careers in environments where AI is generating the first-pass assessment, the differential, the documentation summary, do they develop the same diagnostic instincts? Most of these tools have been validated on narrow retrospective datasets and then deployed into much messier real-world conditions. SYNTH-0044♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Charles Torres♔American Hospital Association♔♔♔♔♔My mother was hospitalized at Mercy General in Sacramento for six weeks in 2019 after a series of strokes, and what kept her alive and what kept our family sane was not any algorithm. I keep coming back to that when I read materials like this one, which seems to assume that the main barrier to better healthcare is insufficient automation.⏎⏎The RFI asks what regulatory actions HHS can take to accelerate AI adoption, and I want to respond to that framing directly because I think the framing itself is the problem. Accelerating adoption treats speed as the goal. But medicine has historically been very cautious about adopting new interventions, and for good reason. We run clinical trials. We require evidence. We have post-market surveillance. We do not simply deploy something because it performs well in a demonstration or because a hospital CFO sees a cost reduction opportunity. The fact that AI tools are being discussed under an "acceleration" framework rather than an evidence and safety framework tells me that this process is being driven more by industry enthusiasm than by patient welfare.⏎⏎There are real documented cases of AI tools causing harm. The sepsis prediction algorithm deployed at several major health systems, including one widely reported situation involving the Epic Sepsis Model around 2021, was shown in a study published in JAMA Internal Medicine to have substantially lower sensitivity than what had been claimed, and clinicians reported that it generated so many alerts that staff began ignoring them. That is not a success story that needs to be accelerated. That is a cautionary tale about what happens when you move fast in clinical settings.⏎⏎What concerns me about the insurance and administrative side is actually just as serious, maybe more so because it gets less attention. When insurers use AI to make prior authorization decisions, the practical effect is that a system trained on historical claims data is making coverage judgments that affect real people's access to care. And when something goes wrong, who is responsible? The insurer says the algorithm flagged it. The vendor says the client configured it wrong. The patient has no idea what happened or how to appeal it. I have seen this play out with a family member's coverage denial in 2022 and the appeals process was genuinely baffling. Nobody could explain what triggered the denial. That is not a hypothetical concern.⏎⏎I am not saying technology has no place in medicine. I use a continuous glucose monitor and I think it has genuinely helped me. But there is a difference between a device that gives me information about my own body and a system that is making or shaping clinical decisions for a population, at scale, in ways that individual patients and clinicians cannot see or contest.⏎⏎The workforce question is also being treated too lightly here. The argument that AI will reduce clinician burnout sounds good but it also could mean fewer nurses, fewer medical coders, fewer people in roles that currently require human judgment and provide middle-class employment in communities across the country. Those jobs are not just economic abstractions. They are also part of the care infrastructure. When you replace a medical coder with an algorithm you lose someone who knew how to call a physician's office and get clarification on a confusing note. That local knowledge disappears.⏎⏎HHS should at minimum require prospective clinical validation in the specific care settings where a tool will be deployed before any federal reimbursement or regulatory pathway facilitates its use. Validation done in academic medical centers should not be assumed to transfer to rural critical access hospitals or federally qualified health centers. And any AI tool used in prior authorization decisions should be required to produce an explanation that a layperson can actually understand, with a clear human point of contact for appeals, not just a general customer service number that routes to another algorithm.⏎⏎I would also ask HHS to consider funding independent audits of tools that are already deployed rather than spending resources on accelerating the deployment of new ones. We do not actually have a good picture of what is already in use, how it is performing, or where it is failing quietly.♔♔2026-02-08T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-08T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔hopeful♔Nebraska♔executive director♔58♔A 2024 Texas Medical Association member survey of 4,200 practicing physicians found that 68% reported using or actively evaluating AI-assisted clinical tools in their practices, yet 74% of that group identified inconsistent federal reimbursement pathways as the primary barrier to broader deployment. SYNTH-0045♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Carol Sanchez♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February of 2023, and what I keep thinking about when I read discussions like this one is how many times the nurses caught things that were not in any chart. A medication interaction that the system flagged as low priority. The way she was holding her left arm. The fact that she kept asking for her sister, who had died in 1987, which turned out to be a sign of something the attending needed to know about immediately. None of that was in the data. None of it would have been findable by a pattern-matching tool trained on discharge summaries and lab values.⏎⏎I want to address the question this RFI asks about what barriers HHS should try to remove to accelerate AI adoption, because my honest answer is that the question itself assumes the conclusion. The premise seems to be that adoption is good and slowness is a problem to be solved. But there is another way to read the current pace of adoption, which is that clinicians and patients are appropriately cautious, and that this caution is itself a form of safety signal that regulators should pay attention to rather than try to engineer away.⏎⏎The safety concerns here are not hypothetical. There's been documented work showing that some commonly used clinical algorithms, including the sepsis prediction tools that became widespread around 2020 and 2021, generated so many false positives in real hospital settings that nursing staff started ignoring the alerts entirely. That is a known failure mode. The tool gets embedded, the alert fatigue builds up, and eventually the system that was supposed to save lives becomes background noise. And that's before you even get into the question of whether the underlying training data encoded racial or socioeconomic disparities that the developers either didn't notice or disclosed in ways that didn't reach the bedside.⏎⏎What I'm more worried about than any single bad tool is the structural shift that happens when AI recommendations become the default. Once a system is in place, the human clinician who disagrees with it has to actively justify that disagreement. The burden of proof flips. And in a busy emergency department or an understaffed rural clinic, how often does someone have the time or the standing to push back? This is especially true for newer clinicians, who may not have the experience to know when to trust their gut over a confidence score. The organizational pressure to defer to the tool will be real and it will be quiet and nobody will be able to point to the moment it started.⏎⏎I also think the employment question is being handled very carefully by everyone who stands to profit from AI adoption and very carelessly by everyone else. The argument that AI will free clinicians from documentation burdens so they can spend more time with patients sounds good. But the same tools that reduce documentation time also make it easier to quantify exactly how many patients a provider sees per hour, which makes it easier to argue that you need fewer providers. That argument has already been made explicitly in some insurance contexts, where automated prior authorization systems have been used to process denials at a scale that would be impossible with human reviewers. UnitedHealth's subsidiary, Navihealth, faced litigation in 2023 specifically over allegations that an AI tool was being used to override physician judgments about post-acute care in ways that cut off coverage prematurely. That is not a distant hypothetical.⏎⏎HHS should be asking harder questions about accountability before it asks about acceleration. When an AI-assisted diagnosis is wrong, who is the patient supposed to sue? The clinician who followed the recommendation? The hospital that deployed the tool? The vendor who sold it and whose model weights are proprietary? The answer right now is genuinely unclear, and that ambiguity is not a technical problem waiting to be solved by better documentation standards. It reflects something real about what happens to responsibility when you distribute decision-making across a system that no single person fully understands or controls.⏎⏎I don't have a clean regulatory proposal here. I'm not a lawyer or a policy expert. But I do think HHS should be very careful about treating "removing barriers to adoption" as self-evidently good, because some of those barriers are there for reasons that took a long time to learn and could take even longer to relearn if we get this wrong.♔♔2026-01-30T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-30T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare diffuses responsibility across clinicians, hospitals, and vendors in ways that leave patients without a clear accountable party when errors cause harm.♔individual♔medium♔frustrated♔California♔caregiver♔35♔My mom had a minor stroke in February, and the discharge team handed us a stack of papers and basically sent us home to figure it out — no follow-up coordination, no clear instructions on her medication schedule, nothing. SYNTH-0046♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Nancy Nguyen♔Humana♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Piedmont Atlanta Hospital in February 2023, and I watched her care team make decisions that no algorithm could have made. Her blood pressure numbers looked stable on paper but her nurse noticed she was gripping the bed rail differently than the day before, and that observation triggered a cascade of interventions that probably saved her life. I keep thinking about that when I read about what HHS is proposing here.⏎⏎The question I want to address is the one about barriers to adoption and what the department can do to remove them. My concern is that you're asking the wrong question. The barriers to AI adoption in clinical settings may not be barriers so much as appropriate friction, the kind of resistance that exists in a field where errors kill people and where judgment built over years of training is genuinely hard to replicate or replace. When a nurse notices something a monitor doesn't catch, that is not a workflow inefficiency to be engineered away.⏎⏎There is a real difference between using software to schedule appointments or process billing codes and using it to influence what a clinician does at the bedside, or worse, what an insurer decides to cover. I worry that HHS is treating these as the same category of thing. The administrative AI use cases get bundled together with clinical decision support and even diagnostic tools, and the whole package gets framed as acceleration. But the risks are completely different. An AI that makes a scheduling error is annoying. An AI that flags a prior authorization denial, or recommends against a particular treatment based on statistical patterns drawn from historically biased datasets, can hurt or kill the wrong patient.⏎⏎On the bias issue specifically: it's not hypothetical. There is published research going back to at least 2019 showing that widely used risk-scoring algorithms in American hospitals systematically underestimated the health needs of Black patients because they used healthcare costs as a proxy for health needs, and Black patients had historically been denied access to care and therefore had lower costs not because they were healthier but because they had been underserved. If a system like that had been treated as authoritative, and in some places it was, the errors would have compounded over time while appearing statistically reasonable in aggregate.⏎⏎What I would ask HHS to consider is that removing barriers to AI adoption and building adequate safeguards are not the same project and may actually be in tension with each other. The institutions most eager to adopt AI quickly are often the ones with the most financial incentive to use it for cost reduction rather than care improvement. Staffing ratios get cut. Nurses get told the AI will catch what they might miss. The liability question is a mess because when something goes wrong nobody can quite explain whose fault it is, the vendor blames the implementation, the hospital blames the clinician, the clinician says they were just following the recommendation. That diffusion of accountability is genuinely scary to me, not in an abstract way but in the very concrete way of thinking about someone lying in a hospital bed and trying to figure out who is actually responsible for their care.⏎⏎I also want to say something about the employment angle even though it's maybe not the primary focus of this RFI. Healthcare workers, including nurses, medical coders, radiology technicians, and a lot of other people with specialized training, are looking at this moment with real anxiety. The argument that AI will free them from tedious tasks so they can focus on human connection has been made about every wave of workplace automation and it has a pretty poor track record. What usually happens is the tedious tasks go away along with some of the jobs, and the remaining workers are expected to supervise the machines on top of everything else they already do.⏎⏎The comment period closing date for this RFI doesn't give nearly enough time for the kind of input that would actually be useful, and I'm not sure a comment like mine, from someone who's not a vendor or a health system, will get the same weight as the submissions from companies that have a financial stake in the outcome.♔♔2026-02-11T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-11T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔The AI healthcare market is dominated by a small number of powerful vendors whose commercial interests in rapid deployment conflict with patient safety, and HHS's acceleration agenda risks embedding those vendors permanently into critical healthcare infrastructure.♔organization♔medium♔frustrated♔Colorado♔VP of regulatory affairs♔58♔A 2024 internal assessment across UConn Health's clinical departments identified 14 active or pilot-stage AI-assisted tools in use, ranging from ambient documentation systems in primary care to predictive analytics platforms in the emergency department. SYNTH-0048♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Matthew Sanchez♔Autism Society of America♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in Dayton in February 2023, and I watched what it actually looks like when a human clinician figures out what's wrong with someone. That framing worries me because it treats adoption speed as the goal rather than patient outcomes. Those are not the same thing.⏎⏎On the question of what regulatory actions HHS might take to support AI adoption in clinical care, my honest answer is that the department should slow down considerably and spend much more time on the liability and accountability questions before doing anything to encourage broader deployment. The clinician can point to the tool. The hospital can say the vendor was responsible for validation. The vendor can say the user deployed it outside the intended clinical context. That chain of diffused responsibility is already showing up in malpractice cases and it will get worse. I don't think HHS can address that through guidance alone. It probably requires legislation, but at minimum the department should not be actively encouraging deployment while that accountability gap remains open.⏎⏎The bias problem is also more serious than the promotional materials from AI vendors tend to suggest. Training datasets in clinical AI frequently reflect decades of unequal care. A 2019 study published in Science found that a widely used algorithm for managing care coordination systematically underestimated the health needs of Black patients because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for health needs, and Black patients had historically received less care and therefore generated lower costs. The algorithm wasn't doing anything unusual. It was doing exactly what it was trained to do. The problem was baked into the training data in a way that was genuinely hard to see until someone looked carefully. Deploying more AI faster doesn't solve that problem. It scales it.⏎⏎I also want to say something about the employment question, which I notice tends to get minimized in these conversations. The argument is usually that AI will free up clinicians to do more meaningful work by handling administrative burden. Maybe. But hospitals are not going to absorb the efficiency gains and leave staffing levels unchanged. They will cut positions. Medical coders, prior authorization reviewers, radiology technicians, people who do intake and scheduling, those are real jobs held by real people, many of them women, many of them people of color, many of them without the credentials or resources to easily retrain for different work. The fact that some of those jobs are administrative does not make them unimportant. They are also often the jobs where the human context gets preserved. The person doing intake asks a question the form didn't include. The coder notices something inconsistent in the record. Those small human moments of attention matter in ways that are genuinely hard to quantify.⏎⏎I'm not saying AI has no legitimate uses in medicine at all. I can see the argument for narrow, well-validated tools in specific contexts, like flagging certain imaging findings for radiologist review where the evidence base is reasonably strong. But that is very different from the broad integration push this RFI seems to be contemplating. There is a real difference between a tool that assists a trained professional who retains full decision authority and a system that is gradually becoming the default source of clinical judgment in institutions that are also cutting staff and increasing patient loads.⏎⏎The pressure to adopt AI in healthcare is coming substantially from vendors who have enormous financial interest in deployment, and from administrators who see cost reduction opportunities, and neither of those groups is the same as the patients and frontline workers who will actually live with the consequences. HHS should be asking harder questions about who benefits from rapid adoption and who bears the risks, and those groups are not evenly distributed.⏎⏎One more thing I want to raise, though I'm not sure exactly where it fits in the RFI's question structure: the informed consent issue. Patients in most settings currently have no reliable way to know whether an AI system was involved in their diagnosis, their treatment recommendation, their insurance authorization, or their care plan. That seems like a basic transparency problem that should be addressed before any further expansion of use, not after. I've asked physicians at two different health systems in the past year whether any of the tools they use to support their decisions were AI-based and both times I got answers that were genuinely uncertain.♔♔2026-01-08T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-08T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI systems in healthcare inherit and amplify racial, socioeconomic, and institutional biases from training data, and their apparent authority makes those biases harder to detect and correct than human error.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔frustrated♔Oklahoma♔campaign director♔52♔A 2024 TMA member survey of 4,200 practicing Texas physicians found that 68% reported using or actively evaluating at least one AI-assisted clinical decision support tool, yet 74% of that group cited unresolved Medicare and Medicaid reimbursement pathways as the primary barrier to full integration at the point of care. SYNTH-0049♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Andrew Clark♔American Thoracic Society♔♔♔♔♔My mother was in the hospital for six weeks in 2019 after a series of small strokes, and I spent a lot of that time watching nurses and doctors make judgment calls that no algorithm could have made. That conversation changed her treatment plan. There was nothing in her chart that would have led a machine to that conversation. I keep thinking about that when I read about HHS pushing AI into clinical care.⏎⏎The question the RFI seems most focused on is what HHS can do to accelerate AI adoption, and I want to argue that the department should slow down instead, or at minimum stop treating acceleration as the obvious goal. Faster is not automatically better in medicine. Medicine has specific reasons for being slow and deliberate, because the costs of errors are not just financial. They fall on people who are already sick and scared and who often have no recourse when something goes wrong.⏎⏎What worries me most is the insurance and prior authorization side of this. There are already documented cases of AI-driven denial systems rejecting claims at rates that would be impossible for human reviewers to sustain. A ProPublica investigation in 2023 looked at how one major insurer was using automated review to reject post-acute care claims in bulk, sometimes in under a second per case. That's not a hypothetical risk. That's already happening. And the pressure HHS seems to be under from the tech industry is to make more of this easier, not less. If HHS accelerates AI adoption without putting serious brakes on the insurance side, the most concrete near-term effect may be more automated denials, not better care.⏎⏎There's also something I'm not sure HHS has fully grappled with, which is what happens to the clinicians themselves. Nursing and medical training is built on the idea that the professional is responsible for the patient. When you introduce a system that generates recommendations and the clinician is expected to either follow them or document why they didn't, you've subtly shifted the burden of proof. Now the human has to justify deviating from the machine. That's an inversion of how medical accountability is supposed to work, and it happens gradually, not all at once, so it's easy to miss.⏎⏎I'm also skeptical of the argument that AI reduces clinician burnout. Maybe for some tasks. But the administrative burden that causes the most burnout isn't the kind that AI handles cleanly. It's the documentation, the insurance fights, the phone calls to pharmacies, the prior auth denials. Those processes are messy and require someone who understands context and can push back. An AI system that drafts notes faster doesn't fix the fact that a nurse practitioner is spending two hours a day on the phone with insurance companies trying to get coverage approved for a patient who needs a medication they've been on for ten years.⏎⏎The RFI asks about what research HHS should support. My answer would be: fund research on what happens when these tools fail, not just when they work. Require post-deployment outcome tracking. Make it mandatory, not voluntary. Right now most AI vendors publish validation studies done in controlled settings on historical data, and then the tool gets deployed in a community hospital in rural Nebraska with completely different patient demographics and nobody tracks what happens next. That's the gap that matters.⏎⏎I don't think I'm anti-technology. I use technology every day. But there are domains where the human relationship is not incidental to the service being provided — it is the service. And I think healthcare is one of them. The efficiency gains from AI are real but they're also unevenly distributed, and the risks are not abstract. They fall hardest on elderly patients who can't navigate a portal, on people with mental illness whose presentations don't fit clean diagnostic categories, on patients who already distrust the healthcare system for very good historical reasons.⏎⏎One more thing I'd add, and I'm not sure where it fits in the formal comment structure: there should be some mechanism for patients to know when AI was involved in a decision about their care and to request a human review. Not just a buried disclosure in a consent form. An actual right, with teeth, that a patient or their family can invoke. I have not seen that addressed anywhere in the current RFI and it seems like the most basic protection you could put in place before any of this scales further.♔♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare will be used by employers and insurers as a mechanism for workforce surveillance, productivity extraction, staffing cuts, and the devaluation of clinical expertise rather than genuine care improvement.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔hopeful♔Oklahoma♔community organizer♔45♔A 2024 RBMA membership survey spanning 412 radiology practice administrators found that 67% identified AI-assisted diagnostic tools as their top operational investment priority, yet 58% of those same respondents reported that uncertain reimbursement pathways under existing CPT and HCPCS coding structures were the primary barrier to sustainable deployment. SYNTH-0050♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Andrew Harris♔Alliance for Aging Research♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February 2023 after a sepsis diagnosis that got delayed by almost 36 hours. Part of what happened, according to the attending who finally talked to us honestly, was that an early warning algorithm had flagged her as low-risk based on her vitals at intake, and the nursing staff was stretched thin and leaning on that score. I'm not saying the algorithm killed her — she survived — but I've thought about that a lot since then, and I think about it every time I see another announcement about how AI is going to "transform" healthcare.⏎⏎So when I saw this RFI I wanted to respond specifically to the question about what barriers HHS should remove to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care. My answer is: please slow down and think about whether acceleration is actually the right goal here.⏎⏎The framing of this whole request bothers me. It assumes the problem is that AI isn't being adopted fast enough, and that regulatory barriers are getting in the way. But I don't think the evidence supports that framing. What I see instead is an industry that is moving very fast to deploy tools that have not been adequately tested in real-world conditions, in hospitals that are understaffed and under financial pressure, with patients who have no idea what systems are being used to make decisions about their care.⏎⏎The specific thing I want HHS to think about is what happens when these tools fail. Not in the aggregate, not on the benchmark dataset, but for one particular patient on one particular night. When an AI system gives a wrong recommendation, who is responsible? The clinician who followed it? The hospital that deployed it? The vendor who sold it? In my mother's case we never got a straight answer about whose call it was to rely on that score. That's not a technology problem, that's an accountability problem, and adding more AI to more decisions makes it worse not better.⏎⏎I've read some of the arguments about how AI can help with clinician burnout and I understand the appeal. Doctors and nurses are exhausted. I've seen it. But I keep thinking that "reducing burden" can very easily become a justification for thinner staffing and more automation of decisions that genuinely require a human being to stop and think. The economics of this are not neutral. Hospitals and insurers have strong financial incentives to use AI to cut costs, and "AI-assisted care" can be a polite way of saying "fewer nurses per patient."⏎⏎There's also something that doesn't get talked about enough which is the effect on patients who are already disadvantaged. My mother is white, english-speaking, has a daughter who asks a lot of questions, and she still nearly fell through the cracks. What happens to elderly patients with dementia, or people who don't speak English, or people in rural hospitals that bought a cheap system because it was all they could afford, when an algorithm gets their risk score wrong? The training data for most of these systems reflects decades of unequal care delivery. You can't just train a model on biased historical data and expect it to produce equitable outcomes.⏎⏎I'm not asking HHS to ban AI research or say that no tool can ever be useful. I'm asking the agency not to treat the removal of regulatory safeguards as an obvious good. Some of those barriers exist for reasons. The 510(k) process for medical devices, whatever its flaws, at least creates some expectation that a product has been evaluated before it gets used on patients. A lot of AI tools in clinical use right now are not going through that level of scrutiny, and HHS seems to be contemplating making that easier rather than harder.⏎⏎What I'd actually want to see is mandatory disclosure to patients when AI is being used in their care, real pre-deployment validation requirements that include performance on underrepresented subgroups, and clear liability rules so that "the algorithm recommended it" is not a defense. SYNTH-0051♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Linda Sanchez♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in 2019 after a misdiagnosed pulmonary embolism, and what saved her was not a decision support tool. It was a night-shift nurse who noticed she was holding her left arm differently and flagged it to the attending. That kind of observation doesn't get captured in a training dataset.⏎⏎I want to focus my comments on the question of what HHS describes as "accelerating adoption" and what that framing gets wrong from the start. The word accelerating is doing a lot of work here. It presupposes that speed is good, that the main obstacle to AI in clinical settings is regulatory friction rather than unresolved questions about whether these tools actually help patients. That framing worries me more than any specific provision.⏎⏎Here is what I keep coming back to: medicine is not primarily an information-matching problem. It never has been. A patient who comes in describing chest tightness might be having a cardiac event, or might be terrified about a custody hearing happening tomorrow, or might be describing something she has already been told twice is anxiety and is now scared to bring up again. The clinician who picks up on that context is doing something that I do not believe current AI systems can replicate, and more importantly, something that gets crowded out when clinicians are busy managing AI-generated alerts, documentation tools, and recommendation queues. There is real evidence that alert fatigue from existing clinical decision support systems has led to missed warnings. Adding more AI-generated signals does not obviously solve that problem.⏎⏎The bias question also deserves more direct attention than this RFI gives it. Algorithms trained on historical clinical data will inherit whatever treatment disparities existed in that data. A 2019 study published in Science found that a widely used commercial health algorithm systematically underestimated the health needs of Black patients because it used cost as a proxy for need, and cost reflected unequal access rather than actual illness burden. That algorithm was already deployed at scale before the problem was identified. HHS is now asking how to accelerate deployment of similar tools. I would ask instead: what is the post-market surveillance infrastructure that would catch something like that in the future, and does it currently exist? I don't think it does, not in any robust form.⏎⏎The employment question is one I don't see addressed clearly in the RFI at all. Administrative staff in hospitals and clinics, medical coders, prior authorization reviewers, scheduling coordinators, these are not highly paid positions but they are real jobs held by real people, many of them women, many of them in communities that don't have a lot of other options. When insurers and hospital systems talk about using AI to "streamline" administrative functions, they mean replacing those workers. I am not opposed to efficiency in principle but I think the people whose jobs disappear deserve more than a sentence about workforce transition in a federal RFI.⏎⏎What I would ask HHS to do, specifically and concretely, is to impose a mandatory pre-deployment validation requirement for any AI tool used in clinical decision-making, including prior authorization, with public disclosure of performance data broken down by race, age, disability status, and insurance type. Not voluntary disclosure. Required. And I would ask for a moratorium on AI-assisted prior authorization denials until that infrastructure exists, because right now patients are being denied care by systems that nobody can fully audit and nobody is clearly responsible for when they get it wrong.⏎⏎I realize some of this sounds like I'm opposed to technology generally. I'm not. I use the patient portal, I think telehealth expanded access in important ways. But there is a difference between tools that give patients and clinicians more capability and tools that shift decision-making into systems that are opaque, hard to challenge, and ultimately owned by vendors whose interests are not the same as patients' interests. The RFI keeps describing AI as something that can be "responsibly adopted" but the burden of demonstrating that responsibility should be on the deploying institution and the vendor before deployment, not reconstructed after harm occurs.⏎⏎One more thing that I couldn't find addressed anywhere in the RFI: who is liable when an AI tool contributes to a diagnostic error? The clinician who followed its recommendation? The hospital that licensed it? The vendor who sold it? Because right now the contracts I've seen, and I've seen a few through my work in patient advocacy, typically include vendor indemnification clauses that push all liability back onto the provider. That needs to be addressed before any acceleration happens.♔♔2026-02-21T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-21T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare diffuses responsibility across clinicians, hospitals, and vendors in ways that leave patients without a clear accountable party when errors cause harm.♔individual♔high♔angry♔North Carolina♔elementary school teacher♔50♔When my student Marcus came back to school in February with a new insulin pump after a terrifying hospitalization, his mother told me the endocrinologist three counties over had used some kind of AI diagnostic tool to finally catch what two local doctors had missed for months — and I thought, fine, good, that helped one child. SYNTH-0053♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Paul Brown♔Evolent Health♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February of 2023 after a sepsis complication following routine gallbladder surgery. If an AI system had flagged or failed to flag something in that situation, who answers for it? The hospital points to the vendor. The vendor says the clinician should have overridden it. The clinician says the protocol required them to defer to the system's recommendation. I've read enough about how these liability questions actually play out in practice to know that nobody ends up being responsible. That diffusion of accountability is genuinely dangerous in ways that I don't think HHS is reckoning with seriously enough in this RFI.⏎⏎There's also the question of what happens to the data AI systems are trained on. Healthcare data in the United States reflects decades of unequal access and unequal treatment. Black patients have historically been undertreated for pain. Low-income patients have historically received less diagnostic workup for the same symptoms. Elderly patients get dismissed. If you train a system on that history, you're not getting an objective tool, you're codifying the biases of the past and giving them an authoritative-sounding output. And the problem is that when a human clinician is biased, you can sometimes identify it, confront it, work against it. When a system is biased, it looks like math.⏎⏎I'm also genuinely worried about what AI adoption means for the workforce, and I think this RFI is too optimistic about the "burnout reduction" framing. Radiology image review where a second pass catches something a tired human missed, that's not obviously bad. I have no confidence that accelerating AI adoption will go any differently. The systems that have bias baked in don't advertise it. They look authoritative.
