An AI system trained on historical EHR data is, at best, finding patterns in what previous clinicians decided to write down and code correctly, which is already a filtered and imperfect record of what actually happened in those rooms. The biases baked into those records — racial, socioeconomic, gendered — don't disappear when you run a model on top of them. They get laundered. They get dressed up in the language of statistical confidence.⏎⏎I also want to say something about the insurance and prior authorization context specifically because I notice the RFI touches on administrative functions. There is already documented evidence — from ProPublica's reporting on Cigna's algorithm-based denials and from the Senate Finance Committee's 2023 investigation into Medicare Advantage — that AI tools are being used not to improve care but to generate faster denials with a veneer of clinical legitimacy. If HHS removes barriers to AI adoption broadly without first establishing hard limits on how AI can be used in coverage determinations, you are going to accelerate exactly that. Hospitals will be told their AI-assisted documentation is more efficient. Insurers will quietly expand algorithm-driven denials. Patients will have less recourse than they do now, not more, because the decision will appear to have come from a process rather than a person.⏎⏎The accountability issue is the one that worries me the most, honestly. When something goes wrong with an AI-assisted diagnosis or an AI-generated treatment recommendation, who is responsible? The clinician who followed the suggestion? The hospital that deployed the tool? The vendor who trained the model on data they won't fully disclose? Right now there is no satisfying answer to that question, and I do not think HHS should be in the business of normalizing the technology before that answer exists. Once these systems are embedded in workflows, once hospitals have restructured their staffing around the assumption that the AI is handling certain tasks, reversing course becomes politically and economically difficult even if the evidence of harm is clear.⏎⏎I am not opposed to technology in medicine as a general matter. I understand that there are narrow applications — certain kinds of image analysis, medication interaction flags — where the case is more straightforward. But the RFI as written seems to treat AI adoption as a goal in itself, and I think that's the wrong orientation. The goal is better patient outcomes. Sometimes technology serves that. Sometimes it gets in the way by creating the illusion of thoroughness while actually thinning out the human attention that patients need.⏎⏎There are nursing homes in rural areas right now being pitched AI monitoring systems as a substitute for adequate staffing ratios. That is not a hypothetical. CMS should be aware of at least several active pilots along those lines in facilities that are already understaffed by any reasonable standard, and the pitch to administrators is almost always framed in terms of cost savings rather than care quality.♔♔2026-02-12T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-12T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔organization♔high♔angry♔South Carolina♔VP of regulatory affairs♔49♔Lumenex Advisory's 2024 analysis of 47 health system clients—spanning more than 1,200 care sites across 28 states—found that 68% identified fragmented federal guidance on AI validation and liability as the primary barrier to clinical deployment, outpacing budget constraints and workforce readiness by a margin of nearly two to one. SYNTH-0056♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Sharon Rivera♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Emory University Hospital in Atlanta in March of 2023 after a sepsis diagnosis that her primary care doctor had initially missed. What saved her, eventually, was a hospitalist who sat with our family for forty minutes, listened to everything we described about how she'd been acting at home in the weeks before, and put together a picture that none of the labs or scans had shown clearly on their own. That doctor used judgment. He used context. He used the fact that my mother kept saying "something feels wrong" even when she couldn't describe it. I don't know how you feed that into a model.⏎⏎The question I want to address in this comment is the one HHS is asking about what actions the department can take to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care. My answer is: please slow down, and think harder about what you might be destroying in the process of trying to modernize.⏎⏎Medicine is not primarily an information problem. I know that's probably an unpopular thing to say to a technology-focused office, but I think it's true. Patients are scared. They're in pain. They describe their symptoms badly. They leave things out because they're embarrassed or because they don't think a detail is relevant. A good clinician reads the room, reads the patient, reads the family. That is not something that gets captured in an eHR and it's not something an AI system trained on clinical notes is going to learn to do, at least not in any way I would trust with my mother's life.⏎⏎There's also the question of what happens when these systems are wrong. And they will be wrong sometimes - every tool is. But when a human doctor is wrong, there is a person accountable. You can file a complaint, you can sue, you can at least look someone in the eye. When an AI system gives a bad recommendation and a clinician follows it, who is responsible? The vendor will say the clinician should have overridden it. The hospital will say they deployed it according to specifications. The clinician will say the tool told them something different than their instinct and they were trained to trust the tool. I've seen this kind of diffusion of responsibility in other industries and it's genuinely dangerous, but in healthcare the stakes are not a delayed package or a bad credit score. They are permanent injuries and deaths.⏎⏎What worries me most practically is the insurance and prior authorization side. There are already AI systems being used to deny claims automatically, and there have been lawsuits about this - UnitedHealth Group faced significant litigation in 2023 and 2024 over an algorithm called nH Predict that was alleged to be denying post-acute care claims at rates far higher than human reviewers would have approved. If HHS accelerates AI adoption broadly, I think that's the place where the most harm will happen fastest, because insurers have every financial incentive to use AI to say no more efficiently and very little incentive to use it to say yes more accurately.⏎⏎I also want to say something about the workforce piece because I don't think it gets enough attention in these kinds of discussions. There are people who do medical coding, prior auth review, care coordination, and similar work. Many of them have years of experience and genuinely understand the human context behind the cases they handle. Replacing those roles with AI doesn't just eliminate jobs, it eliminates a layer of human judgment that exists in the system right now. Whether that judgment is always good is a separate question. But removing it entirely and replacing it with an optimization algorithm seems like a decision that deserves a lot more public debate than it's getting in this RFI process.⏎⏎I understand HHS is under pressure from multiple directions to show that it's modernizing. I'm not saying AI has zero place in healthcare administration or research. But there's a difference between using computational tools to support human decision-making and using them to replace it, and I think this RFI is oriented too heavily toward acceleration without enough attention to what the brakes are and who gets to use them.⏎⏎One more thing I keep coming back to: once these systems are embedded in clinical workflows and billing systems and insurance platforms, they will be very hard to remove. Vendors will have contracts. Institutions will have built their processes around the tools. The people who knew how to do things the old way will have retired or moved on. Reversibility should be a first-order concern in any policy HHS develops here, and I don't see it mentioned prominently anywhere in the questions being asked.♔♔2026-02-27T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-27T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Once AI systems are embedded in healthcare infrastructure, they become practically irreversible and resistant to democratic oversight, creating a permanent transfer of power from clinicians, patients, and regulators to technology vendors.♔individual♔high♔resigned♔West Virginia♔stay-at-home parent♔47♔My son Mateo was diagnosed with a rare mitochondrial disorder at age nine, after three years of watching specialists in Charleston and Pittsburgh run tests that kept coming back inconclusive — tests that, as I later learned, an AI-assisted diagnostic tool deployed at a children's hospital in Boston had been flagging correctly in similar cases for two years already. SYNTH-0057♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Michelle Johnson♔National Council of Asian Pacific Islander Physicians♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Piedmont Atlanta Hospital in February 2023, and during that time I watched a rotating cast of hospitalists try to make sense of her records, her medications, her history. It was chaotic and imperfect and deeply human. What I keep thinking about now, reading about this push to put AI into clinical decisions, is whether any of that would have been better if a machine had been involved, or whether it would have just felt more organized while actually being worse.⏎⏎The question HHS seems to be asking is basically: how do we speed this up? And I want to push back on the premise that speed is the right goal here.⏎⏎Medicine is not an information retrieval problem. I know that sounds simple but I think it keeps getting lost in these conversations. When my mother's care team made decisions, they were reading her face, listening to how she described pain, noticing that she got confused and scared in the evenings and needed someone to actually sit with her. None of that is in a dataset. And the worry I have is that AI systems create this impression of rigor, this veneer of objectivity, that makes everyone feel like the hard judgment calls are being handled, when really what's happening is that the messy human stuff is just being ignored because it can't be quantified.⏎⏎There's also the bias issue, which I don't think gets taken seriously enough. These systems are trained on historical data, and historical data in American medicine reflects decades of unequal treatment. Studies going back to at least 2019, including work by Obermeyer et al. published in Science, showed that a widely-used commercial algorithm was systematically underestimating the health needs of Black patients because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for health, and Black patients had been given less care historically so they cost less. That's not a bug that got fixed and went away. That's the kind of thing that can be baked into any system trained on real-world data, and it can be much harder to spot when the output looks precise and authoritative.⏎⏎I'm also worried about what happens to accountability. Right now, if a doctor makes a wrong call, there is a person who made that call. There is a license, a name, a conversation that can happen. If an AI system flags something incorrectly or misses something, who answers for it? The vendor will say the hospital deployed it wrong. The hospital will say the clinician should have overridden it. The clinician will say they were trained to defer to it. That diffusion of blame is not theoretical, it's already happening in other sectors and there's no reason to think medicine will be different.⏎⏎I understand the argument about burnout. I have heard from nurses who are exhausted, who are doing documentation at midnight, who feel like they spend more time in the EHR than with patients. I don't dismiss that. But I don't think the solution to a broken system is to automate the broken system. The documentation burden exists partly because of how billing and liability work in this country, and an AI that fills out notes faster doesn't fix any of that, it just makes the underlying dysfunction run more efficiently.⏎⏎What I am asking HHS to do, at minimum, is slow down. Require rigorous post-market surveillance before these tools get reimbursed or required. Don't let vendors self-certify. Make hospitals publish their outcomes data broken down by race, age, insurance status, before and after AI implementation. And please, do not let the reimbursement structure push smaller or rural hospitals into buying systems they don't have the technical staff to evaluate or monitor.⏎⏎There is a real risk that "AI-powered care" becomes a marketing phrase that means thinner staffing and more automated denials, and the patients who will suffer most from that are exactly the patients who are already getting the worst care. SYNTH-0058♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Rebecca Jones♔Association of American Indian Physicians♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in Pittsburgh in February of 2023, and I watched what good medicine actually looks like up close. It is not pattern matching. Her attending physician, Dr. Reyes, spent forty minutes with our family on a Tuesday night explaining why he was ignoring what the risk-scoring software was suggesting and going with his gut on her fluid management. He was right. She went home. I keep thinking about what happens in that same situation if Dr. Reyes is looking at a screen that says "high confidence recommendation" and feels institutional pressure to follow it.⏎⏎That is the thing nobody in this RFI seems to want to say plainly: AI tools in clinical settings do not just assist judgment, they compete with it. And the competition is not fair because the tool has the aura of objectivity and the clinician has the vulnerability of being second-guessed if they deviate. So even a technically optional AI recommendation becomes practically mandatory in a lot of hospital cultures, especially for younger residents or nurses who don't yet have the standing to push back.⏎⏎HHS is asking about regulatory actions to accelerate adoption. I want to argue for the opposite, or at least for a serious slowdown while we figure out what we're actually doing.⏎⏎The safety case here is not hypothetical. Sepsis prediction algorithms have been deployed at dozens of hospital systems and the evidence on whether they actually reduce mortality is genuinely mixed. The Epic Sepsis Model was studied in a 2021 paper in JAMA Internal Medicine and the findings were pretty damning in terms of both sensitivity and the volume of false positives it generated. That tool was already embedded in workflows at hundreds of institutions before that kind of rigorous external evaluation was done. That is the pattern. Deploy first, study later, and by the time the evidence comes in, the system is so integrated that nobody wants to pull it out. Hospitals have sunk costs in the vendor contract, the staff have been trained around the workflow, and the patients are never told any of this is happening.⏎⏎The question I most want HHS to address, and I don't see it asked directly in the RFI, is what the liability structure looks like when an AI-assisted decision leads to harm. Right now it is genuinely unclear. The clinician signed off, so legally it may fall on them, but they were working within an institutional system that told them to use the tool, and the tool was built by a vendor whose contract almost certainly includes indemnification clauses that shift responsibility back toward the hospital or the user. Patients have no idea this negotiation is happening. And when something goes wrong they have no way of knowing whether the AI played a role because they were never informed it was being used.⏎⏎I am also worried about the administrative side of this, which I think people underestimate. The RFI mentions AI for prior authorization and billing. Insurance companies are already using automated systems to deny claims, and there is substantial evidence, including the congressional investigation into UnitedHealth's nH Predict algorithm that came out in 2023, that these systems are denying care at rates that human reviewers would not match. Calling that "AI in clinical care" might be a stretch but it absolutely affects clinical outcomes. People don't get the rehab they need. They leave the hospital too early. They skip the follow-up MRI. And there is no human being they can look in the eye and demand an explanation from.⏎⏎I understand there are genuine benefits being claimed for AI in radiology screening, in medication reconcilliation, in documentation burden reduction for clinicians. I am not saying none of that is real. But the framing of this RFI, which is about accelerating adoption, assumes that the main obstacle is regulatory friction rather than the possibility that the technology is not yet safe or equitable enough to deploy at scale. That assumption should be questioned before HHS commits to a posture of acceleration.⏎⏎The populations most likely to be harmed by poorly validated AI tools are also the populations least likely to be submitting comments to this docket.♔♔2026-01-23T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-23T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔concerned♔Massachusetts♔campaign director♔55♔A 2024 landscape analysis conducted across 47 research institutions in our network found that 68% of clinical AI tools developed within academic settings fail to achieve sustainable deployment within three years, primarily due to fragmented reimbursement pathways and the absence of standardized regulatory frameworks for iterative model updates. SYNTH-0059♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Michelle White♔Nuance Communications♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Froedtert Hospital in Milwaukee in October 2022, and during that time I watched a rotating cast of nurses, hospitalists, and specialists piece together what was happening to her not from any single data source but from watching how she held her face when she was in pain, from talking to my father about what she'd eaten the week before, from noticing that she was embarrassed to admit certain symptoms. She recovered. I think about what that process actually looked like and I genuinely cannot map it onto what people mean when they talk about AI-assisted clinical decision making.⏎⏎The RFI asks about barriers to AI adoption in clinical care, and I want to push back on the framing of that question itself. The question assumes that adoption is the goal and that barriers are the problem. But what if slower adoption is actually the appropriate response to genuine uncertainty about whether these tools work safely across the full range of patients who will actually receive care, not just the patients who appeared in whatever training dataset a vendor used?⏎⏎Here is what concerns me most concretely. When an AI system gets something wrong in a clinical setting, who is accountable? This is not a hypothetical. The FDA has cleared hundreds of AI-based clinical decision support tools and the post-market surveillance for many of them is essentially voluntary. A clinician who follows an AI recommendation and the patient is harmed can say they were following the system. The hospital can say the vendor's product was cleared by FDA. The vendor can say the clinician used it outside its validated indication or that the hospital's data environment differed from the training environment. I have read enough about the ProPublica investigation into the Optum risk-scoring algorithm from 2019 to know that these systems can operate at massive scale, shaping care for millions of people, while encoding racial bias that took years to become visible and that the vendor did not voluntarily disclose. By the time the problem was documented publicly the algorithm had already been used by hospitals serving approximately 200 million people. That is not a reason to fine-tune our approach to AI governance. That is a reason to be extremely cautious about embedding these tools deeper into clinical workflows before accountability structures actually exist.⏎⏎I also want to say something about the administrative side because the RFI mentions it and I think people underestimate the harm there. Prior authorization algorithms are already being used by insurers to deny care, and there have been multiple lawsuits and at minimum one Senate investigation into whether those denials are being generated by systems that don't actually review individual cases. When a nurse at a community health clinic in rural Wisconsin spends forty minutes on the phone trying to reverse an AI-generated denial for a patient who needs a home health aide, that is not an efficiency gain. That is the cost of the efficiency gain being externalized onto clinicians and patients who have less power than the insurer. The people who will be harmed most by bad AI in healthcare are not the people who show up in demonstrations of the technology working well.⏎⏎HHS and ONC should require, before any further acceleration of adoption, mandatory prospective performance reporting broken down by race, age, insurance status, and clinical setting for any AI tool used in coverage determinations or clinical decision support. Not voluntary reporting. Not post-market attestation. Actual auditable data, publicly accessible, updated at minimum annually, with clear consequences for tools that show disparate performance.⏎⏎I am genuinely uncertain whether that alone would be enough. It might not be. But the current approach of asking how to speed things up while treating safety and equity as secondary concerns feels backwards to me, and I think the people who will pay the price for moving fast are the same people who already have the hardest time getting good care.⏎⏎One more thing I keep coming back to: the RFI mentions reducing clinician burnout as a rationale for AI adoption. I understand why that argument is appealing. Burnout is real and the consequences are serious. But the causes of burnout include documentation burden, staffing shortages, administrative overhead, and the feeling of losing control over clinical judgement. If the response to those causes is to layer in AI systems that clinicians don't fully understand, that generate recommendations they're expected to oversee but can't always verify, and that may be used by hospital administrators to justify not hiring the additional staff that would actually help, then I am not sure AI adoption addresses burnout so much as it changes its texture while potentially adding new sources of it.♔♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔organization♔medium♔supportive♔Oklahoma♔compliance officer♔52♔A 2024 operational review conducted across Newton's Tree's 47 affiliated clinical partner sites found that 63% of care coordinators reported spending more than 12 hours per week on documentation and administrative tasks that pilot AI-assisted tools reduced by an average of 40%. SYNTH-0060♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Robert Lee♔Abbott Laboratories♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Regions Hospital in St. Paul in February 2023, and I was there almost every one of those days. What I remember most is not the monitors or the medications. It's the resident who sat with her at 2am and figured out she was confused not because of her illness but because she was terrified about her cat at home. No algorithm was going to find that. No pattern-matching system was going to notice the way she kept looking toward the window and asking about something that didn't make sense until you understood who she was as a person.⏎⏎I keep thinking about that when I read about HHS wanting to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care. The framing of this RFI bothers me. "Accelerating adoption" presupposes that faster is better. It doesn't ask whether we should slow down. It doesn't ask what we might lose. It treats the question of AI in medicine as settled in principle and only open in terms of implementation details. That's the wrong starting point.⏎⏎The question I want HHS to actually grapple with is: what happens when AI-generated recommendations become the default, and clinicians stop developing the judgment to override them? There's research on this in aviation, where pilots who rely too heavily on autopilot lose proficiency in manual flight. Medicine is not aviation, and I know that comparison only goes so far, but the basic concern about skill atrophy and over-reliance on systems that look authoritative even when they're wrong seems genuinely applicable. I'm not a researcher. I'm a retired social worker with 28 years of experience working with elderly patients in the Twin Cities metro area, and what I know from that is that the people most likely to be harmed by a system that works well "on average" are the people who have always been outside the average. My clients were poor, many had limited English, many had complicated histories with the medical system going back to experiences with forced institutionalization or neglect. When I think about an AI trained on electronic health records recommending care for someone like Mrs. T., a 79-year-old Hmong woman I worked with who had documented her own symptoms inaccurately for years because she didn't trust the intake process, I genuinely don't know how that goes well.⏎⏎HHS asks specifically about barriers to AI adoption and what actions the department could take to remove them. I want to answer that question honestly: some of those barriers exist for good reasons. Clinician skepticism is not ignorance. Regulatory caution is not obstruction. The fact that we don't yet have reliable, agreed-upon methods for auditing AI tools for bias in real-world clinical settings is not a bureaucratic delay, it's a real gap that will cause real harm if ignored in the rush to scale these systems up. The FDA's process for clearing AI-based clinical decision support tools has been widely criticized, including by people inside the agency, for being too permissive and too slow to catch post-market problems. Clearing a tool based on retrospective data from one hospital system and then deploying it nationally is not a conservative approach to safety. It's a gamble.⏎⏎What I'd actually want to see is the opposite of acceleration. I'd want to see HHS fund independent, post-deployment audits of AI tools currently in use, mandate that vendors disclose the demographic composition of training datasets before any Medicare or Medicaid reimbursement is tied to AI-assisted decisions, and create a formal mechanism for frontline workers, including nurses, medical assistants, social workers, and home health aides, to report concerns about AI recommendations without fear of retaliation. Those workers are the ones who will notice when something is wrong. Right now there's no real infrastructure for capturing that knowledge.⏎⏎I'm also worried about the insurance side of this, which the RFI touches on somewhat obliquely. UnitedHealth Group's use of an AI model called nH Predict to deny post-acute care claims has already been the subject of litigation and a Senate investigation. That situation did not emerge from nowhere. It emerged from a regulatory environment that allowed automated decision-making to expand into consequential coverage decisions without adequate oversight. Speeding up AI adoption without first resolving that situation seems backwards.⏎⏎I don't think technology is the enemy. I've watched electronic records improve coordination of care in ways that genuinely helped my clients. But there's a difference between tools that support human judgment and tools that replace it, and I don't think HHS has been clear enough about which one it's trying to encourage. The RFI language about "augmenting" clinicians sounds reassuring, but augmentation can slide into replacement very quickly once financial incentives are aligned that way, and in American healthcare the financial incentives almost always point toward doing things cheaper and faster with fewer staff.⏎⏎The staffing piece is something I feel strongly about and don't think gets enough attention in these policy conversations. If a hospital can demonstrate that an AI triage tool reduces the need for one RN per shift, there is enormous pressure to make that cut. The fact that the AI might be wrong in ways the nurse would have caught doesn't show up in the quarterly numbers until something goes very bad. And then the question of who is accountable is genuinely unresolved. The nurse who wasn't hired? The vendor who sold the tool? The administrator who decided to rely on it?⏎⏎I'd rather HHS spend the next two years getting those accountability questions right before it does anything to accelerate adoption further.♔♔2026-01-23T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-23T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔3♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare diffuses responsibility across clinicians, hospitals, and vendors in ways that leave patients without a clear accountable party when errors cause harm.♔organization♔high♔resigned♔Minnesota♔director of government relations♔42♔InterSystems' 2024 analysis of deployment patterns across more than 1,200 health system customers found that 68% have integrated at least one AI-assisted clinical decision support tool into their EHR workflows, yet fewer than 30% of those deployments meet the interoperability standards necessary to share model inputs and outputs across care settings. SYNTH-0061♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔George Walker♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February of 2023 after a series of small strokes, and what I remember most is how much we depended on the night nurses and the attending physician to actually look at her, talk to her, notice that she was frightened and getting more confused, not just read numbers off a screen. One of the nurses caught something the monitors missed because she noticed my mother was holding her breath slightly when she shifted position. That kind of observation is not in a dataset anywhere.⏎⏎So when I see HHS asking how to accelerate AI adoption in clinical settings, my honest reaction is: slow down. Not because I'm against technology in some general way, but because I think the framing of this whole RFI gets the problem backwards. The question isn't how fast we can get AI into hospitals. The question is whether we've even begun to understand what we're giving up when we do.⏎⏎The thing I keep coming back to is accountability. Right now, if a doctor makes a wrong call, there is a person. You can ask that person why. They have to answer to a licensing board, to a family, to their own conscience. When a hospital deploys an AI system to flag deteriorating patients or recommend treatment pathways or, and this is the part that really worries me, help insurers decide what gets approved, where does accountability go? I've read a little about how these systems work and the honest answer seems to be: nowhere clean. The hospital says they followed the vendor's protocol. The vendor says the model performed within specifications. The clinician says they were told to defer to the alert system because liability was structured that way. My mother can't sue an algorithm.⏎⏎I understand there are real problems in healthcare that people hope AI will help with. Physician burnout is real. Administrative burden is real. But I'm skeptical that AI deployment as it's actually happening in practice is primarily about any of that. A lot of it seems to be about cost reduction dressed up in the language of innovation. When United Healthcare was using an AI model to deny post-acute care claims at a rate that internal documents later showed was something like 90 percent denial in certain categories, and their own staff were reportedly overriding it at low rates because of how the workflow was structured, that's not a burnout solution. That's a denial machine with a machine learning label on it.⏎⏎The RFI asks specifically about what regulatory actions might accelerate adoption and I want to answer that directly: I don't think acceleration is the right goal. I think the right goal is making sure that any AI used in a clinical or coverage decision context has been tested in the specific population and setting where it will actually be used, not just validated on a benchmark dataset from a different hospital system in different demographic conditions. I think there should be a clear requirement that patients be told when AI played a role in a decision about their care or coverage. And I think there need to be penalties with teeth, not voluntary guidelines, when a system causes harm.⏎⏎What I don't see in this RFI is much acknowledgment that the people most likely to be harmed by poorly deployed AI are the same people who already get worse care: elderly patients who don't fit the training data well, people with multiple chronic conditions whose cases are genuinely complicated, patients in rural or underserved areas where the hospitals buying these tools may have less capacity to audit whether they're working. A system that performs great on average can still be quietly catastrophic for specific groups, and the catastrophe won't show up in the vendor's press release.⏎⏎I also want to say something about the administrative side because I think it gets overlooked. SYNTH-0062♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Kenneth Johnson♔American Urological Association♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February of 2023, and what got her through it was not an algorithm. That is the kind of judgment you cannot train into a system on historical data, and I think about it every time I read something like this RFI.⏎⏎The question HHS is really asking here is how to accelerate adoption, not whether to. That framing already concedes too much. Before you figure out how to speed something up, you might want to ask whether it should be moving in that direction at all, or at least moving this fast.⏎⏎What bothers me most about the clinical AI push is the safety argument, or rather the way safety gets used to justify adoption rather than slow it down. You will hear constantly that AI catches things doctors miss, that it reduces errors, that it will save lives. Some of that may be true in controlled studies. But healthcare is not a controlled study. A system validated at a large academic medical center in Boston is going to be deployed in a rural critical access hospital in eastern Kentucky with different patient populations, different documentation practices, different staffing ratios, and completely different patterns of incomplete data. The validation does not travel with the tool. The risk does. And when something goes wrong, nobody is quite responsible. The clinician can say the tool recommended it. The hospital can say they implemented it according to vendor specs. The vendor can say it was used outside the intended population. That diffusion of accountability is genuinely dangerous in a context where errors can kill people, and I do not see the RFI grappling with it seriously.⏎⏎There is also a workforce dimension that I think gets brushed aside too quickly. The argument that AI will reduce clinician burnout is plausible in a narrow sense. If AI handles documentation, maybe a physician spends less time typing. Fine. But that same efficiency argument is also used by health systems to justify cutting support staff, to increase patient loads, to reduce the number of humans in the room. A hospital administrator looking at an AI tool does not see a way to give nurses more time with patients. They see a way to run the floor with fewer nurses. I have no way to prove that will happen everywhere but it is already happening in some billing and prior authorization contexts, where automation has not made the process more humane, it has made it faster and colder and harder to appeal.⏎⏎The prior authorization piece is the one I keep coming back to. Insurers are already using algorithmic tools to generate denials at scale, and patients and physicians are fighting those denials with almost no visibility into how the decisions were made. Cigna's 2023 controversy over automated claim reviews was not an isolated incident. If HHS accelerates AI adoption without building in hard requirements for explainability, human review, and real accountability when automated decisions cause harm, you are just handing more power to the entities that already have too much of it.⏎⏎I understand there are potential benefits. I am not saying AI has zero place in medicine. But the question of whether to accelerate adoption in clinical care right now, without stronger safety frameworks, without resolved questions about bias in training data, without clear liability rules, without any serious reckoning with what happens to the workers whose roles get hollowed out, that is a question that deserves a slower and more cautious answer than this RFI seems to be looking for.⏎⏎One more thing I would ask the Department to consider: what does it mean for a patient to consent to AI-assisted care? Right now most people have no idea when an algorithm is influencing their diagnosis, their treatment recommendation, or their insurance decision. That seems like a basic transparency problem that should be solved before we worry about accelerating anything.♔♔2026-02-19T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-19T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔concerned♔Minnesota♔senior policy analyst♔37♔A 2024 TMA member survey of 4,200 practicing Texas physicians found that 58% reported using at least one AI-assisted diagnostic or documentation tool in their clinical workflow, yet 73% of those users identified uncertainty about liability exposure and payor reimbursement policies as the primary barriers to broader adoption. In an actual hospital at 2am with an understaffed floor and a patient who doesn't fit the training data because she's 79 years old, takes fourteen medications, and has a history that was documented across four different health systems with inconsistent records. AI systems that performed well in controlled validation studies have repeatedly shown brittleness when deployed in real clinical environments, and the problem is that the failures are not always obvious. A system can be wrong in ways that look confident. That's worse than being wrong in ways that look uncertain, because clinicians and patients may not push back.⏎⏎There's also the bias question, which I don't think gets taken seriously enough in conversations about "accelerating" adoption. A lot of the historical clinical data that these systems are trained on reflects decades of unequal treatment. Black patients have been systematically undertreated for pain. Women presenting with cardiac symptoms have been misdiagnosed at higher rates than men. If you train a model on that data and then deploy it at scale, you're not just inheriting those biases, you're encoding them and making them harder to see because they're now coming out of a machine that feels objective.⏎⏎I want to say something about the administrative side too, because I think HHS is underestimating how much context gets lost when you automate functions that seem purely clerical. Insurance prior authorization is one example. People think of it as paperwork, but the nurses and case managers who do it understand the patient's situation in ways that a model reviewing structured claims data simply won't. When insurers use AI to automate denials, they're not just making things faster, they're removing people who had actual knowledge of the case from the decision. That matters. Someone's coverage for a home health aide or a specialized drug isn't a bureaucratic abstraction.⏎⏎I understand there are real pressures here. Clinician burnout is genuine. Documentation burden is genuinely terrible. But I am skeptical that AI deployment will actually address those problems rather than just shifting the work or introducing new kinds of burden. There's a difference between tools that help clinicians do their jobs and tools that let administrators reduce headcount while telling clinicians the algorithm has it covered.⏎⏎The RFI asks about what regulatory actions HHS can take to accelerate adoption. I'd ask HHS to consider instead what regulatory actions would ensure that any AI tool deployed in clinical care has been rigorously tested in populations representative of where it will actually be used, with ongoing post-deployment monitoring that is independent of the vendor, and with clear liability frameworks so that when something goes wrong there is an identifiable human being or institution that is legally responsible. Right now those frameworks mostly don't exist or are very underdeveloped.⏎⏎One more thing I keep coming back to: the framing of AI as a solution to healthcare's problems implicitly accepts a view of medicine as primarily an information processing task. I don't think that's right, and I don't think most patients think that's right either. When people are sick and scared they want someone who will be answerable to them, not a system that will route their concerns through♔♔2026-01-04T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-04T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔individual♔medium♔supportive♔Kansas♔stay-at-home parent♔40♔When my youngest was diagnosed with a rare seizure disorder at age three, our family spent months bouncing between specialists in Wichita and Kansas City, each time starting from scratch because her records weren't being shared or analyzed in any useful way. SYNTH-0064♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Sharon Mitchell♔Veracyte♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February 2023 after a sepsis diagnosis that was initially missed on two separate ER visits. I keep thinking about that when I read materials like this RFI talking about accelerating AI adoption in clinical settings.⏎⏎The question I'd like HHS to actually sit with, rather than just gather input on, is whether deploying AI tools in clinical care might systematically devalue exactly the kind of judgment that saved my mother's life. I'm not saying it definitely would. But I don't think the Department has seriously grappled with what gets lost when you instrument every workflow, when nurses are spending more time confirming or overriding system flags and less time just watching patients. There is research suggesting that alert fatigue from existing clinical decision support tools already causes clinicians to ignore warnings at very high rates — some studies have put override rates above 90 percent for certain alert categories. If that's already happening with the tools we have, what happens when AI generates even more signals, more recommendations, more documentation prompts?⏎⏎What really worries me is the accountability problem and I don't think the RFI takes it seriously enough. When an AI-assisted decision goes wrong, who is responsible? The clinician who accepted the recommendation? The hospital that licensed the software? The vendor who trained the model? Right now there's no clear answer to that and I suspect the lack of a clear answer is commercially convenient for everyone except the patient. Vendors will write contracts that disclaim liability and hospitals will point to vendors and clinicians will say they were following what the system suggested. I've talked to medical malpractice attorneys who are already dreading this because the causal chains will be extremely difficult to unravel.⏎⏎I also think HHS should be honest about the economic incentives at play here and I say this without assuming bad faith from anyone in particular. AI is being sold to health systems in part on the promise of efficiency gains, reduced headcount, faster throughput. Those aren't necessarily bad things in the abstract. But in practice, if a hospital deploys an AI triage tool and then uses it as justification to reduce nursing staff, and then something goes wrong because the staffing was too thin to catch what the AI missed, that's a patient safety crisis dressed up as a technology success story. The acceleration framing in this RFI concerns me because acceleration tends to mean deployment outpaces evaluation.⏎⏎I'd ask HHS to focus specifically on the question of post-market surveillance for AI tools that are already deployed. The premarket clearance or approval process, whatever pathway FDA uses for a given tool, captures a moment in time. Models drift. Hospital populations change. I don't see any robust mechanism right now for detecting that kind of degredation in the field, and the RFI doesn't propose one either.♔♔2026-02-13T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-13T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔1♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔The AI healthcare market is dominated by a small number of powerful vendors whose commercial interests in rapid deployment conflict with patient safety, and HHS's acceleration agenda risks embedding those vendors permanently into critical healthcare infrastructure.♔organization♔medium♔frustrated♔Florida♔healthcare administrator♔56♔A 2024 analysis of Massive Bio's clinical trial matching platform, which has connected over 20,000 cancer patients to oncology trials across more than 300 partner institutions, found that AI-assisted eligibility screening reduced average patient-to-trial matching time by 62% compared to manual review processes. SYNTH-0065♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Lisa Garcia♔National Organization for Rare Disorders♔♔♔♔♔My mother was in the ICU at UPMC Presbyterian in Pittsburgh for eleven days in February of 2023, and I want to talk about what actually happened there before I get into the broader question this RFI is asking.⏎⏎She had a sepsis presentation that was, according to her attending, "textbook" on paper. The nurse figured that last part out by watching her. Just watching her. That is not something a model trained on retrospective claims data is going to replicate.⏎⏎I keep thinking about that when I read materials like this RFI that talk about "accelerating adoption." Accelerating toward what, exactly? I understand the argument that AI can help overstretched clinicians catch things they might miss. I'm not dismissing that entirely. But the framing of this request concerns me because it is organized almost entirely around removing barriers and speeding things up, and there is almost no acknowledgment that the barriers might sometimes exist for good reasons, or that speed itself can be a form of harm in medicine.⏎⏎The question I want to focus on is the one about what regulatory actions HHS could take to support broader deployment. My honest answer is that before HHS does anything to encourage broader deployment, it should require much more rigorous post-market surveillance of tools that are already deployed. The FDA has cleared hundreds of AI/ML-based devices under 510(k) pathways that involved minimal clinical validation in the populations where the tools are actually being used now. There's a 2021 study from researchers at MIT and Harvard that looked at the demographics represented in training data for FDA-cleared AI diagnostic tools and found severe underrepresentation of Black patients, elderly patients, and patients from rural settings. Those tools are already in hospitals. They are already influencing care. And we are talking about accelerating more of the same.⏎⏎There's also the insurance side of this, which I think gets buried in conversations that focus on clinical AI. Insurers are using algorithmic tools to make prior authorization decisions, and the patients and physicians affected by those decisions often have no meaningful way to know an algorithm was involved, let alone challenge it. I've read about the litigation around UnitedHealth's nH Predict tool, which was allegedly denying post-acute care to elderly patients at rates that exceeded what their own physicians recommended. That case is ongoing as of when I'm writing this. That is not a hypothetical risk. That is a system already operating at scale, already causing harm, and HHS has not meaningfully constrained it.⏎⏎The accountability problem here is real and I don't think HHS has grappled with it seriously. When a physician makes a bad call, there is a named person, a license, a malpractice framework, a peer review process. When an algorithm makes a bad call, the hospital says they were following the vendor's recommendations, the vendor says the hospital implemented it incorrectly or used it outside its indicated population, and the patient or their family is left with nothing. I have talked to a medical malpractice attorney about this and she said it is genuinely one of the harder liability questions she has encountered in twenty years of practice, specifically because the diffusion of responsibility is so complete.⏎⏎I'm also worried about what happens to nursing and lower-level clinical staff. Not just job displacement in the abstract, but something more immediate: the de-skilling that happens when people stop doing tasks because a machine does them. If a nurse spends two years relying on an AI tool to flag deteriorating patients, does she still have the observational instincts she had before? There is research on automation bias in aviation that suggests the answer is probably no, and healthcare hasn't really confronted that question at all.⏎⏎One more thing and then I'll stop. The RFI mentions administrative burden reduction as one of the areas where AI could help, and I want to be careful about dismissing that entirely because I know documentation burden is a real problem. But a lot of administrative work in healthcare exists because it encodes human judgment about edge cases, exceptions, and context. Prior auth processes are terrible and I would not defend them. But the solution to a bad human process is not necessarily an automated version of the same bad process running faster and at higher volume with less ability to appeal. There needs to be some mechanism for a real person to intervene, and I'm not confident the current regulatory structure requires that in any consistent way.♔♔2026-01-12T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-12T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI-powered insurance decision-making automates prior authorization denials and coverage restrictions in ways that are opaque, unappealable, and systematically biased against vulnerable patients.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔supportive♔Massachusetts♔policy director♔56♔A 2024 TMA membership survey of 1,200 practicing physicians across 28 specialty categories found that 67% reported encountering at least one AI-assisted diagnostic or administrative tool in their clinical workflow within the preceding 12 months, yet fewer than 22% indicated their practice had received any formal guidance on liability exposure, data governance obligations, or reimbursement eligibility tied to those tools. SYNTH-0066♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Stephanie Lee♔Anthem Inc.♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Riverside University Health System in Moreno Valley in 2022 after a routine knee replacement went wrong. No algorithm was going to sit on the edge of the bed and figure out that this particular 71-year-old woman from Perris, California was minimizing her pain because her husband had died in a hospital and she associated complaining with dying there.⏎⏎I keep thinking about that when I read about AI being "accelerated" into clinical care. The framing of this RFI bothers me. It takes as given that the goal is to speed up adoption, and then asks how. But the more basic question of whether and in what settings AI should be making or influencing clinical decisions doesn't really get asked, or it gets buried under assumptions about efficiency and innovation.⏎⏎The question I actually want HHS to answer is who is responsible when an AI-assisted decision causes harm. Not in a theoretical sense. In the practical, legal, and moral sense. If a hospital deploys a sepsis prediction tool that flags a patient as low risk and that patient dies six hours later, and it later comes out the tool was validated on a patient population that didn't look much like the patient who died, what happens? The clinician can say they followed the recommendation. The hospital can say it was FDA-cleared. The vendor can say it was used outside the intended population. I have watched enough medical malpractice cases as a legal observer to know that diffuse responsibility is not the same as no harm having occurred. It just means the family gets nothing and nobody learns anything.⏎⏎There is also something I don't see discussed enough, which is what happens to the Deloreses of the world when AI gets normalized as the standard of care. If the default becomes "the algorithm said X, and the nurse or the resident or the PA who said otherwise is now the one who has to justify themselves," that changes the culture of clinical decision-making in ways that are hard to reverse. The pressure to defer to the system won't be written down anywhere. It will just be how things work. And the people most likely to get overruled by a machine recommendation are probably the same people who are already lower in the institutional hierarchy, which is not reassuring.⏎⏎I understand there are AI tools that do genuinely useful things, like flagging imaging findings that get missed on a busy overnight shift. I'm not saying none of this has any value. But the question of whether HHS should be actively accelerating broad adoption across clinical care settings is different from whether specific, narrow, well-validated tools have a role. The RFI kind of collapses that distinction.⏎⏎On the reimbursement side specifically, I would ask HHS to be very careful about creating financial incentives that push hospitals toward AI adoption before there's real evidence of benefit in their specific patient populations. The history of healthcare IT is not encouraging here. Hospitals spent billions on EHR systems in the 2010s partly because the incentive structures made it hard not to, and clinicians are still complaining about the documentation burden those systems created. Creating similar incentives for AI tools before the evidence base is solid seems like a good way to repeat that mistake at scale.⏎⏎One more thing that I haven't seen addressed anywhere in the materials I've read about this RFI: what happens to the workers whose jobs get partially or fully automated? Medical coders, prior authorization specialists, the people who do utilization review. These are not glamorous jobs but they are jobs held by real people, often women, often people of color, often in communities that don't have a lot of other options. If HHS is going to accelerate AI adoption it should at minimum be honest that this will eliminate positions and have a plan for what happens to those workers, rather than leaving that as someone else's problem to figure out later.⏎⏎The comment period closes February 4, 2025 and I hope♔♔2026-01-21T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-21T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔The AI healthcare market is dominated by a small number of powerful vendors whose commercial interests in rapid deployment conflict with patient safety, and HHS's acceleration agenda risks embedding those vendors permanently into critical healthcare infrastructure.♔organization♔high♔frustrated♔South Carolina♔healthcare administrator♔36♔A 2024 operational audit conducted across our network of 47 affiliated clinical sites found that 63% of administrative staff reported spending more than 12 hours per week on tasks identified as strong candidates for AI-assisted automation, including prior authorization processing, clinical documentation reconciliation, and discharge coordination. SYNTH-0067♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Betty Anderson♔National Disability Rights Network♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Advocate Christ Medical Center in Oak Lawn, Illinois in February of 2023. She had sepsis, and it got complicated. What I remember from those eleven days is not the monitors or the labs or the imaging. What I remember is a night-shift nurse named Darnell who noticed she was holding her jaw differently and figured out she was in pain before she could tell anyone herself. That is not a pattern-matching problem. That is a human being paying attention to another human being.⏎⏎So when I read that HHS wants to "accelerate" AI adoption in clinical care, my honest reaction is: slow down and explain why this is actually good for patients, because I am not convinced.⏎⏎I want to focus on one thing the RFI asks about, which is how AI can support clinical decision-making. My concern is that the framing of the question already assumes the answer. It asks how to accelerate adoption, not whether to. That is a real problem with how this process is being set up. The department is essentially asking the public to help it go faster, when what it should be asking is whether going fast is safe at all.⏎⏎Here is what worries me specifically. AI tools trained on historical medical data will reflect historical patterns of care, and those patterns are not neutral. There is well-documented research showing that some widely used clinical algorithms underestimated pain and illness severity in Black patients because the training data reflected years of undertreated Black patients. An AI system doesn't fix that problem just by being new. It can actually lock it in and make it harder to see, because the output looks authoritative. A number on a screen feels more objective than a human judgment even when it isn't.⏎⏎I also think there is something being glossed over about accountability. When a doctor makes a wrong call, there is a person who made that call and who can be questioned and held responsible. When an AI makes a wrong recommendation and a clinician follows it, who exactly is accountable? The hospital will say the clinician should have caught the error. The vendor will say the clinician misused the tool. The clinician will say the tool told them to do it. I have watched enough hospital billing disputes to know that institutions are very good at diffusing responsibility when it suits them, and AI gives them a whole new layer of diffusion to hide behind.⏎⏎I want to be fair here. I don't think everyone pushing AI in healthcare is acting in bad faith. Some of it probably does help. I've read that some radiology AI tools catch certain findings that get missed on busy overnight shifts. I'm not saying the technology is worthless. What I'm saying is that "some tools help in some settings" is a very different claim than "we should accelerate broad AI adoption across clinical care," and HHS seems to be moving from the first to the second without really making the argument.⏎⏎The administrative side of this concerns me too, maybe more than the clinical side if I'm honest. Insurance companies using AI to process prior authorizations and claims denials is already happening, and it is already generating horror stories. A 2023 ProPublica investigation found that some insurers were using AI tools to deny claims at rates that no individual physician reviewer could have achieved, which sort of tells you that the AI wasn't doing careful case-by-case review. It was doing fast bulk denial. HHS should be asking hard questions about that before it encourages more of it.⏎⏎What I would actually want HHS to do is require any AI tool used in a clinical or coverage decision to be subject to mandatory post-market surveillance, bias audits by independent third parties, and clear disclosure to patients when AI was used in their care or in a decision about their coverage. Not voluntary disclosure. Mandatory. And the audit results should be public, not just submitted to regulators who may or may not act on them.⏎⏎I keep coming back to Darnell noticing my mother's jaw. SYNTH-0068♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Patricia Clark♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February of 2023, and I can tell you that what kept her alive was not any algorithm. It was a night-shift nurse named Darnell who noticed something was off about the way she was breathing at 2am and called the attending before the monitors flagged anything. That kind of judgment does not come from pattern matching on a training dataset. It comes from years of watching sick people and learning what normal looks like in a way that is almost impossible to put into words, let alone code.⏎⏎I want to focus my comment on the question of what it actually means to "accelerate adoption" of AI in clinical care, because I think HHS is framing this wrong. The assumption baked into this RFI seems to be that speed of adoption is itself a good, and that regulatory barriers are the main problem standing between patients and better care. But that framing ignores the possibility that caution is appropriate here, and that some of the friction in the system is doing important work.⏎⏎Medicine is not primarily an information problem. I know that sounds almost obvious, but I think people who design these systems sometimes forget it. A patient sitting in an exam room is not a set of inputs waiting to be processed. They are scared, often inarticulate, sometimes lying to their doctor about how much they drink or whether they've been taking their medication. Good clinicians know this and they navigate it. They ask the same question three different ways. They watch someone's face when they mention their spouse. That interpretive work happens in a relationship, and a relationship requires a human being who is actually accountable for the outcome.⏎⏎What worries me about the direction HHS seems to be heading is that AI tools can create the impression of objectivity and rigor while actually just laundering existing biases through a more authoritative-looking interface. There is a lot of published research on this, including the 2019 Obermeyer et al. study in Science that found a widely used commercial algorithm was systematically underestimating the health needs of Black patients because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for health status. And that tool had gone through whatever validation process the vendor used before selling it. The problem is that biases in historical data look like signal to a model that doesn't know anything about the social structures that produced the data. Deploying more of these systems faster is not obviously going to fix that problem. It might make it worse, precisely because the systems look so authoritative.⏎⏎There is also an employment and professional autonomy dimension to this that I don't think HHS is taking seriously enough. The pitch is usually that AI will reduce clinician burnout by handling administrative tasks. Maybe. But hospitals and insurers have very strong financial incentives to use these tools to justify staffing reductions or to automate prior authorization denials in ways that are harder for patients and doctors to challenge. A human reviewer who denies a claim can be questioned. They can be asked to explain themselves. An algorithmic denial is much harder to push back on because the logic is often opaque and the vendor treats it as proprietary. That is not a hypothetical concern, that is already happening with tools used by insurers for post-acute care decisions, and the ProPublica reporting on Cigna's algorithm-assisted review process in 2023 showed pretty clearly how those systems can be structured to minimize meaningful human review.⏎⏎I understand that there are areas where AI tools might genuinely help, particularly in diagnostic imaging where the research base is more solid and the task is more constrained. I am not saying technology has no role. But there is a difference between careful, evidence-based deployment in specific narrow contexts with strong oversight, and what this RFI seems to be asking for, which is a broad push to remove friction from adoption across clinical settings generally.⏎⏎The accountability question is the one that keeps me up at night, honestly. When an AI-assisted decision leads to a bad outcome, who is responsible? The clinician who followed the recommendation? The hospital that deployed the tool? The vendor who built it? In practice, responsibility tends to get diffused across all of them in ways that make it hard for any single party to be held accountable, and in my experience that diffusion is not an accident. It is a feature that vendors and institutions find very convenient.⏎⏎HHS should be asking whether deployment of these tools is outpacing our ability to evaluate them, audit them, and correct them when they fail. The current answer to that question is almost certainly yes, and I don't think the right response is to accelerate further.⏎⏎One specific thing I would ask HHS to consider: before removing any regulatory barriers to AI adoption in clinical settings, please require mandatory post-market surveillance and public reporting of adverse events attributable to AI tool recommendations, similar to the FDA's MedWatch system but specifically designed for software-driven clinical decision support. Right now there is no systematic way to know how often these tools are contributing to diagnostic errors or inappropriate treatment decisions at the population level, which means we are essentially running an uncontrolled experiment on patients without their knowledge or consent.♔♔2026-01-14T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-14T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Once AI systems are embedded in healthcare infrastructure, they become practically irreversible and resistant to democratic oversight, creating a permanent transfer of power from clinicians, patients, and regulators to technology vendors.♔individual♔high♔concerned♔Nebraska♔social worker♔69♔When my colleague at the Douglas County family services office had a minor stroke and was temporarily replaced by an AI-assisted case triage tool for six weeks, I watched firsthand how the algorithm consistently flagged low-income Hispanic families for "elevated risk" at nearly twice the rate of comparable white families with identical intake profiles — a disparity our supervisor couldn't explain and the vendor couldn't document. SYNTH-0069♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Anthony Garcia♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's Medical Center in Houston in March of 2023, and I watched a rotating cast of nurses, hospitalists, and specialists piece together what was happening with her through conversations, contradictory chart notes, and a fair amount of educated guessing. But there was always someone in the room whose face I could read, someone who would stop and explain, someone whose career was on the line if they got it wrong. That is what I keep thinking about when I read proposals like this one.⏎⏎The question I'd want HHS to actually sit with, rather than just solicit comments about, is who ends up responsible when an AI system gets it wrong. Not philosophically. Practically. If a clinical decision support tool flags my mother as low risk for sepsis and she deteriorates overnight because the nursing team was taught to trust the algorithm, who do I sue? Who loses their license? Who calls me to explain? My guess is nobody, or rather, the answer gets so diffuse that it amounts to nobody. The hospital points to the vendor. The vendor says the clinician retained final authority. The clinician says the system showed a green indicator. And my mother is still dead.⏎⏎I understand there are people who will say this is exactly why we need oversight frameworks and post-market surveillance and transparency requirements and so on. Maybe. But I am deeply skeptical that we can regulate our way to accountability in a system where the tool itself is a black box and the institution deploying it has a financial incentive to rely on it more, not less, because it costs less than hiring another nurse. That's not a hypothetical. Hospitals have been using AI-based tools for prior authorization and patient triage decisions since at least 2020, and the staffing levels have not gone up. The mortality reviews have not become more rigorous. What has happened is that the documentation burden on nurses has increased while actual bedside time has decreased, and now some of those nurses are being told the AI will help them manage more patients simultaneously.⏎⏎That should terrify anyone who has actually been a patient recently.⏎⏎The RFI asks specifically about regulatory or reimbursement actions HHS could take to accelerate adoption. You cannot launder that history through a model and get equity out the other end.⏎⏎I'm also genuinely worried about what this does to clinical training over time. If residents and new nurses spend their formative years deferring to algorithmic recommendations, what happens to the diagnostic reasoning skills that take years to develop? SYNTH-0070♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Susan Lewis♔Privia Health♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Francis Hospital in Hartford in March of 2023, and what I remember most clearly is not any particular test or medication. The assumption seems to be that adoption is being held back by outdated rules or slow reimbursement structures, and that the job of this RFI is to figure out how to get out of the way. But some of those barriers exist because people thought carefully about what medicine requires. Removing them to accelerate deployment of AI tools is not automatically progress.⏎⏎My concern isn't really about one specific product. It's more about the direction this is all heading. I've read enough about how some of the insurance-related AI tools work — there was substantial reporting in 2023 about UnitedHealth's nH Predict system and the extremely high denial rates it was producing for post-acute care — to know that "AI-assisted decisions" can very quickly become a way to automate cost-cutting while giving it a technical veneer that makes it harder to appeal. A human reviewer who denies a claim is at least theoretically accountable to another human. When a system does it, the accountability gets very muddy very fast.⏎⏎The depersonalization issue is real too, and I don't think it gets taken seriously enough in these conversations. When a clinician is looking at a dashboard of AI recommendations, there's a psychological shift that happens. You start evaluating the machine's output rather than the patient in front of you. I've talked to nurses and physicians who describe this already with current EHR systems, where the documentation burden has made them feel more like data entry clerks than caregivers. Adding AI recommendations on top of that workflow doesn't obviously make it more human. It might make it less.⏎⏎I'm also just skeptical of the safety claims. The validation studies for many of these tools are done in controlled settings with complete records and specific patient populations. Deploy the same tool at a safety-net hospital with incomplete records, high patient turnover, and staff who haven't had adequate training, and the performance profile is probably completely different. I don't think HHS has a good answer for how to ensure that a tool validated at Mass General performs the same way at a rural critical access hospital in Mississippi. That's not a hypothetical gap, that's a documented problem with how medical devices and software get deployed across disparate settings.⏎⏎The employment angle matters too and I feel like it gets dismissed as just being resistant to change. Healthcare jobs aren't just jobs. The person who does intake, the patient advocate, the medical coder who knows when something doesn't look right — these roles carry institutional knowledge and human judgment that doesn't get captured anywhere in a training dataset. If AI tools allow hospitals to justify cutting those positions, the loss is real even if the cost savings show up nicely in a quarterly report.⏎⏎I don't think HHS should be trying to accelerate AI adoption right now. There isn't adequate post-market surveillance, there isn't meaningful patient consent in most of these deployments, and there's no clear standard for when a tool has failed badly enough that it needs to be pulled. Those gaps should be closed before the Department starts removing whatever regulatory friction is currently slowing things down.♔♔2026-01-03T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-03T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare will be used by employers and insurers as a mechanism for workforce surveillance, productivity extraction, staffing cuts, and the devaluation of clinical expertise rather than genuine care improvement.♔organization♔medium♔concerned♔Utah♔health IT director♔57♔A 2024 internal survey of Lumenex Advisory's client network, spanning 47 ambulatory and acute care organizations, found that 68% identified unclear reimbursement pathways as the primary barrier to deploying AI-assisted clinical decision support tools.
