SYNTH-0071♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Andrew Jackson♔American Psychiatric Association♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Riverside University Health System in Moreno Valley in the fall of 2022, and what kept her alive was not a pattern-matching algorithm. It was a night nurse named Delores who noticed that my mother's breathing had changed in a way that didn't show up on any monitor, who went and got the attending at 2 in the morning because something felt wrong. That kind of judgment is not a data point. I don't know how you would even begin to train a model on it.⏎⏎So when I read that HHS is looking at ways to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care, my first reaction is honestly just dread. Not because I think technology is bad in some general sense, but because I've watched what happens when hospitals get excited about efficiency tools. The tool gets deployed, the administrators feel good about it, and somewhere down the line the Deloreses of the world are working three more patients per shift because the system flagged everyone as stable.⏎⏎The question I keep returning to is: who is this actually for? When a hospital buys an AI triage tool or a predictive deterioration score or whatever the latest product is, they are making a business decision. They may genuinely believe it will help patients. They may also be looking at staffing budgets. Both things can be true at once, and I think HHS is being a little naive if it doesn't reckon with that tension directly.⏎⏎There's also a bias problem that I don't think gets taken seriously enough in these conversations. A lot of these systems are trained on data from academic medical centers or large hospital networks that already skew toward certain populations. A 2019 study published in Science found that a widely used commercial algorithm systematically underestimated the health needs of Black patients because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for need, and Black patients had historically been given less care and therefore generated lower costs. The algorithm looked objective. It was not. And the scary part is that most of the clinicians using it had no idea that was happening. If HHS accelerates adoption without mandatory, ongoing, post-deployment bias auditing, you are going to scale that kind of error across the entire health system.⏎⏎I want to be honest that I'm not a researcher or a doctor. I'm a social worker who has spent about fifteen years helping elderly and disabled clients navigate the healthcare system in Southern California, and a lot of what I see from the ground level is that my clients already feel like they are being processed rather than cared for. Adding AI into that experience without serious guardrails is not going to fix that feeling. It is going to make it worse. An 80-year-old woman with limited English who is trying to understand why her prior authorization was denied does not benefit from that denial having been generated faster by a machine. She benefits from a human being who can explain it, who can be held accountable, who she can appeal to in a language she understands.⏎⏎On the specific question of administrative AI, which I notice the RFI asks about, I want to push back on the assumption that administrative functions are somehow lower stakes. Prior authorization decisions affect whether people get surgery, whether they get the right medication, whether they stay in the hospital long enough to actually recover. These are clinical decisions wearing administrative clothing. Using AI to make them faster while reducing the number of people available to review appeals is not a neutral efficiency gain. I've had clients wait six weeks to get a prior auth denial reviewed by an actual person at their insurer, and that was before any of this AI acceleration happened. I can only imagine what that timeline looks like when the denial was generated automatically and the appeals queue is handled by a system that can't read the handwritten notes from my client's doctor in Riverside.⏎⏎HHS asks what barriers exist to AI adoption and how the department can remove them. I'd ask a different question: what barriers exist to accountability when AI causes harm, and are those barriers going up or coming down? Right now if a clinician makes a mistake there is a chain of responsibility. Licensing boards, malpractice, peer review, credentialing. When an AI system makes a mistake the vendor says it's a tool not a decision-maker, the hospital says the clinician should have caught it, and the clinician says they were just following the recommendation. My clients don't have lawyers. They can't navigate that.⏎⏎I'm also not sure the evidence base for most of these tools is as strong as the sales pitches suggest. The FDA cleared a lot of AI devices through the 510(k) pathway which doesn't require clinical trial evidence, just substantial equivalence to a predicate device. That is a very low bar for something that might be influencing whether your cardiologist orders a certain test or whether your insurer approves your hip replacement.♔♔2026-02-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-24T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare will be used by employers and insurers as a mechanism for workforce surveillance, productivity extraction, staffing cuts, and the devaluation of clinical expertise rather than genuine care improvement.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔frustrated♔Georgia♔director of government affairs♔47♔A 2024 landscape analysis conducted across GLI's network of 47 hepatology centers of excellence found that 63% had piloted or were actively evaluating AI-assisted diagnostic tools for early-stage fibrosis detection and hepatocellular carcinoma surveillance, yet fewer than 20% had successfully integrated these tools into standard clinical workflows due to reimbursement uncertainty and the absence of validated implementation frameworks. SYNTH-0072♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Rebecca Scott♔Foundation Medicine♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in January 2023 after a sepsis diagnosis that her primary care doctor almost missed because the clinic had recently rolled out an AI-assisted triage tool that flagged her as low-risk twice before a nurse overrode it. She survived. But I have spent the time since then thinking very hard about what it means when a machine is wrong and nobody is quite sure who is responsible for that wrongness.⏎⏎The question HHS is asking here seems to be mostly about acceleration. How do we move faster? What barriers are in the way? I want to offer the opposite perspective, which is that the barriers may be doing important work.⏎⏎Medicine is not primarily an information-processing problem. I know that sounds obvious but the entire logic of AI adoption in clinical care treats it as one. You have symptoms, you have records, you have lab values, and the machine finds the pattern. But my mother was not a pattern. She was a 71-year-old woman who had stopped eating, who was scared and minimizing her symptoms when she talked to the triage intake person, whose blood pressure was slightly elevated but not dramatically so, and who had a history of anxiety that made it easy to attribute her distress to something psychological. The nurse who finally pushed back on the AI's low-risk classification did so because she noticed something in my mother's affect, something that is not in any training dataset I am aware of. That is not a small thing. That is the thing.⏎⏎What concerns me about this RFI is that the framing assumes the main obstacle to AI adoption is regulatory friction. But there is another possibility, which is that the caution is warranted. The FDA has cleared hundreds of AI-enabled medical devices in the last several years, and the post-market surveillance on many of them is thin. We do not have good data on how these tools perform outside of the academic medical centers and well-resourced health systems where they were validated. A tool that works well at Mass General may behave very differently at a rural critical access hospital with incomplete EHR data, different patient demographics, and staff who had two hours of training on the system.⏎⏎I am also worried about what happens to the question of accountability when AI is in the loop. If the sepsis tool had been wrong and my mother had died, who would have been responsible? The nurse who relied on it? The hospital that deployed it without adequate training? The vendor who sold a tool validated on a dataset that did not look like my mother? This is not a hypothetical problem. It is already happening and the answer is usually that nobody is quite responsible in a way that leads to any consequences or any change.⏎⏎There is also a labor question that I think gets glossed over in these discussions. Hospitals are already using AI tools in prior authorization and claims review in ways that appear designed to reduce the number of human reviewers, not to assist them. UnitedHealth's use of an AI model called nH Predict to deny post-acute care claims, which led to a class action lawsuit filed in 2023, is the clearest recent example of what happens when efficiency becomes the governing value. The people most harmed by those denials were elderly Medicare Advantage patients who did not have the resources or the health to fight back.⏎⏎I am not saying AI has no place anywhere in medicine. I am saying that HHS should be asking different questions than the ones in this RFI. Not how do we accelerate adoption, but where has adoption already caused harm that we have not fully documented, and what accountability structures need to exist before we go further.⏎⏎The question of who audits these systems after deployment, and with what authority, and with what consequences for vendors whose tools cause harm, seems like the thing that should come before any acceleration.♔♔2026-01-17T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-17T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔organization♔high♔concerned♔Idaho♔chief medical officer♔49♔A cross-client analysis conducted by EHY Consulting LLC across 47 health system engagements in 2024 found that 68% of clinical AI implementation projects stalled at the integration phase due to conflicting interpretations of existing CMS billing guidance and ambiguous liability frameworks under current HHS regulations. SYNTH-0073♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Ashley Flores♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in Cincinnati in February 2023, and during that time I watched nurses and doctors make decisions about her care that I genuinely do not think any software could have made. Not because the information was complicated in some abstract sense, but because she was terrified and lying about her pain levels, and her doctor figured that out by watching her face when she thought nobody was looking. That is not a data point. I don't know how you train a model on that.⏎⏎I want to respond specifically to the question HHS is asking about what actions the Department can take to accelerate adoption, because I think the framing of the question itself is part of the problem. The assumption baked into "how do we accelerate this" is that more AI in clinical care is self-evidently good and the only obstacle is regulatory friction. I'd ask HHS to sit with a different question for a moment: what if slower is actually the right speed here?⏎⏎The safety argument against rushing this is not hypothetical. There is published research showing that some widely used clinical AI tools perform significantly worse on Black patients than white patients because they were trained on datasets that didn't represent those populations adequately. The pulse oximetry problem is a version of this, where a device that was considered reliable for decades turned out to be systematically less accurate for patients with darker skin. That wasn't caught quickly. It caused real harm to real people. AI systems are more opaque than a pulse ox, and when they fail, the failure mode is harder to see precisely because the output looks authoritative. A number on a screen that says "73% probability of sepsis" carries an air of objectivity that a nurse's gut feeling doesn't, even if the nurse is right and the model is wrong in that specific case.⏎⏎What concerns me most is the employment and accountability piece, though. I keep coming back to this. Hospitals are under enormous financial pressure and they will use any tool available to justify cutting staff. "The AI handles that now" is a cost-cutting argument waiting to happen, and it will hit the roles that are least glamorous but often most important: the care coordinator who knows which patients won't follow up unless someone calls them, the billing advocate who understands which denials are worth fighting. When those jobs get automated or eliminated, the people who needed that human attention will just fall through. They won't show up in any vendor's outcome metrics.⏎⏎I'm not saying AI has zero place in medicine. I genuinely don't know where the line is, and I'd be lying if I said the question is simple. But I think HHS should be asking much harder questions about liability before any of this goes further. Right now if an AI system recommends a treatment and the clinician follows it and the patient is harmed, it's not obvious who is responsible. The vendor will say the clinician should have overridden it. The clinician will say they reasonably relied on a validated tool. The hospital will say they followed the vendor's implementation guidelines. The patient or their family is left holding the consequences of a decision that nobody actually made.⏎⏎Before the Department does anything to accelerate adoption, I'd want to see a clear legal framework that places responsibility somewhere specific. Not a voluntary commitment from vendors to be transparent. An actual binding rule that says: if your AI system is used in a clinical decision and that decision causes harm, here is who is liable and here is what the remedy looks like. Until that exists, "accelerating adoption" just means accelerating the deployment of systems whose failure modes we haven't fully mapped, into a liability vacuum, in a field where the people being harmed are often already vulnerable.⏎⏎I also think the reimbursement question deserves more skepticism than it's getting. If CMS creates billing codes that reward the use of AI-assisted diagnosis or AI-generated treatment plans, you will immediately create a financial incentive for overuse that has nothing to do with patient outcomes. We have seen this before with other technologies and procedures. The incentive structure shapes the behavior, and right now nobody seems to be thinking carefully enough about what happens when there's money attached to running a patient through an algorithm.♔♔2026-02-23T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-23T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare diffuses responsibility across clinicians, hospitals, and vendors in ways that leave patients without a clear accountable party when errors cause harm.♔individual♔high♔angry♔Maryland♔social worker♔63♔When my client Darnell — a 58-year-old man I'd been working with for two years to stabilize his housing and manage his Type 2 diabetes — showed up to his appointment at a federally qualified health center in Baltimore, he was handed a tablet with an AI-generated care summary that misclassified his medication history and flagged him as "non-compliant" before his physician had said a word to him. SYNTH-0074♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Anthony Ramirez♔American Academy of Ophthalmology♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February 2023 after a sepsis diagnosis that was initially missed on her first ER visit. Accelerate toward what, exactly.⏎⏎The question I want to address is the one about what regulatory actions HHS should take to support AI adoption - because my answer is that HHS should pump the brakes, not hit the gas, and I want to explain why that's not just fear of new technology.⏎⏎Medicine has edge cases everywhere. Not unusual ones either - just ordinary patients who don't fit the training data. My mother is 74, speaks some English as a second language, has an unusual medication history because she immigrated and had different care in Poland for decades, and has a body weight that puts her in a category that's consistently underrepresented in clinical datasets. She is not a rare patient. She is an extremely common type of patient in this country. And AI systems trained on American hospital records from the last twenty years will have systematic gaps for people like her. The 2019 ProPublica reporting on the COMPAS recidivism algorithm showed how hard it is to detect bias in a system that produces confident-looking outputs. Healthcare AI has the same problem and the stakes are a lot higher than a parole recommendation.⏎⏎What worries me almost as much as the safety question is the accountability question. Right now if a clinician makes a wrong call, there is a person who made that call. They can explain their reasoning, face a board, be sued, lose their license. I'm not saying that system is perfect - it obviously isn't. But when an AI tool is involved, the hospital says the vendor is responsible, the vendor says the clinician had final sign-off, and the patient's family spends three years trying to figure out who to even depose. That diffusion of responsibility isn't a theoretical risk. It's already happening in prior authorization disputes where insurers use automated denial systems and then claim a physician reviewed the case, when in practice that review is a two-minute rubber stamp on a machine output.⏎⏎HHS should at minimum require that any AI system used in a clinical decision or insurance determination be disclosed to the patient in plain language, that the institution using it maintain full liability for its outputs, and that there be a mandatory human review process with documented reasoning before any AI-assisted denial or treatment recommendation is finalized. I have no reason to believe that AI tools will be used to give nurses more time with patients rather than to justify having fewer nurses on the floor. The incentive structure points entirely in the wrong direction. A hospital that adopts an AI monitoring system doesn't get credit for keeping the same number of nurses - it gets credit for maintaining outcomes with fewer. That's the business case and everyone knows it.⏎⏎The RFI asks about reimbursement mechanisms to support adoption. I'd ask HHS to think very carefully about whether reimbursement incentives for AI adoption might function the same way that some pay-for-performance metrics have - rewarding the appearance of quality while creating perverse incentives that harm actual patients. Tying Medicare reimbursement to AI adoption before we have long-term outcome data from diverse patient populations seems like exactly the kind of thing that looks responsible in a press release and creates problems five years later that are very hard to untangle.♔♔2026-01-07T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-07T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔concerned♔New Mexico♔campaign director♔46♔A 2024 landscape analysis conducted across 47 health systems partnering with the Scientific Knowledge Accelerator Foundation found that 68% of clinical AI tools developed through federally funded research failed to achieve meaningful deployment within five years of publication, citing regulatory ambiguity and misaligned reimbursement structures as the primary barriers. SYNTH-0075♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Kenneth Flores♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in October 2022 after a sepsis diagnosis that was nearly missed because the initial triage system flagged her as "low acuity." My honest answer is: slow down. I don't think that makes me anti-technology. I have a smartphone, I use online banking, I'm not a luddite. But medicine is different and I don't think the people pushing this have really grappled with how different.⏎⏎The core problem is that clinical judgment is not a pattern-matching exercise. When Darlene looked at my mother she was reading something that no algorithm trained on discharge summaries would capture: the particular color of her skin, the way she kept plucking at her blanket, the fact that her husband of 52 years said she just didn't look right. That's not mysticism, it's the accumulated interpretive skill of someone who has spent years at bedsides. An AI system that performs well on a validation dataset does not have that. And the danger isn't just that the AI will be wrong. It's that it will be wrong in a way that looks authoritative, and that clinicians under pressure, rushing between 14 patients, will defer to it rather than trust themselves.⏎⏎There are real numbers on this. A 2019 study in JAMA found that a commercial sepsis prediction algorithm used at the University of Michigan missed nearly two thirds of sepsis cases that human clinicians identified. That's not a fringe result, that's a widely cited finding about a condition that kills 270,000 Americans per year. And yet hospitals keep buying these systems because the vendors are aggressive and the procurement process doesn't require the same level of evidence that the FDA would require for a drug.⏎⏎What I want HHS to do is require that any AI clinical decision support tool demonstrate real-world effectiveness in the specific patient population where it will be deployed before it gets used, not just performance on a test set from a different hospital system in a different state. That seems obvious. Apparently it isn't, because right now it mostly doesn't happen.⏎⏎I'm also worried about something that the RFI doesn't really address directly, which is what happens to nurses and other clinical staff when these systems come in. I've talked to hospital workers in my county and what they describe is not AI freeing them up for the human parts of the job. It's AI generating alerts that they have to document responses to, creating more charting burden, more checkbox compliance, less time for the Darlenes of the world to stand at the bedside and notice something. There is a real possibility that accelerating AI adoption in clinical care makes care worse for vulnerable patients while making the quarterly reports look better.⏎⏎I understand there are things AI can probably do well. Radiology image screening, maybe. Drug interaction flagging. Scheduling. I'm not saying zero AI ever. But the framing of this RFI treats adoption as the goal and asks how to get there faster. I think the goal should be patient outcomes and the question should be whether AI helps or hurts those, case by case, setting by setting, population by population. Those are not the same question and the difference matters a lot depending on which hospital you end up in and whether your night nurse is overworked and has twelve other patients and an alert fatigue problem from a system that cried wolf forty times that shift already.♔♔2026-02-27T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-27T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔individual♔medium♔hopeful♔Oregon♔elementary school teacher♔49♔When my daughter's pediatrician in Bend used a new AI-assisted diagnostic tool to catch an irregular heart rhythm that two previous appointments had missed, I sat in that office and felt something shift in my understanding of what technology could actually do for ordinary families. SYNTH-0076♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Jessica Flores♔Pfizer Inc.♔♔♔♔♔My mother was in the hospital for six weeks in 2019 after a stroke, and what I remember most is not the technology — it's the nurse who noticed she seemed more confused than usual on a Tuesday afternoon and flagged it before it showed up in any test. The framing assumes that more AI adoption is inherently good and that the job of regulators is mostly to smooth the path. I'd push back hard on that assumption.⏎⏎HHS asks about what actions would accelerate AI adoption in clinical care. I want to argue that slowing down, or being very selective, would actually produce better outcomes for patients. The pressure to adopt AI tools in hospitals right now is coming more from vendor marketing and cost-cutting ambitions than from demonstrated patient benefit. I don't have a citation for that in the way a researcher might, but I've been following this space for a while and I keep seeing the same pattern: a hospital announces an AI partnership, the press release is full of words like "revolutionary" and "predictive," and then two years later there's a quiet study showing the system performed worse on Black patients or low-income patients than on the populations it was originally tested on. The sepsis prediction algorithm that was deployed at University of Michigan and later analyzed in critical papers is one example. Epic's sepsis model had a sensitivity rate cited around 63% in independent validation, which means it was missing more than a third of cases. That's not a tool you want clinicians deferring to.⏎⏎The RFI also asks about reimbursement changes that might support AI use. I'd urge HHS not to create financial incentives that push facilities toward AI adoption before there are meaningful standards for what "validated" means in a deployed clinical environment. Validation on a training dataset in a research hospital is not the same as performance in a rural critical access hospital with different patient demographics, different staffing levels, and different EHR systems. Right now those distinctions are blurry enough that a vendor can claim FDA clearance and hospitals assume that means something more comprehensive than it does.⏎⏎There's also something that doesn't get talked about enough in these policy conversations, which is what happens to the people doing the work. When a hospital installs an AI tool for prior authorization review, that's not just automation — it's a restructuring of who has power over the decision. The reviewer whose judgment used to be the final word is now being asked to override a system, which is psychologically and professionally a very different position to be in. Override rates tend to drop over time as workers feel pressure not to challenge the machine. That's documented in behavioral research on automation bias going back to aviation studies in the 1990s. Healthcare is now walking into a problem aviation spent decades trying to correct.⏎⏎I'm not saying there's no role for any computational tool in medicine. I'm saying that HHS should not treat acceleration as the goal. The questions worth asking are whether a specific tool demonstrably improves outcomes for the actual patient population it will be used on, who is accountable when it fails, and whether the clinician using it still has genuine autonomy to override it without institutional penalty. If the answer to any of those is unclear, that tool should not be in clinical use yet, and federal reimbursement policy should not be designed in a way that rewards facilities for deploying it anyway.⏎⏎I'd also add — and I realize this is a separate issue but it bothers me — that some of the administrative AI applications being discussed, things like scheduling and documentation summarization, sound harmless but they're also how institutions get comfortable with the idea of AI making decisions. It normalizes the technology in lower-stakes contexts and then the logic becomes "well we're already using it for notes, why not triage." That creep is worth paying attention to before♔♔2026-02-02T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-02T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔organization♔medium♔urgent♔Georgia♔healthcare administrator♔49♔A 2024 internal survey of 214 healthcare administrators across our network found that 67% identified reimbursement uncertainty as the primary barrier to deploying AI-assisted clinical decision tools, with 43% reporting that existing documentation requirements create workflow friction that offsets potential efficiency gains. The question should be what we lose when we push AI into more and more clinical decisions, and whether those losses are acceptable. A system trained on that data will reproduce those patterns and call it medicine. And because the output comes from a machine, it can actually feel more authoritative than a human judgment that you could at least argue with. Adding an AI layer on top of that system doesn't fix any of those things. SYNTH-0078♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Deborah Hernandez♔Leidos Health♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Piedmont Atlanta Hospital in February of 2023 after a series of medication errors that her care team initially could not explain. Nobody caught it for three days. I am not saying AI caused that error, but I am saying that the attending physician told my sister, almost offhandedly, that the tool "would have caught it if the records were there." That comment has stayed with me. The assumption was that the tool was right and the inputs were the problem. Not that maybe someone should have looked harder at a 74-year-old woman with an autoimmune condition and an active infection.⏎⏎That is what worries me about this whole push to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care. The framing is almost always that the tools are basically sound and the environment just needs to catch up. More data sharing, better training, cleaner records. But healthcare is not a data problem that happens to involve sick people. The incompleteness, the ambiguity, the patient who can't explain her symptoms clearly because she's scared and in pain and her daughter is crying in the corner -- that is not a bug in the input. That is the actual condition of medicine.⏎⏎I want to respond specifically to the question HHS is asking about what barriers are slowing AI adoption and what the department can do to remove them. My answer is that some of those barriers exist for good reasons and removing them would be a mistake.⏎⏎One barrier is clinician skepticism. When nurses and doctors push back on AI tools, the instinct in policy circles seems to be that this is resistance to change that needs to be managed. But clinicians are often skeptical because they are the ones who see what happens when the tool is wrong. They are the ones who have to look the patient in the eye. If a recommendation system tells a physician that a patient is low-risk for sepsis and the physician has a gut feeling that something is off, that gut feeling is not irrational noise. It is pattern recognition built from years of experience. Policies that treat clinician override rates as a problem to be minimized are getting this exactly backwards.⏎⏎Another barrier is liability uncertainty. Vendors and hospitals want clearer legal frameworks so they know who is responsible when an AI tool contributes to a bad outcome. I understand why they want that, but I think the public interest runs in the opposite direction. Diffuse liability is a warning sign, not a regulatory gap to be filled. When a system is designed in a way that makes it genuinely hard to say who is responsible for a decision, maybe that system should not be making that decision. Making it easier for vendors to deploy tools without clear accountability does not protect patients.⏎⏎There is also a real concern that I have not seen addressed adequately anywhere in this RFI's framing: the use of AI in insurance and prior authorization. Administrative AI is often treated as lower-stakes than clinical AI, but that is wrong. A system that automatically denies coverage for a procedure, or flags a claim for review, or decides that a patient's hospitalization was not medically necessary, is making a clinical judgment. It is just making that judgment from a remove, without seeing the patient, and with a financial interest baked into the training objective. The ProPublica and HHS OIG reporting on algorithmic claim denials at major insurers in 2023 documented denial rates that no individual physician reviewer could plausibly have achieved, which means these systems were not reviewing cases, they were processing them. That distinction matters enormously and I do not think HHS has grappled with it seriously in how this RFI is structured.⏎⏎I am not saying technology has no place in medicine. I am saying that acceleration is the wrong goal right now. The question should not be how do we remove barriers to adoption, but what harms are already occurring and who is being harmed. The populations most likely to be hurt by brittle, biased, or misapplied AI tools are the same populations that already get worse care: elderly patients, patients with multiple chronic conditions, patients with limited English, patients in under-resourced hospitals that cannot afford the infrastructure to deploy these tools safely but will deploy them anyway because they are marketed as cost-saving.⏎⏎My mother is fine, by the way. She went home after eleven days and is doing reasonably well. But I think about those three days a lot.♔♔2026-01-13T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-13T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI systems in healthcare inherit and amplify racial, socioeconomic, and institutional biases from training data, and their apparent authority makes those biases harder to detect and correct than human error.♔organization♔high♔urgent♔Oklahoma♔health IT director♔59♔A 2024 operational survey conducted across Lumenex Advisory's 47 client health systems — spanning more than 310 hospitals and ambulatory facilities — found that 68% cited interoperability gaps between AI decision-support tools and existing EHR infrastructure as the primary barrier to clinical deployment at scale. SYNTH-0080♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Daniel Nguyen♔National Association of Social Workers♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Froedtert Hospital in Milwaukee in March of 2023 and what I remember most is that the nurses and one particular hospitalist, Dr. Anand, actually looked at her when they walked into the room. They noticed she was holding her arm differently. They noticed she seemed more confused after a medication change. None of that was in a data field. I keep thinking about what happens to those eleven days if the clinician's first job is reviewing what a system has already decided.⏎⏎I want to address specifically the question HHS is asking about barriers to adoption and what the department can do to accelerate use of AI in clinical settings. My answer is that I think HHS is asking the wrong question. The question should be whether adoption should be accelerated at all, and I don't think the department has made a serious case that it should.⏎⏎Medicine is not fundamentally an information retrieval problem. Even when it involves a lot of data, what clinicians actually do is interpret, and that interpretation depends on things that don't get captured: the patient who minimizes symptoms because they're afraid of a diagnosis, the family member who contradicts the patient's self-report, the way someone's breathing sounds versus how it looks on a chart. An AI system trained on historical records is going to reflect the patterns of who got diagnosed with what, and those patterns in American healthcare are not neutral. There's well-documented research, including work published in Science in 2019 by Obermeyer et al., showing that a widely-used commercial algorithm systematically underestimated the health needs of Black patients because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for health, and Black patients had historically received less care even when sicker. That kind of bias doesn't announce itself. The output just looks like a number.⏎⏎What worries me about this RFI is that it frames the question as how to remove obstacles to AI adoption rather than how to protect patients from premature or inappropriate deployment. Regulatory action to "accelerate" adoption could easily mean weakening the scrutiny that currently exists, making it easier for hospitals to deploy tools that have been validated in academic medical centers on populations that don't look like the patients in rural community hospitals or safety net facilities. Those institutions often have the least capacity to audit what the tools are actually doing.⏎⏎There's also the insurance side of this, which I think deserves a lot more attention than it's getting. AI-driven prior authorization systems are already being used to deny care, and there have been multiple lawsuits and congressional inquiries into companies like UnitedHealth for using algorithmic tools to issue denials at a rate that human reviewers couldn't possibly sustain. Accelerating AI adoption in that context doesn't help patients, it just makes the denial pipeline faster and harder to appeal because you can't really cross-examine an algorithm.⏎⏎I understand there are people who believe AI will genuinely help with diagnostics, with early detection, with reducing the burden of documentation on overworked clinicians. I'm not completely dismissing that. But the question of whether a technology can help is different from whether deploying it widely and quickly, under commercial incentives, in under-resourced settings, with diffuse accountability, will actually produce those benefits at scale. I don't think HHS has answered that second question. I'm not sure it has seriously asked it.⏎⏎The accountability issue is the one I keep coming back to. When my mother's care team made decisions, there were humans who could be asked why. The hospitalist could be asked why he changed the medication. The nurse could be asked what she observed. If something had gone wrong, there were people who bore responsibility. I don't know how that works when the recommendation came from a system that the hospital licensed from a vendor who will say the clinician should have overridden it if it was wrong, while the clinician will say they were trained to trust it, and the hospital will say it met all regulatory requirements at the time of deployment.⏎⏎HHS should at minimum require that any AI tool used in clinical decision-making be subject to ongoing post-market surveillance requirements equivalent to what FDA applies to high-risk medical devices, with mandatory reporting of adverse events and public disclosure of validation data including demographic breakdowns. SYNTH-0081♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Emily Garcia♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February 2023, and I sat with her through most of it. What got her through was not any particular test result or algorithm. It was a night nurse named Darnell who noticed she was holding her breath every time a certain resident came in the room, figured out she was terrified of him specifically because he reminded her of a doctor who had dismissed her years ago, and quietly rearranged the rotation. No system is going to catch that. No system is even looking for it.⏎⏎That's where I want to start, because I think HHS is framing this whole question wrong. The RFI asks how to accelerate AI adoption, which assumes acceleration is the goal. I'd like to push back on that assumption pretty hard.⏎⏎Medicine involves a lot of things that look like information problems but aren't. A patient who says "I feel fine" might mean they feel fine, or they might mean they've given up, or they might mean they're scared of what you'll do if they say otherwise. Clinicians learn to hear the difference over years of practice. That kind of interpretive work is not pattern matching on historical data. And I worry that the more we dress up AI recommendations in authoritative-looking interfaces, the more we train clinicians to stop trusting that interpretive instinct and start deferring to the machine. That shift will not show up in any accuracy metric. It will show up years later in outcomes we can't trace back to a cause.⏎⏎There's also the bias problem, which I don't think the field has come close to solving. Pulse oximeters were found to be less accurate on darker skin tones, and that was a physical device with a simple measurement task and it still took decades to get meaningful attention. AI systems trained on historical clinical data are inheriting every inequity baked into that history, who got admitted, who got believed, who got the aggressive treatment and who got sent home with ibuprofen. When those systems are wrong in ways that track race or income or disability status, the error looks like an objective output. That is genuinely more dangerous than a human bias because at least with a human you can argue back.⏎⏎I also want to say something about the employment question, because I think it keeps getting brushed aside. Radiology techs, medical coders, prior authorization reviewers, clinical documentation specialists, these are real jobs held by real people, many of whom spent years and real money on training and certification. The pitch that AI will free clinicians from administrative burden is probably accurate in some narrow sense, but "administrative burden" is somebody else's actual job. When a hospital cuts its coding department by 40 percent because a vendor promises their system can handle it, those aren't inefficiencies being eliminated. Those are livelihoods. And the accountability that goes with those human roles doesn't automatically transfer to the software that replaces them.⏎⏎What I keep coming back to is the question of who's responsible when something goes wrong. Right now if a doctor misses something, there is a person, a license, a malpractice framework, a professional relationship. If an AI system misses something and a hospital deployed it and a vendor sold it and the training data came from somewhere else entirely, the responsibility gets spread so thin it basically disappears. I've read some of the arguments that liability frameworks will evolve to handle this, and maybe they will, but "liability frameworks will evolve" is not a reassurance you'd accept in any other safety-critical domain.⏎⏎I'm not saying every AI application in healthcare is worthless. Scheduling software is probably fine. Flagging potential drug interactions in a pharmacy system, maybe. But the RFI doesn't distinguish between those relatively low-stakes administrative uses and things like diagnostic support, treatment recommendations, or insurance coverage determinations, all of which carry consequences that are serious enough to require a human being who is actually accountable in a meaningful way.⏎⏎On that last point about insurance specifically: the use of AI in prior authorization and claims denial decisions should not be lumped in with clinical care tools as if they're the same category of thing. Cigna made news in 2023 for a system that was reportedly denying claims at a rate of tens of thousands per hour with physicians spending an average of around 1.2 seconds per review. That's not AI assisting a human decision. That's a human providing legal cover for an automated denial. HHS has authority to address that and I think it should, but the answer is not to build better AI for it.♔♔2026-02-23T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-23T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust, and AI threatens to reduce patients to data points and clinicians to machine overseers.♔individual♔high♔supportive♔West Virginia♔veteran♔66♔After my third surgery at the VA hospital in Martinsburg, my recovery was monitored partly through an AI-flagging system that caught an early sign of infection in my wound drainage data before my care team had even reviewed my chart — a nurse told me later it had shaved nearly eighteen hours off what could have been a dangerous delay. SYNTH-0082♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Andrew Hernandez♔Society of Hospital Medicine♔♔♔♔♔My mother was in the ICU at Brigham and Women's Hospital for eleven days in February 2023, and I watched what it actually looks like when a human clinician makes a hard call. None of that was in the chart. None of it would have been in any dataset. And I keep thinking about what would have happened if the recommendation had come from a system that only knew what was documented.⏎⏎I'm responding specifically to the questions in this RFI about what actions HHS can take to accelerate AI adoption in clinical settings, because I think the framing itself is the problem. The question assumes acceleration is the goal. I'd argue the department should be asking something harder: under what conditions, if any, should AI be making or influencing clinical recommendations at all, and who bears responsibility when it's wrong?⏎⏎The safety case against moving fast here is not abstract. AI systems trained on historical health data inherit the biases in that data. There's well-documented evidence -- the 2019 Obermeyer study in Science being probably the most cited -- that a widely used commercial algorithm systematically underestimated the health needs of Black patients because it used healthcare cost as a proxy for health need, and Black patients had historically received less care. That's one study about one algorithm. We don't know how many systems like that are already deployed, because the transparency requirements don't exist yet, and this RFI seems more interested in getting more AI into clinics than in auditing what's already there.⏎⏎And I do understand the argument that AI could reduce burnout, catch things clinicians miss, help with documentation burden. I'm not dismissing that entirely. But there's a version of this that goes very badly, where hospitals use "AI-assisted care" as cover for cutting nursing staff, where insurers use algorithmic prior authorization to deny claims faster and with less human review, where the clinician becomes someone who rubber-stamps machine outputs because they're seeing thirty patients a day and there's no time to override. That's not a hypothetical. Prior authorization denial rates have been rising for years, and insurers have already begun deploying AI tools to manage that process. UnitedHealth's use of an AI model called nH Predict to deny post-acute care claims was the subject of a federal lawsuit in 2023. The model allegedly had a 90% error rate on appeal. That happened without HHS accelerating anything.⏎⏎The accountability problem is the thing I can't get past. When an AI system contributes to a bad outcome, the liability structure is genuinely unclear. The clinician can say they followed a recommendation, the hospital can say the vendor certified the tool, the vendor can say the clinician had final authority. That diffusion of responsibility is not a technical problem that better regulation can fully solve. It's built into the model. Medicine has always worked because there is a named human being who is accountable to you. Your doctor. Your surgeon. Someone you can look at and who has to look back at you.⏎⏎HHS should not be in the business of accelerating the erosion of that.⏎⏎What I'd actually want to see, if this comment accomplishes anything, is a requirement that any AI tool used in clinical decision-making or insurance determinations be subject to mandatory prospective auditing in the specific population where it's deployed, not just the population where it was validated. And the results of those audits should be public. That's the minimum. Before we talk about acceleration, we should talk about whether the systems already in use are doing what their vendors claim, and for which patients they are quietly failing.♔♔2026-01-06T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-06T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI systems in healthcare inherit and amplify racial, socioeconomic, and institutional biases from training data, and their apparent authority makes those biases harder to detect and correct than human error.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔supportive♔Mississippi♔policy director♔36♔A 2024 survey of 2,800 individuals living with multiple sclerosis conducted through our patient registry found that 67% reported delays of six months or longer in receiving a confirmed MS diagnosis, with neurologist access identified as the primary barrier in rural and underserved communities. SYNTH-0083♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Karen Torres♔Paige AI♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Riverside University Health System in Moreno Valley in March of 2023. She has a rare clotting disorder, speaks limited English, and was admitted after a fall that turned out to involve a subdural hematoma. I watched what it took for her care team to get that right. Her attending physician sat with us for forty minutes on the second day, not reviewing labs, not entering orders, just talking. Trying to understand what her baseline was, what she was afraid of, what she could and couldn't tell him about her symptoms. There is no way to overstate how much of that conversation was irreducibly human. She kept saying her head felt "full of water" and he knew, from context, from watching her face, from asking follow-up questions that weren't in any protocol, that she meant pressure, not dizziness. That distinction mattered clinically. An AI parsing her language would have had no idea what to do with that.⏎⏎I keep thinking about that when I read about accelerating AI adoption in clinical settings. The word "accelerating" is doing a lot of work in this RFI. It assumes the direction is correct and the only question is speed. I don't think that's right.⏎⏎To the extent this RFI is asking what HHS should do to support AI in clinical care, my answer is: slow down considerably, and be honest about what isn't known yet. I want to focus on two things.⏎⏎First, the safety question is not settled and the current frameworks for evaluating these tools are not adequate. When a hospital deploys an AI-assisted diagnostic tool, there is often no requirement that it be validated on a population that resembles the patients actually being treated. A system trained predominantly on records from large academic medical centers in the Northeast may perform very differently at a rural critical access hospital in Mississippi or a safety-net hospital serving recent immigrants. We already know from published research, including the 2019 Obermeyer et al. study in Science on a widely used commercial algorithm, that these tools can embed racial bias in ways that aren't visible from the outside. That study showed a specific commercial health management algorithm was systematically underestimating the health needs of Black patients. That wasn't a hypothetical risk. That was deployed, in real hospitals, affecting real people. And the vendor's response was essentially to update the tool quietly. There was no recall, no mandatory reporting, no clear accountability.⏎⏎HHS should not be accelerating adoption into that regulatory vacuum. Before any new incentives or reimbursement pathways are created for AI clinical tools, there should be mandatory prospective validation requirements, demographic breakdown of performance data, and a clear post-market surveillance mechanism with teeth. The FDA's existing frameworks for software as a medical device are not keeping pace with how quickly these tools are being deployed and updated, and ONC's certification program was not designed with this in mind.⏎⏎Second, and honestly I'm not sure this gets discussed enough in the official framing, there's the question of what AI does to the clinician-patient relationship over time even when it technically works. If a physician knows that an AI flagged something before they did, or that the AI's recommendation is sitting in the chart, that changes how they think. There's research on automation bias going back decades. Clinicians defer to systems even when they have good reason not to. A study published in JAMA Network Open in 2021 found that clinician decision-making shifted measurably when AI recommendations were present, even when those recommendations were wrong. That's not a bug that gets patched. That's a cognitive dynamic that becomes baked into clinical culture.⏎⏎The reimbursement angle worries me too. If CMS creates billing codes or quality incentives tied to AI tool use, hospitals will adopt those tools regardless of whether they improve care, because the financial signal will push them to. We've seen this pattern before with electronic health records. The incentive structure drove adoption faster than the evidence supported and we are still dealing with the usability and burnout consequences of that.⏎⏎I'm not saying AI has no place in medicine ever. Radiology screening, certain genomic analyses, some administrative functions, maybe. But the framing of this RFI treats hesitation as an obstacle to overcome rather than a reasonable response to genuine uncertainty. And the populations most likely to be harmed by poorly validated AI tools are the same populations who already have the least power to push back when something goes wrong, elderly patients in nursing facilities, people with disabilities, people who don't speak English as a first language, people whose medical histories are thin because they've had limited access to care throughout their lives and whose data is therefore underrepresented in every training set these tools are built on.⏎⏎My mother's doctor doesn't use any AI-assisted diagnostic tools that I know of. He's been practicing for about twenty-six years. I don't think that's a coincidence.♔♔2026-01-26T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-26T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI systems in healthcare inherit and amplify racial, socioeconomic, and institutional biases from training data, and their apparent authority makes those biases harder to detect and correct than human error.♔organization♔high♔concerned♔Michigan♔chief medical officer♔46♔A 2024 internal analysis of Sancian LLC's deployment portfolio across 47 clinical implementation sites found that AI-assisted diagnostic tools reduced average time-to-diagnosis by 23% in high-acuity settings, yet 68% of those sites reported that reimbursement uncertainty was the single greatest barrier to sustaining and scaling those deployments. SYNTH-0084♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Sharon White♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Regions Hospital in St. Paul in February of 2023, and not once during that time did any machine tell us what was really happening. Some of the barriers are there for good reasons and removing them without understanding why they exist seems reckless.⏎⏎One barrier is clinician skepticism, and I'd argue that skepticism is rational. There's a study from 2021, I believe it was published in JAMA Internal Medicine, that looked at a sepsis prediction algorithm deployed at University of Michigan and found that the tool flagged so many false positives that nurses were largely ignoring it within months of deployment. The tool was technically "accurate" in some statistical sense but it was creating alert fatigue, which is itself a patient safety problem. So you have a situation where an AI system designed to save lives may have made the environment slightly more dangerous, and the response from the vendor was not to withdraw the product but to say the hospital needed better "implementation." That kind of thing is why frontline workers don't trust these systems. And they shouldn't have to trust them just because the vendor's slides look impressive.⏎⏎What concerns me almost as much as the direct safety issues is what AI does to the structure of accountability. When a physician makes a bad call, there is a person who made that call. They can be questioned, retrained, disciplined, sued. When an AI system contributes to a bad outcome, that chain of responsibility gets very murky very fast. The hospital says they followed the vendor's guidance, the vendor says the hospital deployed it wrong, the physician says they were just acting on the recommendation. Patients and families get left with no clear answer about what happened or why. That isn't a hypothetical, it's already happening in prior authorization contexts where insurers are using AI tools to deny claims and nobody can explain the reasoning in human terms.⏎⏎I also want to say something about the employment angle because I think it gets dismissed too quickly as just being about protecting jobs. It's not only about that. When you reduce the number of skilled humans involved in care decisions, you reduce the number of people who are in a position to notice something is wrong and say so. A hospital that has replaced its utilization review nurses with an automated system has also eliminated the people who might catch a systematic error in that system. There's a kind of institutional knowledge that lives in people, not in data, and once you've cut those positions you don't easily get that back.⏎⏎The administrative functions question is one where I'd push back especially hard on HHS's apparent assumption that AI is neutral or beneficial. Prior authorization is a good example. The argument for automating it is that it's time-consuming and expensive. That's true. But prior authorization also requires judgment about individual circumstances, and the history of insurance companies using automated tools in this space is not encouraging. In 2023, a ProPublica investigation found that Cigna's automated denial system was being used to reject claims without physician review, sometimes in under a minute per case. That's not efficiency. That's a company using the appearance of technological sophistication to avoid the cost of actually evaluating claims.⏎⏎I understand there are people who genuinely believe AI will improve care and I'm not saying they're all wrong about everything. Some narrow applications probably do help. But HHS is asking how to accelerate adoption broadly, and I think the honest answer is that the pace of adoption should be determined by evidence of benefit and by the capacity of oversight systems to catch problems, not by vendor enthusiasm or by the fact that some hospitals have already bought the tools and want to justify the purchase.⏎⏎The regulatory infrastructure for evaluating these tools in real-world clinical settings is honestly pretty thin right now. The FDA's framework for software as a medical device is still being worked out, post-market surveillance for AI tools is not well established, and there's no consistent requirement that hospitals publicly report adverse events linked to AI-assisted decisions. Before HHS does anything to remove barriers to adoption it should probably fix that situation first, because right now we are deploying systems at scale without the monitoring infrastructure to know whether they're helping or♔♔2026-02-07T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-07T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare diffuses responsibility across clinicians, hospitals, and vendors in ways that leave patients without a clear accountable party when errors cause harm.♔individual♔high♔concerned♔Connecticut♔retail worker♔63♔When my cardiologist at Hartford Hospital first mentioned that an AI screening tool had flagged an irregularity in my echocardiogram that she wanted to investigate further, I felt equal parts grateful and unsettled — grateful that something caught it, unsettled that I couldn't get a straight answer about how the system worked or who was accountable if it was wrong. The question is whether any particular use of AI actually makes care better for the patient in front of the clinician, not better on average across a dataset, not more efficient from a billing standpoint, better for that specific scared person in that specific room.⏎⏎What concerns me most about this RFI is what it doesn't ask. It doesn't ask what happens when AI gets it wrong. It doesn't ask who is liable. It doesn't ask how a patient is supposed to know that a recommendation their doctor just made was generated or filtered through a commercial AI product that the hospital licensed from a vendor with no particular accountability to that patient. There are already documented cases of AI-based tools used in prior authorization decisions that have denial rates far above what any individual physician reviewer would approve. ProPublica and others have reported on UnitedHealth's use of an algorithm called nH Predict that apparently generated 90-day post-acute care limits that physicians and patients couldn't effectively challenge. A biller who has worked a department for ten years knows things that are not in the structured fields of a claim form. An intake coordinator knows that a particular patient is homeless and that the address on file is a shelter and that the discharge plan needs to account for that. If AI reduces the need for medical coders, for care coordinators, for certain categories of nursing support staff, where do those people go? These are often jobs held by women, by people of color, by workers without four-year degrees who found stable employment with benefits in healthcare. The efficiency gains from AI will accrue to health systems and vendors. The job losses will fall on specific people in specific communities. If a radiologist can catch a tumor she might otherwise have missed because a model flagged something in a scan, that seems worth exploring carefully. Vendors want to sell products. Health systems want to cut costs. And the patients and workers who bear the risk of getting this wrong don't have nearly as much voice in this process as the people who stand to profit from it.⏎⏎I'd ask the department specifically to consider requiring pre-deployment audits for any AI system used in coverage or denial decisions, with results that are publicly available and broken down by race, age, insurance type, and diagnosis category. Those are not the same populations and they are not the same conditions.⏎⏎I do want to be honest that I am not opposed to every possible use of AI in healthcare. SYNTH-0087♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Charles Ramirez♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February of 2023. She was 74, and her presentation was confusing enough that the team spent the first 48 hours running down possibilities before a senior hospitalist sat with her for about forty minutes, asked questions nobody had thought to ask, and figured out what was actually going on. It wasn't in her chart. It wasn't in any data. It came out in conversation because she trusted this particular doctor and finally told him something she'd been embarrassed to mention. I think about that constantly when I read materials like this RFI.⏎⏎The framing of this request for information bothers me. The question isn't really "should we accelerate AI adoption" — it's structured so that the answer is already assumed to be yes, and the public is just being asked how fast and in what ways. That kind of framing closes off the more important question, which is whether clinical care is even the right place for these tools at all, at least in their current state and deployment context.⏎⏎What I keep coming back to is the accountability problem. When my mother's care went sideways initially, there was a doctor accountable for that. A person. Someone who could be questioned, who felt responsible, who in fact stayed late to correct course. If an AI triage tool or a diagnostic support system had flagged the wrong condition at the outset and the team had anchored on that — which is a well-documented cognitive risk, anchoring bias doesn't go away just because the initial suggestion came from a computer — who answers for it? The vendor says the clinician had final authority. The clinician says the tool said X and they followed standard protocol. The hospital says the tool was FDA cleared. Nobody is actually responsible. That diffusion of accountability is genuinely dangerous and I don't think this RFI takes it seriously enough.⏎⏎There's also something that gets almost no attention in these conversations, which is what happens to the clinician's judgment over time when they are routinely prompted by AI recommendations. There's research going back to automation bias studies in aviation — pilots who flew with increasingly automated systems showed degraded manual flying skills when those systems failed. Medicine isn't the same as aviation but the principle isn't crazy. If a nurse practitioner spends years deferring to a risk-stratification algorithm for every patient, what happens to her ability to recognize the patient who doesn't fit the model? That's not hypothetical. I genuinely don't know the answer, but I am suspicious that we're about to run a massive uncontrolled experiment on an entire clinical workforce and call it progress.⏎⏎The RFI asks about regulatory and reimbursement actions HHS can take to accelerate adoption. My answer to that specific question is: don't. Or at least, stop. Before creating pathways that make it easier to bill for AI-assisted care or that create incentives for health systems to deploy these tools widely, there needs to be a much more rigorous pre-deployment evaluation requirement. Not post-market surveillance after the tool is already embedded in two hundred hospitals. Actual prospective evidence that the tool performs as claimed in the population and setting where it will actually be used, not just in the academic medical centers where it was validated.⏎⏎The bias issue deserves its own paragraph because I think people underestimate how hard it is to detect. A 2019 study published in Science found that a widely used commercial algorithm used to allocate healthcare resources systematically underestimated the needs of Black patients, not because anyone programmed it to be discriminatory, but because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for health needs, and Black patients historically received less care. That algorithm was running for years before anyone caught it. HHS knows about this study. It's been cited extensively. And yet the RFI reads as though the main challenge is just getting more data and moving faster. I'd argue the lesson is almost exactly the opposite.⏎⏎I'm also skeptical of the administrative AI argument, the idea that even if clinical AI is complicated, surely we can agree that AI handling prior authorizations or scheduling is fine. But prior authorization decisions affect whether people get care. The humans who currently do that work, whatever you think of the insurance industry, are at least reachable, trainable, and subject to state insurance commissioner oversight. An automated denial system sitting inside a black box managed by a vendor under a proprietary license is none of those things. And there are already lawsuits — UnitedHealth's nH Predict system, which used a 90% error rate (according to a Senate investigation) to deny post-acute care claims — that suggest this is not a theoretical risk.⏎⏎I don't think I'm anti-technology. I'm writing this on a computer. My mother's imaging was read partly with computer-assisted detection and I'm glad it was. But there's a difference between a tool that assists a human who retains full ownership of the decision and a system that is making recommendations that clinicians, under time pressure and cognitive load, are going to follow most of the time regardless of what the consent forms or the liability waivers say about "final clinical judgment."⏎⏎HHS should require, at minimum, that any AI tool used in clinical decision support or insurance determination be subject to independent third-party auditing for both accuracy and bias, with results publicly posted. Not vendor-conducted validation. Not post-market reporting that takes three years to surface problems. SYNTH-0088♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Nancy Clark♔Booz Allen Hamilton♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's Hospital in Kansas City in February 2023, and what kept her alive was not a pattern-matching algorithm. There is documented evidence going back to a 2019 paper in Science by Obermeyer and colleagues showing that a widely used commercial algorithm systematically underestimated illness severity in Black patients because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for health needs, and Black patients had historically received less care. That is not a fringe concern. That is a peer-reviewed finding about a tool that was already deployed at scale before anyone caught it. And that was a relatively simple algorithm. The systems being discussed now are far more complex and far harder to audit.⏎⏎What worries me about the framing of this RFI is that it treats AI adoption as inherently good and asks only about removing obstacles. But some of those "obstacles" are actually safeguards. Clinician skepticism is not a barrier to be overcome through better marketing or training programs. It might be appropriate skepticism based on direct experience with tools that don't work as advertised in real clinical conditions. The fact that a tool performs well in a controlled study or a well-resourced academic medical center does not mean it will work in a rural critical access hospital with incomplete EHR data and staff who are already stretched thin.⏎⏎There is also something that I think gets glossed over in these discussions, which is what happens to accountability when AI is in the loop. Right now, if a physician misses something, there is a chain of responsibility. It is not a perfect system, obviously, but it exists. When an AI system recommends against a treatment and a clinician defers to it and the patient is harmed, who is responsible? The clinician who deferred? The hospital that deployed the system? The vendor who trained the model? I have not seen a satisfactory answer to this from anyone promoting these tools, and the fact that HHS is considering accelerating adoption before that question is settled is genuinely alarming.⏎⏎I also want to say something about the administrative side of this because I think it gets underdiscussed. There is a lot of enthusiasm about using AI for prior authorization and claims review, which sounds like it would reduce administrative burden. But if you look at how insurers have already deployed automated decision-making in prior auth, the results are not good. ProPublica and other outlets have reported on UnitedHealth's nH Predict tool, which was used to deny post-acute care to Medicare Advantage patients based on algorithmic predictions, and there are active lawsuits about this. That is what AI-assisted administrative decision-making looks like in practice when the incentives are to deny rather than approve. Speeding up that process does not help patients.⏎⏎I am not saying technology has no place in medicine. I use a continuous glucose monitor, and it has changed my life. But there is a difference between a tool that gives a patient more information about their own body and a tool that intermediates between a patient and the clinical judgment of another human being. HHS should be asking much harder questions about where that line is before writing policy that pushes institutions toward faster adoption on the assumption that more AI is categorically better.⏎⏎The question of who benefits economically from accelerated adoption is one I wish this RFI had asked more directly.♔♔2026-02-20T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-20T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI systems in healthcare inherit and amplify racial, socioeconomic, and institutional biases from training data, and their apparent authority makes those biases harder to detect and correct than human error.♔organization♔high♔concerned♔South Carolina♔healthcare administrator♔42♔A 2024 operational review conducted across Sancian LLC's network of 14 affiliated ambulatory care facilities identified that clinicians spend an average of 3.2 hours per shift on administrative documentation tasks that AI-assisted tools have demonstrated capacity to reduce by up to 40% in peer-reviewed implementation studies. SYNTH-0089♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Brian Lopez♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in Cincinnati in March of 2023 after a sepsis diagnosis that her primary care doctor almost missed. Right now, a hospital can deploy a clinical decision support tool and if it's positioned in a certain way, it doesn't even require FDA clearance. The result is that hospitals are buying products that were validated in one population, in one health system, sometimes years ago, and using them on patients who look nothing like the training data. There was a widely reported study from 2019 looking at a commercial algorithm used by health systems across the country that systematically underestimated the care needs of Black patients because it used healthcare cost as a proxy for health need. That kind of bias doesn't announce itself. The clinician sees a score, the score seems authoritative, and the disparity gets baked in deeper than it was before.⏎⏎HHS should not be looking for ways to reduce regulatory friction for these tools. If anything, the post-market surveillance requirements are too weak and the pre-deployment validation standards are inconsistent. I understand there's pressure from the vendor community and from health systems that have already bought these products to make the regulatory environment friendlier, but that pressure shouldn't drive federal policy.⏎⏎The insurance side of this worries me even more than the clinical side, actually. AI-driven prior authorization denials are already a serious problem and the volume is only going up. When a clinician recommends a treatment and an automated system denies it with no explanation that a real person can engage with, that's not efficiency, that's a power shift. The clinician loses standing. The patient loses their advocate. And when they appeal, they're often appealing to a process that's also partly automated or staffed by people who have no authority to deviate from whatever the algorithm decided.⏎⏎I don't think HHS can or should try to ban AI entirely and I'm not sure that's a coherent position anyway.
