The assumption that AI will "augment" rather than replace workers in these roles is mostly coming from the people selling the AI. The economic incentives point clearly toward replacement wherever possible, and health systems that are already operating on thin margins will follow those incentives. Whether that produces better care for patients is a separate question that nobody seems to be seriously asking.⏎⏎Patricia still works at Mercy General as far as I know. SYNTH-0090♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Joseph Hernandez♔Johns Hopkins Malone Center for Engineering in Healthcare♔♔♔♔♔My mother was in the ICU at St. Francis Medical Center in Peoria for eleven days in February 2023. During that time, I watched nurses and doctors make decisions that no algorithm could have made, not because the data was missing but because the data wasn't the point. She couldn't describe her pain clearly. She was frightened and sometimes combative. Her history was complicated in ways that her chart didn't capture. What kept her alive and what eventually got her home wasn't pattern matching. It was a pulmonologist who sat with her for forty minutes and listened to things that weren't in any database. I keep thinking about what her care would have looked like if that doctor had been nudged, prompted, second-guessed, or time-pressured by a system telling him what the most likely diagnosis was.⏎⏎That's the thing I want HHS to sit with before it accelerates anything. The word "accelerate" in this RFI worries me as much as anything else in it. There is no clinical emergency requiring us to move faster on AI adoption. The emergency, if there is one, runs in the other direction: we are at risk of deploying systems whose failure modes we don't understand into settings where errors can kill people, and we are doing it because vendors are selling hard and hospital administrators are looking for ways to cut costs while calling it innovation.⏎⏎I want to respond specifically to the question about what barriers exist to AI adoption, because I think the framing is backwards. The question treats caution as a problem to be removed. But some of those barriers are there for good reasons. Clinicians who are skeptical of AI recommendations are not being obstructionist. They are doing their jobs. A nurse who overrides an alert because she knows her patient isn't like the population the model was trained on is exercising exactly the kind of judgment that makes care safe. If HHS's goal is to reduce that friction, it should explain very carefully why, because from where I sit, that friction is often the last line of defense.⏎⏎There's also something that doesn't get discussed enough, which is what happens when these tools are wrong in ways that look right. An AI system that confidently produces a plausible-looking but incorrect recommendation is more dangerous than one that obviously fails. And the history of clinical decision support tools, even before the current generation of machine learning, is full of alert fatigue, ignored recommendations, and systems that were validated in one context and deployed in twenty others. There's a 2019 study from Vanderbilt that found clinicians overrode drug-interaction alerts at a rate above 90 percent, not because the clinicians were reckless but because the alerts were so poorly calibrated that ignoring them had become rational behavior. I'm not sure the newer AI tools are fundamentally different in that respect.⏎⏎The employment and labor dimension also deserves a direct answer from HHS rather than being treated as a side issue. If AI tools are adopted in administrative roles, in prior authorization, in coding, in care coordination, those are jobs held by real people who have built expertise over years. The argument that AI will free up workers for higher-value tasks has been made about every automation wave in history and it has been wrong often enough that it deserves skepticism, not assumption. I haven't seen HHS ask how many healthcare workers might lose their jobs or have their roles degraded as a result of the adoption it wants to accelerate, and that omission is noticeable.⏎⏎The accountability question is the one I find hardest to let go of. When something goes wrong in medicine, there has to be someone responsible. Not a system, not a vendor, not a hospital policy that says the clinician should have caught it. A person. AI makes that harder in ways that I don't think have been solved, and I'm not sure they can be solved with disclosure requirements or audit logs. If a physician makes a decision partly because an AI told him to, and the patient is harmed, the legal and moral accountability becomes genuinely murky in ways that could take years of litigation to sort out, and the patient or their family bears the cost of that murkiness in the meantime.⏎⏎I recognize that AI tools might help in some narrow, well-defined, carefully monitored circumstances. I'm not saying there's no possible application anywhere. But the question HHS is asking is how to accelerate adoption broadly, and that's a different question, and the answer to that one is: slowly, with much more rigorous pre-deployment evaluation than currently exists, with mandatory post-market surveillance that has real teeth, and with explicit protections for clinicians who decline to follow AI recommendations when their clinical judgment says otherwise. There's currently no federal standard requiring that a clinician be able to override an AI recommendation without it being flagged as a deviation from protocol or used against them in a productivity review, and that gap alone should give HHS pause before it does anything to make deployment faster.♔♔2026-02-09T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-09T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔Once AI systems are embedded in healthcare infrastructure, they become practically irreversible and resistant to democratic oversight, creating a permanent transfer of power from clinicians, patients, and regulators to technology vendors.♔academic_organization♔high♔concerned♔Louisiana♔associate professor♔50♔A 2024 CRA survey of 412 computing research faculty across 89 member institutions found that 67% are actively engaged in AI projects with direct clinical translation potential, yet fewer than 20% reported having established reimbursement or deployment pathways for their developed systems. SYNTH-0091♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Lisa Martinez♔American Society of Anesthesiologists♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Piedmont Atlanta Hospital in February 2023 after a sepsis diagnosis that got missed for almost 36 hours. Part of what delayed the diagnosis was that the clinical decision support tool flagged her presentation as low-risk. The nurses at bedside were suspicious. They kept saying something was wrong. But the tool said otherwise and the attending on duty deferred to it. I am not saying AI killed my mother — she survived, thank God — but I am saying that what I saw in that hospital was not a system where the machine assisted human judgment. It was a system where human judgment was waiting for permission from the machine.⏎⏎That experience is why I am responding to this RFI, specifically to the questions about what regulatory steps HHS should take to accelerate adoption. My answer is: please slow down instead.⏎⏎I understand the argument for AI in healthcare. I have heard it many times. Burnout is real, physician shortages are real, diagnostic errors happen constantly and some of them could theoretically be caught by pattern recognition tools. I do not think everyone who wants to use AI in clinical settings is acting in bad faith. But I think the framing of this RFI, which is built around the word "accelerate," starts from an assumption that more AI faster is obviously the right direction, and I don't think that assumption has been earned.⏎⏎Medicine is not primarily an information processing problem. When my mother was in the ICU, the nurses who were worried about her were picking up on things that no dataset captures well — the way she was breathing, the look on her face, a gut feeling built from years of watching sick people. That is not mysticism. That is clinical expertise. And what concerns me about embedding AI deeper into clinical workflows is that over time, that kind of expertise gets devalued. Clinicians start to second-guess themselves when the tool disagrees. Hospitals start to see clinicians who trust their own judgment as liabilities rather than assets.⏎⏎There is also an accountability problem that I do not think HHS is taking seriously enough in how this RFI is framed. When the tool is wrong, who is responsible? The vendor will say the hospital misused the product. The hospital will say the clinician should have overridden it. The clinician will say they were trained to trust it. I watched something like this play out firsthand. No one was clearly responsible for the 36-hour delay. Everyone had a piece of the process, and the AI tool was sort of floating above all of it, not really accountable to anyone.⏎⏎The economic piece matters too and I don't think it gets discussed honestly. Hospitals and insurers are not going to use AI primarily to make care better. They are going to use it to cut costs. We already see this with prior authorization — automated denial systems that reject claims without meaningful clinical review. Patients with chronic conditions are spending hours on the phone fighting decisions made by algorithms. That is not hypothetical. The American Medical Association put out data in 2023 showing that 94 percent of physicians reported prior authorization delays causing care delays for patients. AI is going to make that worse, not better, because it scales the denial capacity without scaling the appeals process or the human oversight.⏎⏎I keep coming back to the question of what we are actually optimizing for. If the answer is throughput and cost per encounter, then yes, AI probably helps. But if the answer is whether patients feel that a human being is genuinely responsible for their care and will fight for them when something goes wrong, I am not sure AI helps at all. It might actively make that worse.⏎⏎The specific regulatory action I would urge HHS to take is a moratorium on the use of AI in insurance coverage decisions until there is a functioning, independent audit process with real enforcement teeth — not voluntary best practices, not self-reported vendor metrics. And for clinical settings, any deployment should require prospective validation in the specific patient population where it will actually be used, not just aggregate performance data from a training set that may not look anything like the community hospital in rural Georgia where the tool eventually gets installed.⏎⏎There is probably more to say about the liability framework and how CMS reimbursement structures create perverse incentives for hospitals to adopt tools before they're ready, but I don't have the regulatory expertise to lay that out in detail. SYNTH-0092♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Patricia Nguyen♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Regions Hospital in St. Paul in February 2023, and I sat with her through most of it. She changed a medication order after that. I don't know how to put that into a dataset.⏎⏎I understand that HHS is trying to figure out how to speed up AI adoption in clinical settings, and I want to respond specifically to the question about what barriers to adoption exist and whether those barriers should be removed. My answer is that some of those barriers exist for very good reasons, and the framing of this RFI, which treats adoption speed as a goal in itself, worries me.⏎⏎Here is what I keep coming back to: the safety record for AI diagnostic and decision-support tools is genuinely uneven, and the studies showing good performance are often done in controlled conditions that don't look much like the hospitals where most people actually receive care. A 2021 analysis published in The Lancet Digital Health looked at something like 130 published clinical AI algorithms and found that only a small fraction had been tested in real deployment conditions rather than retrospective data. That's not a minor technical footnote. That's a fundamental problem with knowing whether any of this actually works when a tired nurse at 3am in a short-staffed unit is the one supposed to be catching what the algorithm misses.⏎⏎The insurance piece is maybe even more concerning to me than the clinical piece, though they're connected. Automated prior authorization systems already deny care at rates that human reviewers wouldn't, and when those denials get appealed, they're overturned the majority of the time, which tells you something. UnitedHealth Group's use of an AI model called nH Predict to deny post-acute care claims was the subject of a federal lawsuit filed in 2023, where the allegation was that the system had a 90 percent overturn rate on appeal but continued to be used anyway. If HHS accelerates AI adoption in administrative functions without first requiring that these systems meet some minimum standard of accuracy and explainability, we are just going to see more of that, at larger scale, faster.⏎⏎I'm not someone who thinks all technology is bad. I use electronic health records every day in my work, I'm not a Luddite. But there is a difference between tools that support clinical judgment and tools that replace it or that get used to justify cost cutting dressed up as efficiency. The hospitals and insurers that are most eager to deploy AI are often not the ones most concerned about outcomes for low-income or elderly patients. They're concerned about margin.⏎⏎What I would ask HHS to consider is whether the right question right now is really how to accelerate adoption, or whether it's how to make sure that whatever gets adopted actually works, doesn't embed existing disparities into automated systems that are much harder to challenge than a human reviewer, and preserves some meaningful form of accountability when things go wrong. Right now if a physician makes a mistake there is a process, imperfect as it is, for understanding what happened and holding someone responsible. When an algorithm makes a mistake the vendor says it was used off-label, the hospital says it was following the vendor's guidance, and the clinician says they were just following the recommendation. I've talked to patient advocates who deal with exactly this situation and they describe it as hitting a wall.⏎⏎The question of who is actually accountable when an AI system contributes to a bad outcome has not been answered. I don't think adoption should be accelerated before it is.♔♔2026-01-28T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-28T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare will be used by employers and insurers as a mechanism for workforce surveillance, productivity extraction, staffing cuts, and the devaluation of clinical expertise rather than genuine care improvement.♔individual♔medium♔frustrated♔Ohio♔retired nurse♔75♔After 32 years working cardiac step-down at Riverside Methodist, I watched my hospital introduce three different electronic charting systems, and every single one of them created more paperwork for the nurses and less time at the bedside — the vendors always promised it would be the opposite. SYNTH-0093♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Carol Gonzalez♔Society for Women's Health Research♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Riverside University Health System in Moreno Valley in October 2023, and what kept her alive was not an algorithm. It was a night-shift nurse named Darlene who noticed something was off about how my mother was breathing and flagged the attending at 2 in the morning before any monitor alarmed. That kind of judgment is not a data pattern. I don't know how you would even begin to train a model on it.⏎⏎So when I read that HHS is looking for ways to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care, my first reaction is honestly fear. Not fear of technology in general, I use technology constantly, but fear of what happens when hospitals and insurers get ahold of a tool that lets them justify decisions that used to require a human being to be accountable for.⏎⏎The question I want to address is the one about what barriers exist to AI adoption and whether HHS should act to remove them. My answer is that some of those barriers are there for good reasons, and the Department should be very careful before treating every obstacle as a problem to be solved.⏎⏎Take prior authorization. Insurers are already using automated systems to deny claims at enormous scale, and there is documented evidence, including a 2023 Senate investigation into UnitedHealth Group's nH Predict tool, that these systems deny post-acute care at rates that a physician reviewer would not replicate. When that gets called an AI efficiency gain, what it actually is is a transfer of decision-making power away from clinicians and patients and toward a system that nobody can really interrogate. The clinician can appeal, technically, but the appeals process is designed to exhaust people. Making that faster and more automated does not improve care. It just makes the denial faster.⏎⏎I keep thinking about what it means for a patient to feel that a human being is responsible for their care. When things go wrong in medicine, and they do go wrong, what matters enormously is that there is a person who is answerable. A doctor who makes a mistake can be questioned, disciplined, sued, can feel guilt, can learn. What happens when the recommendation came from a vendor's model trained on data from a different hospital system in a different region, deployed under a license agreement that limits liability? The hospital says the vendor made the tool. The vendor says the clinician should have overridden it. The clinician says they were trained to defer to the system's outputs under the hospital's protocol. Nobody is responsible. That is not a hypothetical, there are already cases working through state courts with exactly that structure.⏎⏎And I do want to say something about the workforce piece because I think it gets dismissed too quickly. The argument that AI will free nurses and doctors from administrative burdens so they can focus on patients sounds good. But hospitals are businesses. If an AI system allows a hospital to process the same number of patients with fewer billing coders, fewer intake coordinators, fewer case managers, the likely outcome is not that those workers get reassigned to patient care. The likely outcome is that those positions are eliminated. The people who do those jobs are disproportionately women, disproportionately people of color, and they are often the people who have the most sustained contact with patients navigating a confusing system. Losing them is not a neutral administrative efficiency.⏎⏎HHS should at minimum require that any AI tool used in clinical or insurance decision-making be subject to ongoing post-deployment auditing with results made publicly available, not just submitted to the vendor. The FDA's current framework for software as a medical device is not adequate for this, and the gap between what gets validated in a controlled study and what gets deployed in a community hospital in a rural county is enormous. I've read some of the ONC interoperability work and I understand there are certification pathways being discussed, but certification at the point of deployment tells you very little about how a system performs six months later on a different patient population.⏎⏎There is also the question of what happens to patients who can't navigate an AI-mediated system. My mother speaks primarily Spanish and has limited health literacy. When I wasn't there, she had real difficulty communicating with the care team even with human interpreters available. An AI triage tool or a chatbot-based symptom checker is going to fail her in ways that are hard to measure and easy to ignore in aggregate accuracy statistics.⏎⏎I am not saying no AI ever in any context. I'm saying the framing of this RFI, which treats acceleration as the goal and barriers as friction to be cleared, has the logic backwards. The question should be which uses of AI genuinely improve outcomes for patients, particularly vulnerable patients, and what protections need to be in place before deployment, not after a harm has already occurred. Right now the incentive structure rewards vendors for selling and hospitals for buying, and neither of them bears the cost when the system fails on an edge case that wasn't in the training data.⏎⏎The auditing requirement I mentioned above should also apply retroactively to systems already in use. Many hospitals deployed ambient documentation tools and clinical decision support tools during and after the pandemic under emergency conditions and those tools have never been formally evaluated in the populations where they're now being used every day.♔♔2026-02-07T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-07T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔3♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare will be used by employers and insurers as a mechanism for workforce surveillance, productivity extraction, staffing cuts, and the devaluation of clinical expertise rather than genuine care improvement.♔advocacy_organization♔high♔urgent♔Indiana♔executive director♔41♔A 2024 landscape analysis conducted across GLI's network of 47 hepatology centers of excellence found that 68% of participating programs reported delays exceeding six months in implementing AI-assisted diagnostic tools, citing inconsistent reimbursement pathways and unclear FDA-HHS coordination as the primary structural barriers. SYNTH-0094♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Sandra Sanchez♔Cigna♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in 2022 after a sepsis diagnosis that was initially missed. What saved her wasn't a better algorithm. It was a night-shift nurse named Theresa who noticed something was off about how my mother was holding her arm and pushed back on the attending until he ordered a second blood culture. I keep thinking about that when I read materials like this RFI, which seems to assume that the main problem with American healthcare is that there isn't enough computational power involved.⏎⏎The question HHS is essentially asking is how to accelerate AI adoption, which already embeds an assumption I'd push back on hard. The question isn't how fast we can deploy these tools. The question is whether deploying them at scale actually improves patient outcomes, and for which patients, and under what conditions, and who is accountable when it goes wrong. Those questions are getting skipped.⏎⏎On the safety and effectiveness piece specifically: I am genuinely skeptical that the current evidence base justifies the enthusiasm here. A lot of the published validation studies for clinical AI tools are done by the same institutions or vendors that developed the tools, on patient populations that look nothing like a rural Medicaid patient in Mississippi or an elderly monolingual Spanish speaker in a busy urban ED. The FDA has cleared hundreds of AI-enabled devices over the past several years and the post-market surveillance requirements are, by most accounts I've read, pretty thin. Systems get validated in one hospital system and then sold to fifty others where the patient mix, the EHR configuration, and the workflow are all different. That's not a minor technical concern. That's how you get a tool that performs fine in the demo and then quietly produces bad recommendations in real deployment, and nobody catches it because the clinicians have been trained to trust the output.⏎⏎There's also something that bothers me about the framing of AI as a solution to clinician burnout. I've talked to enough nurses and hospitalists to know that what's burning them out is documentation burden, staffing ratios, and the feeling that they have no control over their work environment. AI tools often add to documentation burden, not reduce it, because now you have to document your interaction with the AI recommendation on top of everything else. And if a hospital uses "we have AI now" as a justification for cutting staff or reducing the number of physicians per unit, that's not burnout relief. That's a labor substitution strategy dressed up in patient-safety language.⏎⏎The reimbursement angle matters too. If CMS and commercial insurers start reimbursing AI-assisted care at higher rates, or if AI-generated prior authorization decisions become normalized, you are essentially building a financial architecture that locks in these tools before anyone has done the long-term outcome studies. That's backwards. Reimbursement should follow demonstrated clinical benefit, not precede it.⏎⏎I'm not saying there are zero legitimate uses. Radiology screening tools in settings with genuine radiologist shortages, that's a different conversation. But that's a narrow, specific case. What concerns me is the broad push to get AI into clinical decision support, into insurance determinations, into administrative functions that currently require humans who have actual accountability for their decisions. When a prior auth is denied by a human reviewer, there's a person you can appeal to, a paper trail, a licensed professional whose name is on the decision. When it's denied by an algorithm run by a vendor under contract, the diffusion of responsibility is real and the patient is the one who absorbs the cost of that confusion.⏎⏎HHS should at minimum require prospective, independent clinical validation before any AI system is used in patient-facing decisions, mandatory incident reporting when AI recommendations are overridden or contribute to adverse events, and clear rules about who bears liability. I'd also want to see explicit restrictions on using AI outputs as the sole or primary basis for insurance coverage denials, which is already happening at some large commercial insurers regardless of what the policies technically say.⏎⏎I realize I haven't addressed every section of the RFI. Honestly the document is long and a lot of the questions assume a level of enthusiasm for rapid deployment that I don't share. The question about what barriers HHS should remove to accelerate adoption assumes removal of barriers is the right goal. Some of those barriers exist because people got hurt.♔♔2026-01-18T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-18T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI-powered insurance decision-making automates prior authorization denials and coverage restrictions in ways that are opaque, unappealable, and systematically biased against vulnerable patients.♔organization♔high♔frustrated♔Michigan♔practice manager♔44♔A 2024 operational review conducted across Sancian LLC's managed practice portfolio identified that 68% of participating clinicians reported spending more than 90 minutes per patient encounter on documentation and administrative tasks directly addressable by AI-assisted tools. SYNTH-0095♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Melissa Martin♔American Association of Neurological Surgeons♔♔♔♔♔My mother was in the hospital for six weeks in 2021 with a complicated post-surgical infection, and what got her through it was not any piece of technology. I don't know how you code that into a training dataset.⏎⏎That's what I keep coming back to when I read about HHS wanting to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care. The framing of this RFI assumes that faster adoption is the goal and that the question is just how to remove obstacles. I'd like to push back on that assumption pretty directly.⏎⏎Medicine is not primarily an information retrieval problem. I understand why it can look like one from the outside, especially to people who work in technology policy or health informatics, where the problems that are visible are the ones involving data flow and interoperability and documentation burden. Those are real problems. But the solution to documentation burden is not to hand decision support over to a system trained on historical records that already contain decades of documented racial and socioeconomic bias. There's published research, including work from Ziad Obermeyer and colleagues going back to 2019, showing that a widely used commercial algorithm systematically underestimated the health needs of Black patients because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for health needs, and Black patients had historically received less care, so the algorithm essentially learned to replicate that disparity. That's not an edge case. That's what happens when you treat medicine as an optimization problem without asking what you're optimizing for or whose experience is encoded in the training data.⏎⏎I'm also worried about what AI does to accountability structures. When a clinician makes a mistake, there is a person who is answerable. Licensing boards, malpractice law, hospital credentialing, peer review, these are imperfect systems but they exist and they name a responsible human. When an AI system contributes to a bad outcome, the accountability becomes genuinely murky. The clinician says they were following the tool. The hospital says the vendor validated it. The vendor says the hospital deployed it outside the approved use case. I've watched this dynamic play out in other regulated industries and it is not hypothetical.⏎⏎The RFI asks specifically about reimbursement pathways and regulatory changes that could accelerate adoption. My honest answer is that the current pace of adoption is already faster than the evidence base supports. The FDA's regulatory framework for software as a medical device is still catching up to the products already on the market. Creating new reimbursement incentives before the oversight infrastructure is mature would be putting the cart about three miles ahead of the horse.⏎⏎There's also an employment issue that I don't think gets taken seriously enough. When hospitals talk about AI reducing clinician burnout, what they often mean in practice is that AI will allow them to justify thinner staffing ratios. The productivity gains get captured by the institution, not the worker. Radiology techs, medical coders, prior authorization staff, these are real jobs held by people with specific training and expertise, and there is a pretty direct line between AI deployment in those functions and pressure to reduce headcount. The argument that new jobs will appear to replace them is the same argument that has been made about every automation wave, and it has a mixed record at best.⏎⏎I want to be clear that I'm not saying technology has no place in healthcare. Electronic records, telemedicine access in rural areas, better imaging analysis as a second-check tool, there are specific applications where the evidence is reasonable and the oversight is workable. But that is very different from the broad acceleration agenda this RFI seems to be building toward. The question HHS should be asking is not "how do we remove barriers to adoption" but "what evidence standard should be required before a clinical AI tool can be reimbursed, and who is legally responsible when it fails."⏎⏎On the question of insurance and prior authorization specifically, I'd urge HHS to look very carefully at what has already happened. SYNTH-0096♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Mark Rodriguez♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at St. Luke's in Kansas City in February 2023, and what kept her alive wasn't an algorithm. It was a night nurse named Darnell who noticed she was breathing slightly differently at 3am and called the attending before the monitors flagged anything. I've been thinking about that a lot while reading about what HHS wants to do here.⏎⏎The question I want to address is really the framing of this whole request, which assumes that accelerating AI adoption is the goal and that the only thing left to figure out is how to remove obstacles. That's the wrong starting point. HHS should be asking whether AI belongs in clinical care at all, at least in anything like the scope being contemplated, before building regulatory infrastructure to speed it up.⏎⏎Here's what bothers me most. Medicine is not primarily an information problem. I know that sounds like a strange thing to say in 2024 but I genuinely believe it. When my mother couldn't describe her symptoms clearly because she was frightened and her english gets worse when she's scared, Darnell figured it out anyway by watching her, by knowing what she looked like the day before, by asking her son questions. No model trained on clinical notes from a hospital system in a different city, with different patient demographics and different documentation habits, was going to replicate that. And the danger isn't just that AI would have missed it. The danger is that an AI system would have produced some output, some risk score or recommendation, that looked authoritative and might have given the team a false sense that the situation was being monitored.⏎⏎There's actually published research on this. A 2019 study in Science by Obermeyer et al. found that a widely used commercial algorithm for identifying high-risk patients was systematically underestimating the severity of illness in Black patients because it used healthcare cost as a proxy for health need, and Black patients had historically been given less care so their costs were lower even when they were sicker. The algorithm looked like it was working. It wasn't. And the people using it had no particular reason to suspect anything was wrong because the outputs seemed reasonable and the vendor wasn't volunteering information about how it worked.⏎⏎That's the thing that I don't think this RFI takes seriously enough. These systems can be wrong in ways that are invisible to the people relying on them. And in healthcare, invisible wrongness gets people hurt or killed before anyone figures out the pattern. There's a reason we have clinical trials and post-market surveillance for drugs and devices. We don't say "this medication looks promising, let's accelerate adoption and figure out safety as we go." But that is essentially what accelerating AI adoption in clinical settings means.⏎⏎I'm also genuinely worried about what this does to clinicians. Not just job losses, though that's real and I don't want to brush past it. I mean what it does to the people who stay. If a doctor's job increasingly becomes reviewing AI outputs and either accepting or overriding them, there's a lot of research on automation bias suggesting they will override less and less over time even when they should. Their skills atrophy. Their confidence in their own judgment erodes. And then when the system fails in some unusual edge case, the person nominally responsible for catching it hasn't been exercising the judgment muscles needed to catch it.⏎⏎HHS should not be in the business of accelerating this. HHS should be asking what guardrails would need to exist before any clinical AI tool is deployed on real patients, and whether those guardrails are even achievable given how these systems work. At a minimum I'd want to see mandatory prospective validation studies in the specific deployment context, not just retrospective performance on historical data. I'd want to see real auditing requirements with teeth, not voluntary transparency frameworks. And I'd want to see liability rules that don't let vendors hide behind "the clinician should have caught it."⏎⏎I realize this puts me on the skeptical end of this conversation. I'm not saying there's zero application for computational tools in medicine. But the specific framing of accelerating adoption as a policy goal, the idea that the regulatory job is to get out of the way faster, worries me quite a bit. The people who will bear the cost of getting this wrong are patients who are already in vulnerable situations, and most of them are not going to be submitting comments to this docket.⏎⏎One more thing I want to flag, and I'm not sure where it fits exactly in the RFI questions: the insurance and prior authorization piece. AI is already being used by insurers to deny claims, and there are active lawsuits about this. UnitedHealth's nH Predict tool was the subject of litigation in 2023 alleging that it was systematically terminating coverage for post-acute care patients before they had recovered, with a denial rate reportedly much higher than human reviewers would have produced. That's not a hypothetical risk. That's already happening. Whatever HHS does on the clinical care side, it should not be creating an environment where that kind of deployment gets easier or more normalized.♔♔2026-02-21T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-21T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔The AI healthcare market is dominated by a small number of powerful vendors whose commercial interests in rapid deployment conflict with patient safety, and HHS's acceleration agenda risks embedding those vendors permanently into critical healthcare infrastructure.♔individual♔high♔concerned♔Indiana♔firefighter♔52♔When my crew pulled a 67-year-old man out of a house fire on Walnut Street in Terre Haute two winters back, the ER docs spent nearly forty minutes sorting through his paper medication records trying to figure out why he wasn't responding the way they expected — turns out he had an undocumented penicillin allergy that nearly compounded the injury. SYNTH-0097♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Richard Martin♔Stanford Center for Biomedical Informatics Research♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in Cincinnati in March of 2023, and what got her through it was not any algorithm. The word "accelerate" is doing a lot of work in this RFI and I don't think HHS has really grappled with what it means to speed up the deployment of systems that, by the agency's own framing, are still being figured out. You don't accelerate into uncertainty in medicine. You're supposed to be cautious.⏎⏎On the specific question of what regulatory actions HHS can take: my honest answer is that the most responsible thing the department can do right now is slow down, not speed up. I understand that's not the answer the RFI is looking for, but the framing of the question itself troubles me. The RFI asks how to "accelerate adoption" as though adoption is the goal rather than patient outcomes. These are not the same thing. A hospital can adopt an AI-powered prior authorization tool tomorrow and adoption goes up while actual care gets worse, because now a nurse has to argue with a machine to get a patient's medication approved instead of making a phone call to someone with discretion.⏎⏎That prior authorization example isn't hypothetical. UnitedHealth's use of the nH Predict algorithm to deny post-acute care claims was the subject of a federal lawsuit filed in 2023. The complaint alleged the system had a 90% overturn rate on appeal, meaning the AI was wrong nine times out of ten when humans actually reviewed the decisions. And yet the system kept running because it was cheaper to let it run and let some percentage of patients not bother to appeal. That is what AI adoption in healthcare actually looks like in practice, not the demos, not the whitepapers.⏎⏎I'm also genuinely worried about what happens to the people doing this work. Radiology techs, medical coders, prior auth nurses, even some diagnostic roles -- these are real jobs held by people with real training, and the case for AI in those spaces is often just a case for doing the same work with fewer people. I don't think HHS should be in the business of subsidizing that through reimbursement incentives or certification pathways that make it easier for health systems to replace workers with tools that haven't been validated in their specific patient populations.⏎⏎The validation point matters a lot and I don't think it gets enough attention. A model trained on data from a large academic medical center in Boston is not the same as a model deployed in a rural critical access hospital in eastern Kentucky. The patient populations are different, the documentation practices are different, the staffing ratios are different. When the model fails, and some will fail, the question of who is responsible becomes genuinely unanswerable. The clinician says they were following the tool's recommendation. The hospital says they implemented it according to vendor specs. The vendor says the hospital used it outside the indicated population. This is not a hypothetical liability puzzle, it's already happening and there isn't a clear regulatory framework for it.⏎⏎I think HHS should require, at minimum, prospective outcome studies before any AI clinical decision support tool can be eligible for reimbursement incentives. Not retrospective validation on historical data, actual prospective studies in the deployment setting. That would slow things down considerably and that is the point.⏎⏎One more thing and I realize this is somewhat separate from the regulatory question but it keeps coming back to me: there is something lost when patients know or suspect that their care is being shaped by a system rather than a person. I've talked to older patients, my mother included, who feel a kind of abandonment when they sense that the human in the room is really just confirming what a screen already decided. That feeling isn't irrational and it isn't just a communication problem that better UI design can fix. Trust in medicine is built on the belief that someone is actually thinking about you specifically, and that belief is harder to sustain the more the process looks like pattern matching.⏎⏎I don't know what the right threshold is for when AI assistance becomes AI replacement, and I'm not sure anyone does yet, which is itself a reason for caution rather than acceleration. The RFI asks about removing barriers to adoption and I'd ask HHS to consider whether some of those barriers exist for good reasons that just aren't visible yet because we haven't had enough time with these systems in real clinical environments to see what goes wrong at scale.♔♔2026-02-05T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-05T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔The AI healthcare market is dominated by a small number of powerful vendors whose commercial interests in rapid deployment conflict with patient safety, and HHS's acceleration agenda risks embedding those vendors permanently into critical healthcare infrastructure.♔academic_organization♔high♔frustrated♔New Mexico♔assistant professor♔39♔A 2024 CAP survey of 1,847 practicing pathologists found that 68% had encountered at least one AI-assisted diagnostic tool in their clinical workflow within the preceding 12 months, yet only 23% reported that their institution had a formal governance framework for validating or monitoring such tools post-deployment. SYNTH-0098♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Charles Martinez♔♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in Cincinnati in February 2023, and during that time I watched three different physicians come to her bedside, look at her, talk to her, ask her questions that weren't on any checklist, and change their clinical approach based on things that I genuinely don't think would show up in any dataset. The assumption seems to be that adoption is the goal and the question is just how fast and how smoothly. But nobody seems to be asking whether the underlying technology is actually ready for the consequences of being wrong in a hospital setting, as opposed to being wrong in, say, a streaming recommendation algorithm where the stakes are whether someone watches a bad movie.⏎⏎There's published research showing that several widely-used clinical AI tools perform significantly worse on Black patients than on white patients, not marginally worse, significantly, because the training data reflects decades of undertreatment and different care patterns that the models just absorb and replicate. The pulse oximetry problem is maybe the most well-documented version of this, where devices were systematically less accurate on darker skin and that error propagated into clinical records for years before people started taking it seriously. If AI systems get trained on those records, they inherit those errors and then present them with a confidence score. That's not progress.⏎⏎What bothers me most though is the accountability question, and I keep coming back to this even when I try to think about it charitably. Right now if a physician makes a wrong call, there are malpractice standards, there's a licensing board, there's some mechanism for consequence. If an AI system contributes to a wrong call, who exactly is responsible? The vendor will say the hospital didn't implement it correctly. The hospital will say the clinician had the final call. The clinician will say the AI said it was fine. I've seen this dynamic play out in other regulated industries and it doesn't resolve cleanly, it just means patients or their families end up with nobody to hold accountable and a very expensive legal process that goes nowhere.⏎⏎I'm also genuinely skeptical of the argument that AI will reduce clinician burnout. Every technology introduced into healthcare in the last twenty years has been sold partly on that promise and most of it has made documentation worse, not better. The EHR transition was supposed to free up clinician time. I think most physicians would laugh at that claim now. I'm not saying AI is the same, but I am saying the burden of proof should be on the people making the promise, not on the people asking for evidence.⏎⏎If HHS wants to do something useful, it could start by requiring that any AI tool used in direct clinical decision support be validated prospectively on the actual patient population at the institution deploying it, not just on whatever dataset the vendor used for the original study. Retrospective validation on a homogeneous academic medical center dataset and then deployment at a safety-net hospital serving a very different population is not acceptable. And the validation data should be public, or at least available to independent reviewers, not just sitting in a vendor's internal files.⏎⏎I also think there needs to be a much more honest conversation about what AI in insurance prior authorization actually means. It means automated denials, faster and at higher volume. The companies using these tools have a financial incentive to deny and a tool that lets them do it at scale with a veneer of clinical legitimacy. UnitedHealth's use of an algorithm called nH Predict to make post-acute care coverage decisions resulted in a class action lawsuit in 2023 precisely because the denial rate was so high and so disconnected from individual patient circumstances. That's already happening. SYNTH-0099♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Steven Walker♔American Association of Physician Assistants♔♔♔♔♔My mother was in the hospital for six weeks in 2019 after a series of strokes, and what I remember most is how much it mattered that her neurologist actually knew her. The correct response to that is hiring more staff, not deploying software that monitors and nudges and second-guesses the remaining staff. There's a study from 2023 by researchers at UCSF looking at an AI sepsis alert tool that was supposed to catch early sepsis cases - and when they actually evaluated it in practice, nurses were ignoring the alerts at high rates because there were so many false positives. The hospital says the vendor is responsible, the vendor says the clinician should have overridden the recommendation, the clinician says they were following protocol. That's not AI assisting human judgment. I'm not saying there's no possible application of machine learning that could ever help anyone in a clinical context. Those aren't the same thing and the difference matters a lot.⏎⏎What I'd want to see instead is a moratorium on AI-driven insurance coverage decisions until there are actual audit mechanisms with teeth, not voluntary transparency commitments from vendors who have every incentive to obscure how their systems work. SYNTH-0100♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Daniel Jackson♔Suki AI♔♔♔♔♔My mother spent eleven days in the ICU at Mercy General in Bakersfield in February 2023, and what got her through it was not an algorithm. It was a night nurse named Patricia who noticed something was off with her breathing at 2am and called the attending even though the monitors were technically fine. I don't know how to put that in a dataset.⏎⏎That's the thing I keep coming back to when I read about HHS wanting to accelerate AI adoption in clinical settings. The word "accelerate" is doing a lot of work there. Accelerate toward what, exactly? The RFI asks about barriers to adoption and how to remove them, but I think the more honest question is whether some of those barriers exist for good reasons and whether we are making a serious mistake by treating them as problems to be solved rather than safeguards to be respected.⏎⏎Medicine is not primarily an information-processing problem. I know that's how it can look from the outside, especially to people who come to healthcare from tech or from insurance, where efficiency metrics make sense. But a lot of what a good clinician does is interpret things that are ambiguous, incomplete, or contradictory. Patients don't always tell the truth. They leave things out because they're embarrassed or scared or because they don't know what's relevant. They describe symptoms in ways that don't match textbook presentations. Their records are incomplete because they moved, or because a previous provider used a different system, or because they just never got diagnosed for something they've been quietly managing on their own for twenty years. An AI trained on historical data is going to perform well on patients who look like the patients in the training set. That's basically what these systems do. And the patients who don't fit that profile, the ones with unusual presentations, the ones from communities that were historically underserved and therefore underrepresented in the data, those are exactly the patients who are most likely to be harmed when the system gets it wrong.⏎⏎There's a study from 2019 by Obermeyer and colleagues in Science magazine that found a widely used commercial algorithm in hospital systems was significantly underestimating the health needs of Black patients compared to white patients with the same actual health status, because it used healthcare costs as a proxy for health needs, and Black patients had historically had less access to care and therefore lower costs. The bias wasn't obvious. The system looked fine from the outside. That's the part that worries me most. These systems can be wrong in ways that are hard to see and hard to audit, especially once they're embedded in clinical workflows and everyone just assumes the output is reasonable.⏎⏎I want to be fair here. I don't think everyone pushing for AI in healthcare is acting in bad faith. Some of this technology probably does help in specific, narrow applications. Radiology image review, maybe. Certain kinds of pattern detection in labs. I'm not saying none of it is ever useful. But the RFI is asking HHS to think about how to speed up adoption broadly, across clinical care generally, and I think that framing gets things backward.⏎⏎What concerns me about the reimbursement angle specifically is that once Medicare and Medicaid start paying for AI-assisted care, and once hospitals realize they can justify thinner staffing because they have "AI support," the economic pressure becomes very hard to reverse. A hospital administrator in 2027 who is trying to cut costs is going to look at that reimbursement structure and see an opportunity, and the clinical staff who think the AI is wrong about a particular patient are going to be in a very difficult position arguing against a tool that the institution has financially committed to. That's not a hypothetical. That's just how institutional incentives work.⏎⏎Patricia, the night nurse, is probably already dealing with documentation software that takes time away from patient contact. Adding AI recommendations on top of that workflow doesn't automatically make her job easier or her patients safer. It adds another layer of things to second-guess or defer to, and the research on automation bias in clinical settings suggests that humans working alongside automated systems tend over time to over-rely on the system's outputs, especially under stress and time pressure. Which is most of nursing, most of the time.⏎⏎I don't think HHS should be in the business of accelerating this. I think HHS should be asking much harder questions about what safeguards need to exist before any of this scales, who is accountable when an AI-influenced decision causes harm, and whether the patients most likely to be affected by errors in these systems have any meaningful way to know that AI was involved in their care or to opt out of it.⏎⏎On that last point I haven't seen anything in this RFI that takes patient consent seriously as a real operational question rather than a checkbox.♔♔2026-02-05T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-05T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-31T00:00:00Z♔2026-03-01T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered policy posture that imposes strict limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance decisions, and healthcare administration — requiring demonstrated safety, equity, and accountability before any deployment, and preserving meaningful human responsibility at every point of care.♔AI in healthcare diffuses responsibility across clinicians, hospitals, and vendors in ways that leave patients without a clear accountable party when errors cause harm.♔organization♔high♔frustrated♔Michigan♔hospital CFO♔36♔Interstella's 2024 Financial Sustainability Survey, conducted across 214 member health systems representing more than 890 acute-care hospitals, found that 68% of CFOs identified AI-driven clinical decision support as a top-three strategic investment priority for the next 36 months, yet 74% of those same respondents cited unclear reimbursement pathways as the primary barrier to deployment at scale. Aggregate capital planning data from our member institutions indicates that delayed or uncertain CMS coverage determinations are causing health systems to defer an estimated $2.3 billion in committed AI infrastructure investment across the cohort. A supplemental analysis of our Q4 2024 operating margin benchmarks further revealed that facilities with active AI-assisted clinical workflows reported a 4.2 percentage-point improvement in care-team efficiency scores compared to non-adopting peers — a performance gap that underscores the tangible cost of regulatory inaction.♔AI-assisted clinical decision support fractures the chain of accountability that medical malpractice doctrine and patient rights depend upon — when a clinician, a hospital, and a vendor can each deflect responsibility for an adverse outcome, the patient is left without a legally or morally identifiable answerable party, and HHS's RFI proposes to accelerate this structural defect at scale before any remediation framework exists. | HHS is proposing to embed AI systems into clinical workflows before the accountability infrastructure — legal, regulatory, and institutional — is capable of sustaining safe deployment; once these systems achieve deep workflow integration, institutional dependency will make them practically immune to audit, challenge, or removal, further insulating vendors from the consequences of harm they help cause.♔As a hospital CFO who has reviewed aggregate capital planning data across hundreds of health systems and who understands both the financial pressure to adopt AI and the institutional liability exposure that adoption creates, Daniel Jackson frames HHS's accelerationist posture as a structural design failure that will leave hospitals holding legal and moral risk that the current accountability architecture cannot assign, adjudicate, or remedy.♔Mello MM, Frakes MD, Blumenkranz E, Studdert DM.
